Jef’s Handbooks · Handbook 2

Identity and Access in the Cloud

From passwords to permission guardrails

Part I · How access works

1

Who are you, and what may you do?

Every login screen, API key, IAM policy and “access denied” in this book is an answer to two questions: who is asking, and are they allowed to do this? This chapter separates those questions, names the parts of every request, and follows one request from sign-in to the audit log. It's the mental model the rest of the book hangs on.

By the end you can

  • Tell identification, authentication, authorisation and accountability apart, and say which one failed.
  • Name the principal, action, resource and context of any request.
  • Trace a request through sign-in, token, policy check and audit log.
  • Explain why a shared account breaks all four questions at once.

Before you start

  • Nothing from this book. You've signed in to websites and used a cloud console or CLI.
  • HTTP requests and TLS help (Handbook 1, Chapter 6), but aren't required.

This chapter

Reading
about 35 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, Python 3, no cloud account
Main provider
provider-neutral, with AWS, Azure and Google Cloud in 1.8

At Larder

On Monday 16 November 2026, a database migration from Ben's orders team went wrong and quietly damaged three days of substitution records. The plan was simple: restore the orders database to Friday night and replay from there. It failed. The database kept automated backups for one day, not the fourteen everyone assumed.

The audit log showed the change: on Thursday 12 November at 22:14, someone set backup retention to one day, probably to save money. The log named who: ops. That's the IAM user engineers share for production fixes. Six people know its password, including Jasper, who left the company fifteen months ago. Nobody remembers doing it. Rebuilding the records from the order events took Ben's team two days.

Eli, the platform tech lead, is asked to find out who did it. Section 1.7 explains why that's the wrong question.

1.1Four questions

“Access control” sounds like one thing. It's really four questions, asked in order, and most confusion about identity comes from mixing them up:

  1. Identification: who do you say you are? A name: tomas@larder.example, an access key ID, a service account. Claiming a name proves nothing.
  2. Authentication: prove it. Something only the real owner of that name can show: a password, a passkey, a signed token, a private key. Authentication turns a claim into an identity you can rely on.
  3. Authorisation: may you do this? Given who you are, is this action on this resource allowed, right now? This is where policies live.
  4. Accountability: who did what, and when? A record, written as the request happens, that ties the action to the identity. Without it, you can't investigate, prove compliance or learn from mistakes.

When something goes wrong, the first job is to work out which question failed. “I can't log in” is authentication. “I logged in but the button is greyed out” is authorisation. “We don't know who changed it” is accountability. They have different causes and different owners.

401 vs 403HTTP mixes the words up. Status 401 Unauthorized actually means unauthenticated: you didn't prove who you are. 403 Forbidden means you did, and you're not allowed.

You'll often see the first three shortened to AuthN (authentication) and AuthZ (authorisation), and the whole field called IAM, identity and access management. AWS also uses IAM as the name of one specific service. This book says “AWS IAM” when it means the service.

1.2Principals: who can ask

A principal is anything that can make a request and be granted access: a person, or a piece of software acting on its own behalf. A principal has an identity (who it is), one or more credentials (how it proves it), and permissions (what it may do). These are three different things, and good designs keep them separate: you can rotate a credential without changing the identity, or move a person to a new team without issuing new credentials.

Table 1.1 · Kinds of principal, at Larder
KindExampleTypical credentialBook
EmployeeTomás, store manager at Noordpassword + second factor, through single sign-onChapters 2, 12
Customersomeone ordering groceries in the apppassword or passkey; then a session tokenChapters 2, 3
Partner's useran office manager at Korenwolftheir own company's sign-in, trusted by LarderChapter 5
WorkloadDispatch, running in containersshort-lived credentials from the platformChapter 15
Pipelinethe deploy job in CIa token from the CI system, exchanged for cloud credentialsChapter 17
Partner systemthe payment provider calling Larder's webhooka signature or API keyChapter 19

Software principals, often called non-human or machine identities, usually outnumber people many times over, and they're where most leaked credentials come from. Half of this book is about them.

1.3Credentials: how a principal proves it

A credential is whatever a principal shows to authenticate. For people, the classic categories are called factors:

  • Something you know: a password or PIN.
  • Something you have: a phone with an authenticator app, a security key, a passkey stored on a device.
  • Something you are: a fingerprint or face, usually unlocking something you have, rather than sent anywhere.

Combining two different kinds is multi-factor authentication (MFA). Chapter 2 takes these apart, including which second factors actually stop phishing.

For software, credentials are keys and tokens: an API key, an access key pair, a signed token, a client certificate. The property that matters most is how long it lives:

  • A long-lived credential (an access key, an API key, a password) works until someone remembers to revoke it. If it leaks into a log, a laptop backup or a Git history, it keeps working for whoever finds it.
  • A short-lived credential (a session token valid for an hour) is issued after authenticating with something stronger, and expires by itself. A leak is a problem for minutes, not years.

Key idea

Prefer credentials that expire by themselves. Most of the work in this book is replacing long-lived secrets with short-lived ones.

1.4Resources, actions and context

Authorisation needs more than “who”. Every request carries four things, and every access rule you'll ever write is about some combination of them:

  • Principal: who is asking (after authentication).
  • Action: what they want to do: read, update stock, delete, change backup retention.
  • Resource: what they want to do it to: the Noord store's stock, the orders database, one S3 bucket.
  • Context: everything else known about the request: the time, the network it came from, whether MFA was used, the device, the tags on the principal and the resource.

Write a request as one sentence and the four parts fall out: “Tomás wants to set stock on Noord's bananas, at 06:12, from the store's tablet, signed in with a passkey.” A rule is a sentence of the same shape with blanks: “Store managers may set stock on their own store's items.” Chapter 9 writes these as AWS policies, and Chapter 6 compares the ways of filling the blanks (roles, attributes, relationships).

One more rule underpins all of them. Unless a rule allows a request, it's denied. This is default deny, and it means that a forgotten rule fails safe: something someone needs doesn't work, and they ask. The opposite, default allow, fails silently: something nobody intended works, and nobody notices.

1.5The life of a request

Put the pieces together. At 06:12, before the morning rush, Tomás corrects the banana count in Larder's stock tool. Here is everything that happens:

Three roles in that picture have names you'll meet everywhere:

  • The identity provider (IdP) authenticates people and issues tokens that describe them. Larder's staff will sign in through one (Chapter 12).
  • The policy enforcement point (PEP) is the code that stops a request until it has an answer. Here it's the stock tool; on AWS, it's every service's API endpoint.
  • The policy decision point (PDP) holds the rules and answers allow or deny. In a small app it's a function; on AWS, it's the IAM policy engine, which evaluates every API call.

Notice what the token in step ② does. Tomás authenticates once; for the next 30 minutes, the stock tool trusts the token instead of asking for his password again. That's convenient, and it's also why a stolen token is as good as a stolen password while it lasts. Chapter 3 is about tokens.

1.6Trust: whose word are you taking?

In step ③, the stock tool doesn't check Tomás's passkey. It checks the token, and believes what the token says because it was signed by an identity provider it's configured to trust. Every access decision rests on a chain like this: the database trusts the stock tool, the stock tool trusts the identity provider, the identity provider trusts Tomás's passkey.

Trust isn't a feeling; it's configuration you can read. Somewhere there's a setting that says “accept tokens signed by this key, issued by this provider, meant for this app”. When that setting is too broad (any issuer, any audience), anyone who can get a token from somewhere gets in. Two places you'll meet this soon:

  • Federation: Larder trusts Korenwolf's identity provider to vouch for Korenwolf's office managers, so they don't need Larder passwords (Chapter 5).
  • Role trust policies on AWS, which say who may assume a role (Chapter 8). A trust policy that's too broad is one of the most serious IAM mistakes there is.

Key idea

For every access decision, you should be able to say whose word it takes, and where that trust is written down.

1.7Why shared identities break everything

A shared account, such as Larder's ops user, one login for a whole team, or one API key used by three services, looks like a convenience. Run it past the four questions and every one of them fails:

Table 1.2 · The ops user against the four questions
QuestionWith opsWith one identity per person
IdentificationThe name says ops, which is six people.The name is one person.
AuthenticationA shared password can't have a personal second factor; it's in a chat history and a former employee's memory.Each person has their own factors; a leaver's are switched off.
AuthorisationEveryone gets the union of what anyone needed: admin.Each person gets their own job's access.
AccountabilityThe log says ops. Investigation stops there.The log names the person, so you can ask them what they were trying to do.

And removing access, for one leaver, means changing the password for everyone, so it rarely happens. That's how a co-founder who left in 2025 still knew the production password in 2026.

At Larder: what Eli does instead

Eli can't find out who changed the backups, and he stops trying: there's no answer in the data, and asking six people to accuse each other helps nobody. The useful question is how to make the next log line name a person, and make the change impossible without deliberate effort. His plan, which the rest of this book carries out: an identity for every engineer through single sign-on (Chapter 12); admin access only when needed, with approval (Chapter 14); and a guardrail that denies shortening backup retention in production, whoever asks (Chapter 10 and Chapter 20). The ops user's password is changed that afternoon, and the user is deleted in Chapter 12.

Watch out: shared machine credentials

The same applies to software. One API key used by three services means you can't tell which one made a call, can't give each one only what it needs, and can't revoke one without breaking the others. Give each workload its own identity (Chapter 15).

1.8Across providers

Every cloud splits identity into the same two halves: a directory of who exists and how they authenticate, and a permission system that decides what they may do with the cloud's resources. The providers draw the line in different places:

Table 1.3 · Where identity lives at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Directory of peopleIAM Identity Center (its own, or connected to your IdP); IAM users are legacyMicrosoft Entra IDCloud Identity or Google Workspace, or federated from your IdP
Software identitiesIAM rolesmanaged identities and service principals in Entra IDservice accounts
Permission systemIAM policiesAzure RBAC role assignmentsIAM allow and deny policies
Boundarythe accountthe tenant (directory), with subscriptions inside itthe organisation, with projects inside it
Audit logCloudTrailEntra sign-in and audit logs, Azure activity logCloud Audit Logs

On Azure

Entra ID (formerly Azure Active Directory) is the centre of everything: people, groups, apps and managed identities all live in one tenant, and the same directory signs people in to Microsoft 365. Azure RBAC then grants those identities roles on subscriptions and resources. Many companies already have Entra ID from Microsoft 365 before they use Azure at all.

On Google Cloud

People are Google accounts managed by Cloud Identity or Workspace, usually synced or federated from the company's IdP; software runs as service accounts, which are both identities and resources (you can grant someone permission to act as one). IAM policies attach to the organisation, folders, projects and individual resources.

1.9Lab: the four questions in forty lines

Lab 1 · Authenticate, authorise, record

You'll build a tiny access system with a shared identity, see what its log can and can't tell you, then give everyone their own identity and remove a leaver. You need Python 3, nothing else. Cost: nothing.

access.pyPython 3.8+

# access.py · authenticate → authorise → record, in one small file
import hashlib, hmac

def h(secret):
    return hashlib.sha256(secret.encode()).hexdigest()

# Who exists, the hash of the key each one proves itself with, and their groups
PRINCIPALS = {
    "ops": {"key_hash": h("ops-key-known-by-six-people"), "groups": ["admins"]},
}

# What each group may do: (action, resource prefix); "*" matches anything
POLICIES = {
    "admins":     [("*", "*")],
    "developers": [("db:Describe", "db/"), ("logs:Read", "logs/")],
}

AUDIT = []

def authenticate(name, key):
    p = PRINCIPALS.get(name)
    return p is not None and hmac.compare_digest(p["key_hash"], h(key))

def authorise(name, action, resource):
    for group in PRINCIPALS[name]["groups"]:
        for act, prefix in POLICIES[group]:
            if act in ("*", action) and (prefix == "*" or resource.startswith(prefix)):
                return True
    return False                      # default deny

def request(name, key, action, resource):
    if not authenticate(name, key):
        decision = "unauthenticated"
    elif not authorise(name, action, resource):
        decision = "denied"
    else:
        decision = "allowed"
    AUDIT.append(f"{name:<6} {action:<15} {resource:<14} {decision}")
    return decision

if __name__ == "__main__":
    shared = "ops-key-known-by-six-people"
    request("ops", shared, "db:Describe", "db/orders")              # Ben looks
    request("ops", shared, "db:SetBackups", "db/orders")            # someone changes backups
    request("ops", "a-guess", "db:Delete", "db/orders")             # a stranger tries
    print("\n".join(AUDIT))
  1. Run it with python3 access.py. You should see: 
    ops    db:Describe     db/orders      allowed
ops    db:SetBackups   db/orders      allowed
ops    db:Delete       db/orders      unauthenticated
    Find each of the four questions in the code: the name is identification; authenticate compares the key's hash (so the system never stores the key itself); authorise checks rules and falls through to default deny; AUDIT is the record. Now try to answer the Larder question from the log: who changed the backups?
  2. Give everyone their own identity. Save this next to access.py as access_personal.py and run it: 
    # access_personal.py · the same system, one identity per person
from access import PRINCIPALS, AUDIT, h, request

PRINCIPALS.clear()
PRINCIPALS.update({
    "ben":    {"key_hash": h("ben-key"),    "groups": ["developers"]},
    "eli":    {"key_hash": h("eli-key"),    "groups": ["admins"]},
    "jasper": {"key_hash": h("jasper-key"), "groups": ["admins"]},
})

request("ben", "ben-key", "db:Describe", "db/orders")
request("ben", "ben-key", "db:SetBackups", "db/orders")
request("eli", "eli-key", "db:SetBackups", "db/orders")
del PRINCIPALS["jasper"]                     # a leaver: remove one entry, nobody else changes
request("jasper", "jasper-key", "logs:Read", "logs/dispatch")
print("\n".join(AUDIT))
    Output: 
    ben    db:Describe     db/orders      allowed
ben    db:SetBackups   db/orders      denied
eli    db:SetBackups   db/orders      allowed
jasper logs:Read       logs/dispatch  unauthenticated
    Every line names a person; Ben, as a developer, can look but not change backups; and removing a leaver touched nobody else's key.
  3. Break it on purpose. In POLICIES, add ("*", "*") to developers, “just for this week”. Run access_personal.py again: Ben can now change backups. Then delete the line return False at the end of authorise. The function now returns None when no rule matches, which happens to be falsy, so nothing changes; replace it with return True and every authenticated request succeeds, whatever it asks for. That's default allow.

Stretch goals. (1) Add a context argument to request with an mfa flag, and a rule that db:SetBackups needs mfa=True. (2) Make the audit line include a timestamp and the request's source address. Which of the four questions does each new field help with?

Why SHA-256 is fine hereThe lab hashes keys with plain SHA-256 because they're long and random, so guessing is hopeless. Human passwords are short and guessable, and need a slow hash instead (Chapter 2).

1.10Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Shared accounts and shared keysNo accountability, no personal MFA, leavers keep access.One identity per person and per workload.
Treating “logged in” as “allowed”Any authenticated user can reach admin pages or other people's data.Check authorisation on every request, for every resource.
Default allowA missing rule silently grants access.Default deny; add rules for what's needed.
Long-lived credentials everywhereA leak works until someone notices, often years.Short-lived credentials issued after strong authentication.
Logging only successesAttacks and broken access go unseen.Record every decision, allowed or not.
Trusting any token that looks validTokens from the wrong issuer or for another app get accepted.Configure exactly which issuer, key and audience you trust (Chapters 3–5).

✓Chapter summary

  • Access control is four questions: identify, authenticate, authorise, record. When something fails, find which one.
  • A principal (a person or a workload) has an identity, credentials and permissions; keep the three separate.
  • Every request has a principal, an action, a resource and context. Rules are sentences about those four.
  • Default deny: nothing is allowed unless a rule allows it.
  • Authentication happens once, at an identity provider; tokens carry the result; authorisation happens on every request.
  • Every decision rests on trust that's written down somewhere. Know where.
  • Shared identities fail all four questions at once. Prefer short-lived credentials to long-lived ones.

Key terms

Identification
Claiming an identity, such as a user name.
Authentication
Proving a claimed identity with a credential.
Authorisation
Deciding whether an authenticated principal may perform an action on a resource.
Accountability
Recording who did what, when, so actions can be traced to identities.
Principal
A person or workload that can make requests and be granted access.
Credential
What a principal shows to authenticate: a password, key, token or certificate.
Default deny
The rule that anything not explicitly allowed is denied.
Identity provider (IdP)
A system that authenticates principals and issues tokens describing them.
PEP / PDP
Policy enforcement point (blocks the request) and policy decision point (decides).

Check your understanding

  1. A user enters the right password but gets “You don't have permission to view this page”. Which of the four questions failed? recall

    Show answer

    Authorisation. Authentication succeeded (the password was right); the system then decided this identity may not do this action on this resource.

  2. Split this request into principal, action, resource and context: “At 23:40, from a hotel Wi-Fi, Eli signed in with his security key and tried to delete the staging database.” apply

    Show answer

    Principal: Eli. Action: delete. Resource: the staging database. Context: 23:40, the hotel network's address, MFA with a security key.

  3. Why is a short-lived token safer than a long-lived API key, even if both are stored equally carefully? explain

    Show answer

    Storage fails eventually: logs, backups, screenshots, laptops. When a short-lived token leaks, it stops working by itself within minutes or hours. A long-lived key keeps working until someone notices and revokes it.

  4. Your team shares one admin login for a SaaS tool, and someone leaves. List what you have to do, and what you can't do. apply

    Show answer

    You must change the password and tell everyone who still needs it, and re-enrol any MFA tied to it. You can't tell what the leaver did with it, or prove they no longer have access through a saved session or copied password. With personal accounts you'd just disable theirs.

  5. Design the access for Larder's stock tool: who are the principals, and what context would you check before allowing a stock change? design

    Show answer

    Principals: store managers, pickers, operations staff, and the sync service from the picker app. Context worth checking: that the user's store matches the item's store, that they signed in with MFA, and (for large changes) the device. Every decision is logged with the person's identity.

  6. Compare where people's identities live on AWS and on Azure. compare

    Show answer

    On AWS, people should come from IAM Identity Center, connected to an external IdP or its own directory; IAM users exist but are legacy. On Azure, people live in Entra ID, the same directory used for Microsoft 365, and Azure RBAC grants them roles.

Going further

  • NIST SP 800-63, Digital Identity Guidelines: the standard vocabulary for identity and authentication.
  • AWS: How IAM works.
  • Azure: What is Microsoft Entra ID?
  • Google Cloud: IAM overview.

Part I · How access works

2

Passwords, MFA and passkeys

Authentication is where most breaches start: a reused password, a code typed into the wrong site, a help desk talked into a reset. This chapter explains why passwords fail and how to store them so a leak doesn't hurt, then compares second factors by the one property that matters most: whether they stop phishing. It ends with passkeys, which do.

By the end you can

  • Name the four ways passwords get broken, and the defence for each.
  • Store passwords with a salted, slow hash, and choose its settings.
  • Explain how a one-time code is calculated, and why it can still be phished.
  • Explain how passkeys stop phishing, and where they still need care.
  • Choose authentication for customers, staff and administrators.

Before you start

  • The four questions, principals and credentials (Chapter 1).
  • What a hash function is helps; 2.2 recaps it.

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
25 minutes, Python 3, no cloud account
Main provider
provider-neutral, with AWS, Azure and Google Cloud in 2.8

At Larder

On the night of Saturday 21 November 2026, Larder's login endpoint received about 40,000 sign-in attempts in four hours, from about 2,000 different addresses. Each tried one email address and one password, and moved on. 310 of them worked. By the time the pattern was spotted on Sunday morning, the attackers had used saved payment methods to send 41 orders to addresses that weren't the customers'.

Nobody had stolen anything from Larder. Its password database was intact, and properly hashed since Jasper first built it. The attackers had a list of email addresses and passwords leaked from other sites, and 310 Larder customers had used the same password here. Section 2.1 names the attack; section 2.9 shows what Larder changed.

2.1How passwords fail

A password is a secret that the user and the system both know. That design has four weak points, and it helps to keep them apart because each has a different defence:

Table 2.1 · Four ways passwords get broken
AttackHow it worksMain defence
GuessingTry common passwords against many accounts (“password spraying”), or many passwords against one.Rate limits and lockouts; reject common passwords (2.3)
Credential stuffingReplay email–password pairs leaked from other sites, betting on reuse.Check against breached passwords; MFA; passkeys
PhishingA fake site, email or call gets the user to type the password, and often the second factor, for the attacker.Phishing-resistant factors (2.4, 2.6)
Stolen databaseThe attacker copies your password store and cracks it offline, at their own speed.Salted, slow hashing (2.2)

Larder's Saturday night was credential stuffing. It's cheap for attackers, because billions of leaked pairs circulate, and it works because people reuse passwords. Strong hashing doesn't help against it: the attacker isn't attacking your database, just logging in. Only the defences in the last column do.

2.2Storing passwords: salted, slow hashes

You must be able to check a password without being able to read it. The tool is a hash function: a one-way calculation that turns any input into a fixed-size fingerprint. You store the fingerprint; at login you hash what was typed and compare. Never store passwords as they are, and never encrypt them: encryption can be reversed by anyone who gets the key, and in a breach they often do.

A plain hash such as SHA-256 isn't enough, for two reasons:

  • It's fast. Fast is good for checking files and bad for passwords: an attacker with your database can try billions of guesses a second on graphics cards. In the lab, one laptop core manages millions.
  • It's the same for everyone. Two customers with the password winter2026 get the same hash, so cracking one cracks both, and precomputed tables of common passwords work instantly.

The fix has two parts. A salt, a random value generated for each password and stored next to it, makes every hash unique even for identical passwords. A password hashing function is deliberately slow and, in modern designs, needs lots of memory, so that each guess costs real time and real hardware.

Table 2.2 · Password hashing functions, in order of preference
FunctionStarting settings (OWASP, at the time of writing)Notes
Argon2id19 MiB memory, 2 iterations, 1 threadThe modern default; memory-hard, which hurts graphics-card attacks
scryptN = 217, r = 8, p = 1 (128 MiB)Memory-hard; in Python's standard library
bcryptcost 10 or moreOlder but solid; only uses the first 72 bytes of a password
PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256600,000 iterationsUse when you need FIPS-approved algorithms

Pick the slowest settings your login servers can afford at peak, and measure: if a check takes 50 ms of one core, a login spike of 100 attempts a second needs five cores just for hashing. Better still, don't store passwords at all: use a managed identity service (Amazon Cognito, Microsoft Entra External ID, Google's Identity Platform, or a specialist provider), which does this for you.

2.3Password rules that actually help

Many familiar password rules make things worse. People forced to add a symbol write Password1!; people forced to change every 90 days write Winter2026, then Spring2027. NIST's Digital Identity Guidelines (SP 800-63B, revised in 2025) reflect what the evidence shows:

  • Length over complexity. At least 15 characters when a password is the only factor, at least 8 when it's combined with MFA; allow at least 64. No rules about mixing character types.
  • Check against breached and common passwords at sign-up and at password change, and reject matches. Services such as Have I Been Pwned's Pwned Passwords let you check without revealing the password: you send the first five characters of its SHA-1 hash and compare the returned list yourself.
  • No forced periodic changes. Force a change only when there's evidence of compromise.
  • Let password managers work: allow pasting, and don't block autofill.
  • Limit failed attempts per account, and watch failure rates across all accounts, which is how stuffing shows up.

2.4Second factors, and which ones stop phishing

Multi-factor authentication adds something you have to something you know. All of the factors below stop credential stuffing, because the attacker has the password but not the phone or key. They differ sharply against phishing.

Table 2.3 · Second factors compared
FactorHow it worksPhishingOther weaknesses
SMS or voice codeA code sent to your phone numberPhishable: you type it into the fake siteSIM swapping; the code travels over the phone network
Authenticator app code (TOTP)A code calculated from a shared secret and the time (2.5)PhishableThe secret sits on the server too
Push approval“Is this you signing in?” on a phone appPhishable; and push fatigue: attackers send prompts until someone taps yesNumber matching (type the number shown on screen) helps a lot
Security key or passkey (FIDO2/WebAuthn)The device signs a challenge with a private key for this exact site (2.6)Resistant: the signature only works for the real siteAccount recovery must be as strong (2.7)

Why can codes be phished? Because a code is just a value. A real-time phishing proxy sits between the user and the real site: it shows the real login page, passes along everything the user types, including the code, and keeps the session cookie the real site sends back. The user notices nothing. With a passkey, the browser itself checks which site is asking, and the signature it produces is only valid for that site:

2.5How a one-time code is made

An authenticator app's six-digit code is a TOTP, a time-based one-time password (RFC 6238). It's worth knowing exactly how it works, because it explains both its strengths and its limits:

  1. At enrolment, the server generates a random secret and shows it as a QR code. The app stores it; so does the server.
  2. Both sides count time steps: the Unix time divided by 30, rounded down. Every 30 seconds, the number goes up by one.
  3. Both compute an HMAC (a keyed hash, here with SHA-1) of the time step, using the secret as the key.
  4. Both take four bytes from the result, at a position chosen by its last byte, and keep the last six decimal digits. That's the code.

The server accepts the current code, usually plus or minus one step for clock drift, and should refuse a code that's already been used. Nothing is sent anywhere, which is why it works offline. But the server must store the secret in a form it can use, so a server breach exposes every secret; and a code is a value, so it can be phished.

2.6Passkeys and WebAuthn

A passkey replaces the shared secret with a key pair. It's built on the FIDO2 standards; the part browsers implement is called WebAuthn. At registration, the user's device creates a new key pair just for this site. The private key never leaves the device (or the user's password manager or platform keychain); the site stores only the public key.

At sign-in:

  1. The site sends a random challenge.
  2. The browser asks the device to sign it, and adds the origin it's actually on (https://larder.example) to what gets signed. The user unlocks the key with a fingerprint, face or PIN, which never leaves the device.
  3. The site checks the signature with the stored public key, and checks that the origin is its own.

Key idea

Passkeys resist phishing because the browser, not the user, decides which site a signature is for. There's no secret for the user to type into the wrong place.

There's also nothing worth stealing on the server: a database of public keys is useless to an attacker. Passkeys come in two kinds. Synced passkeys are backed up by the platform (Apple, Google, Microsoft or a password manager) across a person's devices; they're convenient and right for most customers. Device-bound passkeys live on one hardware security key and can't be copied; use them for administrators, where you want to know exactly where the key is.

Passkey or MFA?A passkey combines something you have (the device) with something you are or know (the unlock), so on its own it counts as phishing-resistant MFA. A security key used as a second factor after a password gives the same protection.

2.7Recovery: the back door

Every authentication system has a way in for people who've lost their phone or forgotten their password, and attackers know it. A strong passkey is worthless if “I lost my phone” to the help desk gets a new one by email. Design recovery as carefully as login:

  • Offer several registered factors (two passkeys, or a passkey and recovery codes), so losing one isn't a recovery event.
  • Give one-time recovery codes at enrolment, to be stored offline.
  • For staff, recovery goes through verified identity: a video call with a manager who knows them, or showing ID in person, never a phone call alone. Help-desk social engineering is behind many well-known breaches.
  • Notify on every change of factor or email address, through the old channel as well as the new.
  • Slow down sensitive changes after recovery: no payout-address changes for 24 hours, for example.

2.8Across providers

For your cloud accounts, you won't store passwords yourself: the provider or your identity provider does. What you choose is which factors are allowed, and for whom.

Table 2.4 · Strong authentication for cloud administrators
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Where people sign inIAM Identity Center, or your IdP through itMicrosoft Entra IDGoogle accounts (Cloud Identity / Workspace), or your IdP
Phishing-resistant optionsFIDO2 security keys and passkeyspasskeys, FIDO2 keys, Windows Hello for Business, certificate-based authenticationpasskeys and security keys
Enforcing itMFA settings in Identity Center; IAM conditions on aws:MultiFactorAuthPresentConditional Access with the “phishing-resistant MFA” authentication strength2-Step Verification enforcement, including “security keys only”, per organisational unit
The account's own superuserroot user: MFA required, ideally a security key (Chapter 7)Global Administrators in Entra IDsuper administrators in Cloud Identity / Workspace

On Azure

Microsoft Authenticator push uses number matching by default (the user types the number shown on the sign-in screen), which blunts push-fatigue attacks. For administrators, a Conditional Access policy requiring the built-in phishing-resistant MFA authentication strength allows only passkeys, FIDO2 keys, Windows Hello for Business and certificates.

On Google Cloud

Google Cloud console access is a Google account sign-in, so the controls live in the Cloud Identity or Workspace admin console: enforce 2-Step Verification, and for administrators allow only security keys. Google's Advanced Protection Program goes further for the highest-risk people.

2.9Choosing for customers, staff and administrators

There's no single right answer, because the people and the risks differ. A reasonable baseline:

  • Customers: offer passkeys and make them the easy path; keep passwords with breached-password checks and rate limits; ask for extra verification on risky actions (a new delivery address with a saved card) rather than on every login.
  • Staff: single sign-on with MFA for everything (Chapter 12); push with number matching at minimum, passkeys preferred.
  • Administrators and production access: phishing-resistant factors only: device-bound passkeys or hardware security keys, two per person.

At Larder: what changed

By Sunday afternoon, the 310 accounts were locked and their owners emailed; the 41 orders were refunded, and Lena reported the incident to the Dutch data protection authority within the 72 hours GDPR allows. In the following two weeks, Dee's mobile team and Eli shipped four changes: failed-login limits per account and per address, with an alert when the overall failure rate jumps; a breached-password check at sign-up, login and password change; a step-up check (a code sent to the account's email) before a saved card can pay for an order to a new address; and passkeys in the app and on the web. Within a month, 18 % of active customers had added a passkey.

For staff, the incident started a harder conversation. Engineers used push approvals without number matching, and the ops user from Chapter 1 had no second factor at all. Eli ordered two hardware security keys for each of the nine people with production access; Chapter 12 puts them to use.

2.10Lab: hash a password, and make a one-time code

Lab 2 · Slow hashes, salt and TOTP

You'll measure how much slower a password hash is than a plain hash, see what salt does, store and verify a password, then build TOTP from scratch, check it against the standard's test vectors and, if you like, compare it with your own authenticator app. You need Python 3.8 or later, nothing else. Cost: nothing.

passwords.pyPython 3.8+ (hashlib.scrypt needs OpenSSL, as standard builds have)

# passwords.py · fast hashes, slow hashes, salt, and a storage format
import hashlib, hmac, os, time

def fast(password):
    return hashlib.sha256(password.encode()).hexdigest()

def slow(password, salt, n=2**14, r=8, p=1):
    return hashlib.scrypt(password.encode(), salt=salt, n=n, r=r, p=p,
                          maxmem=64 * 1024 * 1024).hex()

def guesses_per_second(fn, seconds=1.0):
    count, start = 0, time.perf_counter()
    while time.perf_counter() - start < seconds:
        fn()
        count += 1
    return count / seconds

# 1. How fast can an attacker try guesses against each kind of hash?
salt = os.urandom(16)
print(f"sha256: {guesses_per_second(lambda: fast('correct horse')):>12,.0f} guesses/s")
print(f"scrypt: {guesses_per_second(lambda: slow('correct horse', salt)):>12,.0f} guesses/s")

# 2. Two customers with the same password
print("same without salt:", fast("winter2026") == fast("winter2026"))
print("same with salt:   ", slow("winter2026", os.urandom(16)) == slow("winter2026", os.urandom(16)))

# 3. What you store, and how you check a login against it
def store(password):
    salt = os.urandom(16)
    return f"scrypt$16384$8$1${salt.hex()}${slow(password, salt)}"

def verify(password, stored):
    _, n, r, p, salt, digest = stored.split("$")
    attempt = slow(password, bytes.fromhex(salt), int(n), int(r), int(p))
    return hmac.compare_digest(attempt, digest)

record = store("winter2026")
print(record[:40] + "…")
print("right password:", verify("winter2026", record))
print("wrong password:", verify("Winter2026", record))
  1. Run it with python3 passwords.py. Your numbers will differ; on the machine used for this book: 
    sha256:    2,606,019 guesses/s
scrypt:           37 guesses/s
same without salt: True
same with salt:    False
scrypt$16384$8$1$8f9a27e11ee1f73b7d26865…
right password: True
wrong password: False
    The slow hash is about 70,000 times slower per guess, and that's on one core with lab settings (N = 214, 16 MiB). An attacker with graphics cards gains a lot more speed on SHA-256 than on scrypt, because scrypt needs memory for every guess. With salt, identical passwords give different records.
  2. Make it slower. Change the defaults to n=2**17 and maxmem=256 * 1024 * 1024 (the production settings in Table 2.2) and run again. How many logins a second could one core of your machine check?

totp.pyPython 3.8+

# totp.py · time-based one-time passwords (RFC 6238), from scratch
import base64, hashlib, hmac, struct, time

def hotp(key, counter, digits=6):
    mac = hmac.new(key, struct.pack(">Q", counter), hashlib.sha1).digest()
    offset = mac[-1] & 0x0F                                  # dynamic truncation
    code = struct.unpack(">I", mac[offset:offset + 4])[0] & 0x7FFFFFFF
    return str(code % 10 ** digits).zfill(digits)

def totp(key, at=None, step=30, digits=6):
    now = time.time() if at is None else at
    return hotp(key, int(now // step), digits)

def check(key, code, at=None, window=1):
    now = time.time() if at is None else at
    return any(hmac.compare_digest(totp(key, now + d * 30), code)
               for d in range(-window, window + 1))

# 1. The test vectors from RFC 6238, appendix B (SHA-1, 8 digits)
key = b"12345678901234567890"
for t, want in [(59, "94287082"), (1111111109, "07081804"), (1234567890, "89005924")]:
    got = totp(key, t, digits=8)
    print(f"{t:>10}  {got}  {'ok' if got == want else 'WRONG'}")

# 2. A secret like the one inside an authenticator app's QR code
secret = base64.b32encode(b"larder-lab-not-real!").decode()
print("otpauth://totp/Larder:lab?secret=" + secret + "&issuer=Larder")
code = totp(base64.b32decode(secret))
print("code now:", code, "| accepted:", check(base64.b32decode(secret), code))
print("code from 5 minutes ago accepted:",
      check(base64.b32decode(secret), totp(base64.b32decode(secret), time.time() - 300)))
  1. Run it with python3 totp.py. The first three lines must say ok: your code now matches the standard. Then: 
    otpauth://totp/Larder:lab?secret=NRQXEZDFOIWWYYLCFVXG65BNOJSWC3BB&issuer=Larder
code now: 960043 | accepted: True
code from 5 minutes ago accepted: False
    Your code will differ, because the time does.
  2. Compare with a real app. In any authenticator app, add an account manually with the secret NRQXEZDFOIWWYYLCFVXG65BNOJSWC3BB, then run the script again: both show the same code. Delete the account from the app afterwards; this secret is printed in a book, so it protects nothing.
  3. Break it on purpose. Change window=1 to window=10 in check: a code from five minutes ago is now accepted, giving a phisher ten minutes instead of one. Then change step=30 to step=60 in totp and compare with the app: the codes no longer match, because both sides must agree on every parameter.

Stretch goal. Make check remember the last time step each secret was used, and refuse a code from that step or earlier. That's the replay protection RFC 6238 asks for.

2.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Fast or unsalted hashes (MD5, SHA-1, SHA-256)A stolen database is cracked in hours.Argon2id, scrypt or bcrypt with a per-password salt; or a managed identity service.
Encrypting passwords instead of hashingWhoever gets the key gets every password.One-way hashing.
Composition rules and forced 90-day changesPredictable passwords (Winter2026!).Length, breached-password checks, change on evidence.
SMS or push as the only MFA for adminsPhishing proxies and push fatigue get through.Passkeys or hardware security keys.
Recovery weaker than loginAttackers skip the passkey and call the help desk.Several factors, recovery codes, verified identity for staff.
Watching only per-account failuresStuffing tries each account once and is never locked.Alert on failure rates across all accounts and by source.

✓Chapter summary

  • Passwords fail four ways: guessing, stuffing, phishing and stolen databases. Each needs its own defence.
  • Store passwords with a salt and a slow, memory-hard hash (Argon2id or scrypt), with the settings stored alongside; or don't store them at all.
  • Good password policy is length, breached-password checks and rate limits, not composition rules or forced changes.
  • Every second factor stops credential stuffing; only passkeys and security keys stop phishing.
  • A TOTP code is an HMAC of the time with a shared secret: offline and simple, but phishable.
  • Passkeys sign a challenge bound to the real origin; the server stores only public keys.
  • Recovery is part of authentication. Make it as strong as login.

Key terms

Credential stuffing
Logging in with username–password pairs leaked from other sites.
Salt
A random per-password value hashed with it, so identical passwords give different hashes.
Password hashing function
A deliberately slow, often memory-hard hash such as Argon2id, scrypt or bcrypt.
MFA
Multi-factor authentication: two or more different kinds of factor.
TOTP
Time-based one-time password: an HMAC of the current 30-second step and a shared secret.
Phishing-resistant
An authenticator whose output can't be relayed to a site other than the one it was made for.
Passkey
A FIDO2/WebAuthn credential: a per-site key pair whose private key stays on the user's device or keychain.
Origin
The scheme, host and port of a web page, such as https://larder.example.

Check your understanding

  1. Larder's password hashes were never stolen, yet 310 accounts were taken over. Name the attack and two defences that would have helped. recall

    Show answer

    Credential stuffing. Breached-password checks, MFA or passkeys, and rate limits with alerting on the overall failure rate. Strong hashing doesn't help, because the attacker logs in normally.

  2. Why does adding a salt not make a single password any harder to crack, and why use it anyway? explain

    Show answer

    The salt is stored next to the hash, so an attacker cracking one record uses it. Its value is across records: every password must be attacked separately, identical passwords aren't revealed, and precomputed tables don't work.

  3. A login server checks 200 passwords a second at peak, and an Argon2id check takes 40 ms of CPU. How many cores does hashing need? apply

    Show answer

    200 × 0.04 s = 8 core-seconds per second, so eight cores, plus headroom for spikes and attacks (which also arrive as login attempts).

  4. An administrator uses an authenticator app code. Explain how a real-time phishing proxy gets into their account, and what would stop it. explain

    Show answer

    The proxy shows the real login page, relays the password and code to the real site as they're typed, and keeps the session cookie the real site returns. A passkey or security key stops it, because the browser signs for the fake origin and the real site rejects the signature.

  5. Design authentication for Larder's three groups: customers, pickers on shared store tablets, and engineers with production access. design

    Show answer

    Customers: passkeys first, passwords with breached checks and rate limits, step-up for risky actions. Pickers: personal badges or short PINs on a managed device, with short sessions, since the tablet is shared but the identity shouldn't be. Engineers: single sign-on with hardware security keys or device-bound passkeys, two each, and recovery through a verified call with their manager.

  6. Compare how you'd require phishing-resistant MFA for administrators on Azure and on Google Cloud. compare

    Show answer

    Azure: a Conditional Access policy for the administrator roles requiring the phishing-resistant MFA authentication strength. Google Cloud: in the Cloud Identity or Workspace admin console, enforce 2-Step Verification with security keys only for the administrators' organisational unit.

Going further

  • NIST SP 800-63B, Authentication and Authenticator Management.
  • OWASP Password Storage Cheat Sheet and MFA Cheat Sheet.
  • RFC 6238, TOTP; W3C Web Authentication; FIDO Alliance on passkeys.
  • AWS: MFA in IAM Identity Center. Azure: authentication strengths. Google: deploying 2-Step Verification.

Part I · How access works

3

Sessions and tokens

Nobody types a password for every click. After authentication, a person or program carries something instead: a session cookie, a bearer token, temporary cloud credentials. Whoever holds that thing is you until it expires. This chapter shows what these tokens contain, how to check one properly, and how to keep their lives short enough that losing one isn't a disaster.

By the end you can

  • Explain the difference between a server-side session and a self-contained token.
  • Read a JSON Web Token and list every check a server must make before trusting it.
  • Set cookie attributes that stop the common browser attacks.
  • Design token lifetimes, refresh and revocation, including “sign out everywhere”.

Before you start

  • The life of a request and the identity provider (Chapter 1, 1.5).
  • What an HMAC is (Chapter 2, 2.5). HTTP headers and cookies help (Handbook 1, Chapter 6).

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, Python 3, no cloud account
Main provider
provider-neutral, with AWS, Azure and Google Cloud in 3.8

At Larder

On Tuesday 24 November 2026, a customer's phone was stolen on the tram. That evening she changed her Larder password on the website, and felt safe. The next morning, two orders were placed from the Larder app on her stolen phone, paid with her saved card.

Changing the password hadn't touched the phone at all. The app hadn't seen her password since she first signed in, months earlier. It held two tokens: an access token the API accepted for 24 hours, and a refresh token that could fetch new ones for a year. Neither knew anything about passwords. Section 3.6 shows what Larder changed.

3.1Why sessions exist

HTTP is stateless: each request stands alone, and the server doesn't remember the last one. So after a person authenticates, the server hands back a session credential, something the client sends with every following request to say “it's still me”. There are two ways to build one:

  • A server-side session: the server stores the session (who, since when, what was proved) and gives the client only a long random session ID, usually in a cookie. To check a request, the server looks the ID up.
  • A self-contained token: the server writes the facts into the token itself and signs it. To check a request, any server with the right key verifies the signature and reads the facts, with no lookup.
Table 3.1 · Two ways to remember who's asking
Server-side sessionSelf-contained token (such as a JWT)
Client holdsa random IDthe facts, signed
Checking a requesta lookup in a session storea signature check, no lookup
Ending it earlydelete the session: immediatehard: it's valid until it expires (3.6)
Works across servicesonly those sharing the session storeany service that trusts the signer
Typical usea web app's own usersAPIs, mobile apps, single sign-on, cloud credentials

Neither is better. Self-contained tokens scale and cross boundaries easily; server-side sessions are easy to end. Most real systems use both, and the art is in combining them (3.6).

3.2Cookies, done properly

In a browser, session credentials usually travel in a cookie, which the browser stores and sends back automatically with every request to the site that set it. That automatic sending is what makes cookies convenient, and also what attackers abuse. A cookie's attributes are its defences:

Table 3.2 · Cookie attributes for a session
AttributeEffectDefends against
Securesent only over HTTPStheft on an unencrypted network
HttpOnlyinvisible to JavaScripttheft by injected script (XSS)
SameSite=Lax or Strictnot sent on requests started by other sitescross-site request forgery (CSRF)
Path=/, no Domainsent only to the exact host that set itleaking to other subdomains
Max-Age or Expireswhen the browser forgets itsessions that outlive the person's visit
__Host- name prefixthe browser enforces Secure, Path=/ and no Domainother subdomains overwriting it

A good session cookie looks like this:

Set-Cookie: __Host-session=k3Pz9Vq7xTn2Lw8RcY4bHs1dMf6Ga0Ue; Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax; Path=/; Max-Age=28800

Sessions also need two timeouts: an idle timeout (end the session after, say, 30 minutes without activity) and an absolute timeout (end it after eight hours, however active). Issue a new session ID at login and whenever privileges change, so an attacker who planted an ID before login can't ride on it (session fixation).

3.3Bearer tokens

APIs and mobile apps usually skip cookies and send a token in a header:

GET /v1/orders HTTP/1.1
Host: api.larder.example
Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9.eyJpc3MiOi...

A bearer token is exactly what the name says: whoever bears it gets access. There's no proof that the sender is the party it was issued to. That makes bearer tokens simple, and it makes every copy dangerous: in a log line, a crash report, a screenshot, a browser's history, a support ticket. Treat them like passwords, and keep them short-lived.

Bearer tokens come in two forms. An opaque token is a random string; the API asks the issuer what it means (introspection), so it behaves like a server-side session. A structured token carries its own facts, signed, and the commonest format is the JWT.

Sender-constrained tokensStandards such as DPoP and mutual TLS bind a token to a key the client holds, so a stolen copy is useless without the key too. They're increasingly common in banking and high-risk APIs.

3.4Inside a JSON Web Token

A JSON Web Token (JWT, often pronounced “jot”) is three parts separated by dots: a header, a payload of claims, and a signature. The first two are JSON, encoded with base64url (base64 with URL-safe characters and no padding). Encoded is not encrypted: anyone who has a JWT can read it.

The registered claims you'll see everywhere:

  • iss (issuer): who signed it, usually a URL.
  • sub (subject): who it's about: a user or client ID.
  • aud (audience): which API or app it's meant for.
  • exp (expiry), nbf (not before), iat (issued at): Unix times.
  • jti (JWT ID): a unique ID, useful for revocation lists and replay detection.

Plus whatever the issuer adds: scopes, roles, group names, tenant IDs. Keep that short and non-sensitive: tokens end up in logs, and every request carries the whole thing.

3.5Checking a token: every step

The signature is only the beginning. An API that receives a JWT must check all of this, in this order, before it believes a single claim:

  1. Algorithm: accept only the algorithms you expect (an allow-list), whatever the header says. Never accept none.
  2. Key: find the right key for this issuer. For asymmetric tokens, issuers publish their public keys at a JWKS URL, and the header's kid says which key signed it. Cache the keys; refresh them when an unknown kid appears.
  3. Signature: verify it, comparing in constant time.
  4. Issuer: iss must be exactly the issuer you trust.
  5. Audience: aud must be you. A valid token for another API must fail here.
  6. Time: exp in the future and nbf in the past, allowing a small leeway (30–60 seconds) for clock differences.
  7. Then authorise: the token says who; your rules still decide whether this request is allowed (Chapter 1).

Watch out: letting the token choose the algorithm

Two famous bugs come from trusting the header. With "alg": "none", some libraries skipped the signature check entirely. With algorithm confusion, an attacker changes RS256 to HS256 and signs with the issuer's public key as if it were a shared secret; a library that picks the method from the header then verifies it. Always tell your library which algorithms and keys to accept, and use a well-maintained library rather than writing your own.

HS256 or RS256? HS256 uses one shared secret to sign and verify, so every API that checks tokens could also create them: fine inside one service, wrong across many. Asymmetric algorithms (RS256, ES256) sign with a private key only the issuer has, and verify with a public key anyone can have. Identity providers use asymmetric signatures for exactly that reason.

3.6Expiry, refresh and revocation

A self-contained token can't be taken back: every API that trusts the signer will accept it until exp. So the main defence is a short life. But nobody wants to sign in every ten minutes. The standard answer is two tokens:

  • An access token: self-contained, sent with every API call, short-lived (5–15 minutes).
  • A refresh token: opaque, sent only to the identity provider to get a new access token, longer-lived, and recorded server-side, so it can be revoked.

Refresh token rotation issues a new refresh token every time one is used, and treats a second use of an old one as theft: it revokes everything descended from that sign-in. Combine that with short access tokens, and you have revocation that works within minutes:

  • Sign out revokes this device's refresh token.
  • Sign out everywhere, password change and account lock revoke all of the user's refresh tokens.
  • If you need faster than the access token's lifetime, keep a small denylist of revoked token IDs (jti) or a per-user “sessions valid after” time, and check it on sensitive actions such as payments.

Key idea

A token you can't take back must die young. Keep the power to end a session in something you can revoke.

At Larder: solved

Ben's and Dee's teams made four changes, live on 10 December. Access tokens now last 10 minutes instead of 24 hours. Refresh tokens rotate on every use, with reuse detection, and expire after 30 days without use. A password change, “sign out everywhere” (new, under Account → Devices, which lists every signed-in device) and an account lock all revoke every refresh token. And paying with a saved card checks a per-customer “sessions valid after” time, so a revoked phone can't order even in its last ten minutes.

Replayed against the new design, Tuesday's theft ends within ten minutes of the password change, and the thief's next refresh fails.

3.7Where tokens should live

  • Browsers: keep tokens out of JavaScript's reach. Storing an access token in localStorage means any injected script can read and send it. The safer pattern is a backend for frontend: the browser holds only an HttpOnly session cookie for your own server, which holds the tokens and calls the APIs.
  • Mobile apps: the platform's secure storage (Keychain on iOS, Keystore-backed storage on Android), never plain preferences or files.
  • Servers and workloads: in memory, fetched from the platform when needed (Chapter 15), not in environment files committed to Git.
  • Never in URLs (they end up in logs, browser history and the Referer header), and never logged in full. If you must log one, log its jti or a hash.

3.8Across providers

The clouds themselves run on the same ideas. When you assume a role on AWS, sign in to Azure or run code on Google Cloud, you get short-lived tokens, and the long-lived things (passwords, keys) stay elsewhere:

Table 3.3 · Short-lived credentials at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
For calling the cloud's APIsSTS temporary credentials: access key, secret and session token; 15 minutes to 12 hoursEntra ID access tokens (JWTs), typically about an hourOAuth access tokens, one hour by default
How they're renewedassume the role again, or refresh through Identity Center's sessionrefresh tokens held by the client librarythe client library, from the user's or workload's credentials
Revoking earlya deny policy on tokens issued before a time (aws:TokenIssueTime; the console's “Revoke active sessions”)revoke refresh tokens; Continuous Access Evaluation cuts access tokens on critical eventsrevoke the user's or service account's tokens; short lifetimes
For your own apps' usersAmazon Cognito: ID, access and refresh tokensEntra ID and Entra External IDIdentity Platform / Firebase Authentication

On Azure

Continuous Access Evaluation lets supporting services (Exchange, SharePoint, Microsoft Graph and others) learn about critical events, such as a disabled account, a password reset or a changed network location, and reject a still-valid access token straight away, instead of waiting for it to expire.

On Google Cloud

Google's access tokens are opaque strings, not JWTs; Google's ID tokens are JWTs. Workloads ask the metadata server for an access token when they need one, so there's nothing long-lived on disk to steal (Chapter 15).

3.9Lab: make and check a JWT

Lab 3 · Sign, read, verify and attack a token

You'll build an HS256 JWT by hand, read it without the key, and write a verifier that rejects a forged subject, an expired token, alg: none and a token for another API. Then you'll remove checks and watch attacks get through. You need Python 3. Cost: nothing. (In real code, use a maintained library such as PyJWT; writing one teaches what the library does for you.)

tokens.pyPython 3.8+

# tokens.py · make and check a JSON Web Token (HS256) with the standard library
import base64, hashlib, hmac, json, time

def b64url(data):
    return base64.urlsafe_b64encode(data).rstrip(b"=").decode()

def unb64url(text):
    return base64.urlsafe_b64decode(text + "=" * (-len(text) % 4))

def encode(part):
    return b64url(json.dumps(part, separators=(",", ":")).encode())

def sign(claims, key):
    signing_input = encode({"alg": "HS256", "typ": "JWT"}) + "." + encode(claims)
    signature = hmac.new(key, signing_input.encode(), hashlib.sha256).digest()
    return signing_input + "." + b64url(signature)

def verify(token, key, issuer, audience, now=None, leeway=30):
    now = time.time() if now is None else now
    try:
        h64, p64, s64 = token.split(".")
        header, claims = json.loads(unb64url(h64)), json.loads(unb64url(p64))
    except ValueError:
        return "rejected: malformed"
    if header.get("alg") != "HS256":                  # an allow-list; never obey the header
        return f"rejected: alg {header.get('alg')}"
    expected = hmac.new(key, f"{h64}.{p64}".encode(), hashlib.sha256).digest()
    if not hmac.compare_digest(expected, unb64url(s64)):
        return "rejected: bad signature"
    if claims.get("iss") != issuer:
        return "rejected: wrong issuer"
    if claims.get("aud") != audience:
        return "rejected: wrong audience"
    if now > claims.get("exp", 0) + leeway:
        return "rejected: expired"
    scope = f" may {claims['scope']}" if "scope" in claims else ""
    return f"ok: {claims['sub']}{scope}"

if __name__ == "__main__":
    KEY = b"lab-only-key-printed-in-a-book-1"
    NOW = 1795597200                                   # 25 Nov 2026, 09:00 UTC
    ISS, AUD = "https://login.larder.example", "larder-api"
    claims = {"iss": ISS, "sub": "customer-48213", "aud": AUD,
              "iat": NOW, "exp": NOW + 600, "scope": "orders:write"}
    token = sign(claims, KEY)
    print(token, "\n")
    print("anyone can read it:", unb64url(token.split(".")[1]).decode(), "\n")

    h64, p64, s64 = token.split(".")
    forged_claims = encode(dict(claims, sub="customer-1"))
    none_token = encode({"alg": "none", "typ": "JWT"}) + "." + p64 + "."
    tests = [
        ("as issued",            token,                          NOW + 60),
        ("someone else's id",    f"{h64}.{forged_claims}.{s64}", NOW + 60),
        ("an hour later",        token,                          NOW + 3600),
        ('alg "none"',           none_token,                     NOW + 60),
        ("for another API",      sign(dict(claims, aud="larder-admin"), KEY), NOW + 60),
    ]
    for name, t, at in tests:
        print(f"{name:<20} {verify(t, KEY, ISS, AUD, now=at)}")
  1. Run it with python3 tokens.py. The clock is fixed, so you'll see exactly this: 
    eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9.eyJpc3MiOiJodHRwczovL2xvZ2luLmxhcmRlci5leGFtcGxlIiwic3ViIjoiY3VzdG9tZXItNDgyMTMiLCJhdWQiOiJsYXJkZXItYXBpIiwiaWF0IjoxNzk1NTk3MjAwLCJleHAiOjE3OTU1OTc4MDAsInNjb3BlIjoib3JkZXJzOndyaXRlIn0.50-ZrTCS8tR64tATvnFMEdyxU3Esy6ddzmGvzO14c-I

anyone can read it: {"iss":"https://login.larder.example","sub":"customer-48213","aud":"larder-api","iat":1795597200,"exp":1795597800,"scope":"orders:write"}

as issued            ok: customer-48213 may orders:write
someone else's id    rejected: bad signature
an hour later        rejected: expired
alg "none"           rejected: alg none
for another API      rejected: wrong audience
  2. Read a token without the key. Paste the token into any JWT decoder, or decode the middle part yourself with unb64url. Nothing in a JWT is secret unless it's encrypted (JWE), which is rare.
  3. Break it on purpose. Remove the two lines that check aud, and run again: the token meant for larder-admin is now accepted by larder-api. Put them back. Then set leeway=7200: the hour-old token is accepted. Finally, replace the algorithm check with if header.get("alg") == "none": return "ok: " + claims["sub"], the bug some early libraries had in spirit, and watch the unsigned token get in.

Stretch goals. (1) Add a jti to the claims and a REVOKED set to the verifier; revoke a token and check it's refused before it expires. (2) Install PyJWT (pip install pyjwt) and verify the same token with jwt.decode(token, KEY, algorithms=["HS256"], audience="larder-api", issuer=ISS, options={"verify_exp": False}). Then try it without algorithms=: the library refuses, which is the right design.

3.10Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Long-lived access tokensA stolen phone or leaked log grants access for hours or days.5–15 minute access tokens with refresh.
Password change doesn't end sessionsThe thief keeps going, as at Larder.Revoke refresh tokens on password change and account lock.
Skipping aud or iss checksTokens for other apps, or from other issuers, are accepted.Check every claim in 3.5; use a library and configure it.
Trusting the header's algnone and algorithm-confusion attacks.An explicit allow-list of algorithms and keys.
Tokens in localStorage, URLs or logsStolen by injected scripts or read from logs and history.HttpOnly cookies with a backend for frontend; never log tokens.
Sensitive data in claimsAnyone holding the token can read it.IDs and scopes only; look up the rest.

✓Chapter summary

  • After authentication, a session credential stands in for the person: a server-side session ID or a self-contained signed token.
  • Session cookies need Secure, HttpOnly, SameSite, a narrow scope, and idle and absolute timeouts.
  • A bearer token works for whoever holds it. Treat every copy like a password.
  • A JWT is a readable header and payload plus a signature. Check algorithm, key, signature, issuer, audience and time, then authorise.
  • Self-contained tokens can't be taken back: keep access tokens short, and put revocable power in rotating refresh tokens.
  • Password changes and “sign out everywhere” must revoke refresh tokens.
  • Keep tokens out of JavaScript, URLs and logs.

Key terms

Session
The state that lets a server recognise later requests after one authentication.
Bearer token
A token that grants access to whoever presents it.
JWT
JSON Web Token: base64url-encoded header and claims, plus a signature.
Claim
A statement in a token, such as sub, aud or exp.
Audience
The service a token is meant for; others must reject it.
Access token
A short-lived token sent to APIs.
Refresh token
A longer-lived, revocable token used only to get new access tokens.
Rotation
Issuing a new refresh token at each use, and treating reuse as theft.
JWKS
JSON Web Key Set: an issuer's published public keys.

Check your understanding

  1. Why didn't changing her password stop the thief at Larder? recall

    Show answer

    The phone held an access token and a refresh token, neither tied to the password. The API accepted the access token for 24 hours, and nothing revoked the refresh token when the password changed.

  2. You receive a JWT whose signature is valid. List what else you must check before acting on it. apply

    Show answer

    That the algorithm was one you allow (checked before the signature), the issuer is the one you trust, the audience is your service, it hasn't expired and isn't used before nbf, and then whether this subject is authorised for this request.

  3. Explain why an identity provider uses RS256 or ES256 rather than HS256 for tokens many APIs will check. explain

    Show answer

    With HS256, every API that can verify a token holds the same secret and could also forge one. With an asymmetric algorithm, only the provider holds the private signing key; APIs verify with the public key, which can't sign.

  4. A security reviewer says “your access tokens last 15 minutes, so a fired employee keeps access for up to 15 minutes”. When is that acceptable, and what would you add when it isn't? design

    Show answer

    Acceptable for most actions, since refresh is revoked immediately. For sensitive actions (payments, admin changes, data exports), check a denylist of token IDs or a per-user “valid after” time, or require fresh authentication.

  5. Which cookie attribute stops a script injected into your page from reading the session cookie, and which stops another site making the user's browser send it? recall

    Show answer

    HttpOnly hides it from JavaScript. SameSite=Lax or Strict stops it being sent on most cross-site requests.

  6. Compare how you'd cut off a compromised identity's cloud API access early on AWS and on Azure. compare

    Show answer

    AWS: temporary credentials can't be recalled, so attach a policy that denies everything for sessions issued before now (aws:TokenIssueTime), which the console's “Revoke active sessions” does for roles, and disable the source identity. Azure: revoke the user's refresh tokens and disable the account; Continuous Access Evaluation makes supporting services reject existing access tokens quickly.

Going further

  • RFC 7519, JSON Web Token, and RFC 8725, JWT Best Current Practices.
  • OWASP Session Management Cheat Sheet.
  • RFC 9700, OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice (refresh token rotation and sender-constrained tokens).
  • AWS: Revoking role session credentials. Azure: Continuous Access Evaluation.

Part I · How access works

4

OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect

“Sign in with…”, “Allow this app to read your calendar?”, a CLI that opens a browser to log you in, a pipeline that deploys to the cloud without a key: all of these are OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect. This chapter explains the two standards from the problem they solve, walks through the one flow you'll use most, and gives you the checklist that keeps it safe.

By the end you can

  • Name the four OAuth roles in any real integration.
  • Walk through the authorization code flow with PKCE, and say what each parameter protects against.
  • Choose the right grant for a web app, a mobile app, a CLI and a backend service.
  • Tell an ID token from an access token, and validate each where it belongs.

Before you start

  • Tokens, JWTs and refresh tokens (Chapter 3). The lab reuses tokens.py from Lab 3.
  • HTTP redirects help (Handbook 1, Chapter 6).

This chapter

Reading
about 50 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, Python 3, no cloud account
Main provider
provider-neutral, with AWS, Azure and Google Cloud in 4.8

At Larder

On Tuesday 1 December 2026, Korenwolf's finance team emailed Mo, Larder's key account manager. Their expense system, a SaaS product called Spendwise, could import supplier invoices automatically. All it needed, the email said, was the Larder username and password of one of Korenwolf's office managers, “so it can log in and download the PDFs each month”.

Mo forwarded it to Eli with one line: “Is this OK?” It isn't. Spendwise would hold a password that can also place and cancel orders, change delivery addresses and see every office's history. It would work until the office manager changed jobs, and then break, or keep working for someone who's gone. The problem has a standard answer, and section 4.9 shows Larder adopting it.

4.1The problem: access without the password

Spendwise's request is the password anti-pattern: one application asks for your password to another. It gives the app everything you can do, for as long as the password lasts, and you can't take it back from that app alone. It also teaches people to type passwords into other sites, which is exactly what phishing relies on (Chapter 2).

OAuth 2.0 (RFC 6749) solves delegated authorisation: letting an application act on your behalf, with limited permissions, without ever seeing your password. You sign in at the service that holds your data, approve exactly what the app may do, and the app receives a token for that and nothing more. You can revoke it at any time without changing your password.

OAuth on its own says nothing about who you are; it's about what an app may do. OpenID Connect (OIDC) is a thin layer on top that adds sign-in: it tells the app who authenticated. Section 4.6 separates them.

4.2Four roles

Every OAuth integration has the same four parties. Name them first, and the rest of the protocol makes sense:

Table 4.1 · The four OAuth roles, in the Spendwise case
RoleWhat it isHere
Resource ownerthe person who can grant access to the dataKorenwolf's office manager
Clientthe application that wants accessSpendwise
Authorization serverauthenticates the owner, asks for consent, issues tokenslogin.larder.example
Resource serverthe API holding the data; accepts access tokensapi.larder.example (invoices)

Clients also come in two kinds, and the difference matters for every flow. A confidential client runs on a server and can keep a secret (a client secret or a private key) to authenticate itself to the authorization server; Spendwise is one. A public client runs on the user's device (a single-page app, a mobile app, a CLI) where anything it contains can be extracted, so it can't keep a secret.

4.3The authorization code flow with PKCE

The flow you'll use for nearly every app that acts for a person is the authorization code flow, with PKCE (Proof Key for Code Exchange, pronounced “pixie”, RFC 7636). It's worth learning step by step:

Each parameter is there to stop a specific attack:

  • redirect_uri: where the code is sent. The authorization server must compare it exactly with the URIs registered for this client. A loose match (any path, any subdomain) lets an attacker send codes to a page they control.
  • state: a random value the client stores and checks when the browser returns. It ties the response to the request this browser started, which stops an attacker from injecting their own code into your session (a form of CSRF).
  • code: a one-time, short-lived (seconds to minutes) reference to the grant, safe to pass through the browser because it's useless on its own.
  • code_verifier and code_challenge: PKCE. The client makes a random verifier, sends only its SHA-256 hash (the challenge) in step ①, and reveals the verifier only on the back channel in step ⑤. Someone who steals the code from a redirect, a log or a malicious app on the same phone can't redeem it without the verifier.

Key idea

Use the authorization code flow with PKCE for every client that acts for a person: web, mobile, single-page or CLI, public or confidential.

PKCE was invented for mobile apps, which can't keep a client secret, but current guidance (OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice, RFC 9700, and the upcoming OAuth 2.1) recommends it for confidential clients too, because it also protects against code injection. Confidential clients additionally authenticate at the token endpoint with their secret or, better, a signed assertion with a private key.

4.4Scopes and consent

A scope names a slice of access the client asks for: invoices:read, orders:write, openid. The authorization server shows the requested scopes on a consent screen, the user approves them (or a company administrator approves them for everyone), and the access token carries only the approved ones. The API must check the scope on every request, in addition to its own rules about the user: a token with invoices:read for an office manager lets the client read that manager's invoices, not anyone's.

Good scope design is coarse enough to understand on a consent screen and fine enough that common integrations need little: separate read from write, and money-moving actions from everything else. Ask for the minimum, and ask for more later when a feature needs it (incremental consent).

Consent phishingAttackers register real OAuth apps with convincing names and ask for broad scopes, such as reading all mail. Nothing is stolen; the user grants it. Companies limit which apps employees may approve, and review granted apps (Chapter 23).

4.5The other grants

The “grant type” is how a client gets its token. Besides the authorization code, you'll meet these:

Table 4.2 · OAuth grant types
GrantUse it forHow it works
Authorization code + PKCEany app acting for a person4.3
Client credentialsa service acting for itself (no person involved)the client authenticates with its own secret or key and gets a token for its own permissions
Device authorizationdevices without a good browser: TVs, CLIs on remote machinesthe device shows a short code; the user enters it on their phone or laptop; the device polls until approved
Refresh tokengetting a new access tokenChapter 3, 3.6
Token exchangeswapping one token for another, such as a CI system's token for cloud credentialsChapter 17 uses the same idea
Implicit (deprecated)nothingreturned tokens in the browser URL; replaced by code + PKCE
Password (deprecated)nothingthe client collects the user's password: the anti-pattern of 4.1 in protocol form

You've probably used the device grant already: aws sso login and az login --use-device-code can both show a code to enter in a browser, which is handy on a machine you've connected to over SSH.

4.6OpenID Connect: adding sign-in

People used OAuth for sign-in long before it was designed for it: “if this token can read my profile, I must be this person”. That's unsafe, because an access token says what a client may do, not who logged in to this client; a token issued to another app could be replayed. OpenID Connect fixes this by adding:

  • The openid scope, which asks for sign-in.
  • An ID token: a JWT for the client, saying who authenticated, when and how. Its aud is the client's own ID.
  • A nonce, sent in step ① and returned inside the ID token, so the client can check the token was made for this sign-in.
  • A UserInfo endpoint for extra profile details, and discovery: every OIDC provider publishes its endpoints, supported features and signing keys (JWKS) at /.well-known/openid-configuration.
Table 4.3 · ID token or access token?
ID tokenAccess token
Defined byOpenID ConnectOAuth 2.0
Meant forthe client (aud = client ID)the API (resource server)
Sayswho signed in, when, how (sub, auth_time, amr)what the client may do (scopes)
Formatalways a JWTJWT or opaque; the client shouldn't care
Neversend it to an API as an access tokenuse it as proof that a user logged in

The client validates an ID token with the checklist from Chapter 3 (3.5), with two OIDC specifics: aud must be its own client ID, and the nonce must match the one it sent. Once validated, the stable identifier for the person is the pair iss + sub, not the email address, which can change or be reassigned.

4.7The security checklist

  1. Authorization code with PKCE for every client acting for a person. No implicit, no password grant.
  2. Exact redirect URI matching, registered per client; HTTPS only (or the platform's app-link schemes on mobile).
  3. state and nonce generated per request, stored, and checked.
  4. Mobile and desktop apps use the system browser, not an embedded web view, so the app can't read the password and the user's existing session (and passkeys) work.
  5. Minimal scopes, checked by the API on every call, together with its own rules about the user.
  6. Short access tokens, rotating refresh tokens (Chapter 3), revoked when the user disconnects the app.
  7. Validate tokens with a maintained library, configured with the issuer, audience and algorithms.

4.8Across providers

Every big cloud runs an OIDC provider for its own sign-in, sells one for your apps' users, and accepts OIDC tokens from outside as a way for workloads to get cloud credentials without keys:

Table 4.4 · OAuth and OIDC at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
