Identity for your app's usersAmazon Cognito user poolsMicrosoft Entra External IDIdentity Platform
Registering a clientan app client in the user poolan app registration (client ID, redirect URIs, secrets or certificates)an OAuth client ID in the project
Your employees signing in to appsIAM Identity Center applicationsEntra ID enterprise applicationsCloud Identity / Workspace SSO
Trusting outside OIDC tokensIAM OIDC identity providers + AssumeRoleWithWebIdentityworkload identity federation on an app or managed identityworkload identity federation pools

On Azure

The Microsoft identity platform uses the same flows with its own vocabulary: an app registration defines the client; a service principal is that app's identity in a tenant; scopes for Microsoft Graph look like User.Read; and administrators can grant consent for the whole organisation. Restricting which apps users may consent to is one of the first settings to review in a new tenant.

On Google Cloud

OAuth clients live in a project with a configured consent screen; apps that ask for sensitive Google scopes, such as reading Gmail, must pass Google's verification before they can be used by the public. Google's ID tokens are also used to call your own Cloud Run services, where the audience is the service's URL.

4.9At Larder: Spendwise, done properly

At Larder: solved

Eli's reply to Mo: no passwords, but yes to Spendwise. Larder's login service already speaks OAuth and OIDC for its own apps, so in the second week of December Eli's team registered Spendwise as a confidential client with one exact redirect URI, added a new scope, invoices:read, and made the invoices API check it. An office manager clicks “Connect Larder” in Spendwise, signs in at login.larder.example (with a passkey, if they have one), and approves one line: Spendwise wants to read your office's Larder invoices. Spendwise never sees a password, can't place orders, and is disconnected from the office manager's account page with one click, or automatically when the person leaves Korenwolf.

The work turned up one more thing. Larder's own mobile app had been using the deprecated password grant since 2024: it collected the password in its own screen and posted it to the token endpoint. Dee's team moved it to the system browser with code + PKCE in the same release, which is also what made passkeys (Chapter 2) work in the app.

4.10Lab: play out the flow

Lab 4 · Authorization code with PKCE, in one file

You'll compute a PKCE challenge and check it against the standard, build an authorization request, play a tiny authorization server that refuses a stolen code, then validate an ID token as the client and an access token as the API. You need Python 3 and tokens.py from Lab 3 in the same folder. Cost: nothing. (Real authorization servers sign with RS256 and publish keys; this lab reuses Lab 3's HS256 to stay small.)

oauth.pyPython 3.8+, next to tokens.py

# oauth.py · the authorization code flow with PKCE, played out in one file
# Save next to tokens.py from Lab 3; it reuses sign() and verify().
import base64, hashlib, hmac, json, secrets
from urllib.parse import urlencode
from tokens import sign, verify, unb64url

def b64url(data):
    return base64.urlsafe_b64encode(data).rstrip(b"=").decode()

def challenge_for(verifier):
    return b64url(hashlib.sha256(verifier.encode()).digest())   # the S256 method

# 1. Check against RFC 7636, appendix B
rfc_verifier = "dBjftJeZ4CVP-mB92K27uhbUJU1p1r_wW1gFWFOEjXk"
print("RFC 7636 vector:", challenge_for(rfc_verifier) == "E9Melhoa2OwvFrEMTJguCHaoeK1t8URWbuGJSstw-cM")

# 2. The client (an expense tool) starts the flow
CLIENT_ID, REDIRECT = "spendwise", "https://app.spendwise.example/callback/larder"
verifier = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)          # kept secret by the client
state, nonce = secrets.token_urlsafe(16), secrets.token_urlsafe(16)
print("\nsend the browser to:")
print("https://login.larder.example/authorize?" + urlencode({
    "response_type": "code", "client_id": CLIENT_ID, "redirect_uri": REDIRECT,
    "scope": "openid invoices:read", "state": state, "nonce": nonce,
    "code_challenge": challenge_for(verifier), "code_challenge_method": "S256"})[:96] + "…")

# 3. The authorization server: the user signs in and consents; a one-time code is issued
KEY, ISS, NOW = b"lab-only-key-printed-in-a-book-1", "https://login.larder.example", 1795597200
CODES = {}
def authorize(challenge, redirect_uri, nonce):
    code = secrets.token_urlsafe(16)
    CODES[code] = {"challenge": challenge, "redirect_uri": redirect_uri, "nonce": nonce,
                   "sub": "office-manager-207", "scope": "openid invoices:read"}
    return code

def token(code, code_verifier, redirect_uri):
    grant = CODES.pop(code, None)                   # a code works once
    if grant is None:
        return "error: invalid_grant (unknown or used code)"
    if redirect_uri != grant["redirect_uri"]:
        return "error: invalid_grant (redirect_uri)"
    if not hmac.compare_digest(challenge_for(code_verifier), grant["challenge"]):
        return "error: invalid_grant (PKCE verifier)"
    access = sign({"iss": ISS, "sub": grant["sub"], "aud": "larder-api", "iat": NOW,
                   "exp": NOW + 600, "scope": "invoices:read"}, KEY)
    id_token = sign({"iss": ISS, "sub": grant["sub"], "aud": CLIENT_ID, "iat": NOW,
                     "exp": NOW + 600, "nonce": grant["nonce"]}, KEY)
    return {"access_token": access, "id_token": id_token, "token_type": "Bearer", "expires_in": 600}

code = authorize(challenge_for(verifier), REDIRECT, nonce)

# 4. An attacker who intercepted the code, but not the verifier, tries first
print("\nattacker:", token(code, secrets.token_urlsafe(32), REDIRECT))
code = authorize(challenge_for(verifier), REDIRECT, nonce)   # the user tries again
result = token(code, verifier, REDIRECT)
print("client:  ", sorted(result))
print("again:   ", token(code, verifier, REDIRECT))

# 5. The client checks the ID token (it's for the client), the API checks the access token
print("\nID token for client:   ", verify(result["id_token"], KEY, ISS, CLIENT_ID, now=NOW + 5))
print("nonce matches:         ", json.loads(unb64url(result["id_token"].split(".")[1]))["nonce"] == nonce)
print("access token for API:  ", verify(result["access_token"], KEY, ISS, "larder-api", now=NOW + 5))
print("ID token sent to API:  ", verify(result["id_token"], KEY, ISS, "larder-api", now=NOW + 5))
  1. Run it with python3 oauth.py. The random values differ each time; the results don't: 
    RFC 7636 vector: True

send the browser to:
https://login.larder.example/authorize?response_type=code&client_id=spendwise&redirect_uri=https%3A%2F%2Fapp.spendwise.example%2Fcallba…

attacker: error: invalid_grant (PKCE verifier)
client:   ['access_token', 'expires_in', 'id_token', 'token_type']
again:    error: invalid_grant (unknown or used code)

ID token for client:    ok: office-manager-207
nonce matches:          True
access token for API:   ok: office-manager-207 may invoices:read
ID token sent to API:   rejected: wrong audience
    Read it against Figure 4.2: the attacker had a real code but not the verifier; a code works once; the ID token is good for the client and useless at the API.
  2. Remove the slice [:96] + "…" and look at the full authorization URL. Find the challenge in it, and note that the verifier appears nowhere.
  3. Break it on purpose. In token(), delete the PKCE check: the attacker now gets tokens with a stolen code. Put it back, then change the redirect check to if not redirect_uri.startswith("https://app.spendwise.example") and pass "https://app.spendwise.example.evil.example/x" as the redirect URI: a loose prefix match accepts an attacker's domain.

Stretch goal. Look at a real provider's discovery document: curl -s https://accounts.google.com/.well-known/openid-configuration. Find the authorization and token endpoints, the JWKS URL, and the list of supported code challenge methods.

4.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Sharing passwords with other appsUnlimited, irrevocable access for the app.OAuth with narrow scopes.
Implicit or password grantTokens in URLs, or apps collecting passwords.Authorization code + PKCE.
Loose redirect URI matchingCodes delivered to attacker-controlled pages.Exact matching of registered URIs.
No state or nonceAn attacker's code or token injected into a user's session.Random per request, stored and checked.
Using access tokens as proof of loginTokens for another app let attackers sign in as the user.OIDC ID tokens with aud = your client ID.
Email address as the user's keyAccount takeover when an address is reused or changed.iss + sub.
Embedded web views for loginThe app can read the password; no passkeys or existing session.The system browser.

✓Chapter summary

  • OAuth 2.0 is delegated authorisation: an app gets a scoped, revocable token instead of your password. OpenID Connect adds sign-in with an ID token.
  • Four roles: resource owner, client, authorization server, resource server.
  • Use authorization code + PKCE for anything acting for a person; client credentials for services acting for themselves; the device grant for devices without a browser.
  • redirect_uri (exact), state, one-time codes and PKCE each block a specific attack.
  • Scopes limit the client; the API still applies its own rules about the user.
  • ID tokens are for the client, access tokens for the API. Identify people by iss + sub.

Key terms

OAuth 2.0
A framework for granting applications limited access to resources on a user's behalf.
OpenID Connect
An identity layer on OAuth 2.0 that adds sign-in and ID tokens.
Client
The application requesting access; confidential (can keep a secret) or public.
Authorization server
Authenticates the user, collects consent and issues tokens.
Scope
A named slice of access a client requests and a token carries.
Authorization code
A short-lived, one-time reference exchanged for tokens on the back channel.
PKCE
Proof Key for Code Exchange: a secret verifier that proves the redeemer of a code started the flow.
ID token
An OIDC JWT, addressed to the client, describing the authentication.
Discovery
The /.well-known/openid-configuration document describing a provider.

Check your understanding

  1. Name the four OAuth roles when you let a photo-printing website read your photos from a cloud drive. recall

    Show answer

    Resource owner: you. Client: the printing website. Authorization server: the cloud drive provider's sign-in service. Resource server: the cloud drive's photo API.

  2. An attacker's app on the same phone intercepts the redirect carrying an authorization code. Why can't it get tokens? explain

    Show answer

    The token endpoint needs the PKCE code verifier, which only the real app has; the redirect carried only the code, and the challenge sent earlier is a one-way hash.

  3. Which grant would you use for: a nightly job that pulls Larder's invoices for Korenwolf's finance system with no person present; a smart TV app; a single-page web app? apply

    Show answer

    A person must consent once, so: the authorization code flow once, then refresh tokens for the nightly job (or client credentials if Larder offers business-level access without a user). Device authorization for the TV. Authorization code + PKCE for the single-page app, ideally through a backend for frontend.

  4. Your API receives an ID token in the Authorization header. What should it do, and why? apply

    Show answer

    Reject it: its audience is the client, not the API, and it carries no scopes. Accepting it would let any app that received an ID token for the user call your API as them.

  5. Design the scopes for Larder's business API, used by Spendwise and future integrations. design

    Show answer

    Something like invoices:read, orders:read, orders:write, standing-orders:write and account:read, keeping money-moving and address-changing actions separate from reading. Every call also checks that the user belongs to the business account whose data is requested.

  6. Compare how you'd register an app that signs in employees on Azure and on AWS. compare

    Show answer

    Azure: an app registration in Entra ID with redirect URIs and credentials, assigned to users or groups as an enterprise application. AWS: an application in IAM Identity Center (or a Cognito user pool federated with the company IdP), assigned to users or groups.

Going further

  • RFC 6749, OAuth 2.0; RFC 7636, PKCE; RFC 9700, OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice; RFC 8252, OAuth for native apps.
  • OpenID Connect Core 1.0.
  • OAuth 2.1, which folds in today's best practice.
  • Azure: Microsoft identity platform and the authorization code flow. AWS: Cognito OAuth endpoints.

Part I · How access works

5

SAML and federation

Federation means trusting someone else's login: your partner's staff sign in with their own company accounts, and your employees reach a hundred SaaS tools and your cloud consoles with one. Much of that still runs on SAML 2.0, an XML standard from 2005 that every enterprise identity provider speaks. This chapter explains how SAML works, what an assertion says, how to check one, and how to decide how far to trust the identity provider that sent it.

By the end you can

  • Explain identity providers, service providers and the metadata that connects them.
  • Follow an SP-initiated SAML sign-in, and say why IdP-initiated sign-in is riskier.
  • Read a SAML assertion and list every check a service provider must make.
  • Choose between SAML and OpenID Connect for a new integration.
  • Limit what a federated identity provider can vouch for.

Before you start

  • Trust and identity providers (Chapter 1, 1.5–1.6).
  • Token checks (Chapter 3, 3.5) and OpenID Connect (Chapter 4).

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, Python 3, no cloud account
Main provider
provider-neutral, with AWS, Azure and Google Cloud in 5.8

At Larder

Korenwolf's three offices start their Larder for Business service in January 2027, and Korenwolf IT's 40-section solution design template has a firm line in it: all supplier portals used by staff must use single sign-on with the Korenwolf identity provider, via SAML 2.0. Their office managers will sign in to Larder with their Korenwolf accounts, and when someone leaves Korenwolf, their access to Larder must end with their Korenwolf account. Eli has until 18 December to make it work.

The protocol is the easy part. The question that takes Eli a week is a different one: if Larder trusts Korenwolf's identity provider to say who someone is, what stops it saying something Larder shouldn't believe? Section 5.7 answers it.

5.1What federation is for

Federation is an agreement between two systems: one authenticates people, the other accepts its word. The person signs in once, with the account their organisation manages, and gets into applications run by someone else. Everyone gains something:

  • The person has one login and one set of factors, instead of a password per supplier.
  • Their organisation keeps control: it decides who may use the app, enforces its MFA rules, and ends access by disabling one account.
  • The application stores no passwords for these people, and doesn't run their resets.

The same idea runs inside your own company: employees sign in to the AWS console, Slack and Jira through one identity provider (Chapter 12). And it works between machines, when a CI system's token is accepted by a cloud (Chapter 17). This chapter is about people and SAML; the principles carry over.

5.2Identity providers, service providers and metadata

SAML has two roles. The identity provider (IdP) authenticates the person and issues a signed statement about them: Korenwolf's Microsoft Entra ID. The service provider (SP) is the application that relies on it: Larder's login service. (OpenID Connect calls the SP a relying party.)

Before anyone signs in, the two exchange metadata, an XML document each publishes about itself. It's how trust is written down (Chapter 1, 1.6):

Table 5.1 · What each side's metadata tells the other
FromFieldLarder–Korenwolf example
IdPentity ID: the IdP's unique namehttps://sts.korenwolf.example/7c1d
IdPsingle sign-on URL: where to send peoplehttps://login.korenwolf.example/saml2
IdPsigning certificate: the public key that checks its signaturesan X.509 certificate, valid three years
SPentity ID: the SP's unique name, used as the audiencehttps://login.larder.example/saml
SPassertion consumer service (ACS) URL: where responses are postedhttps://login.larder.example/saml/acs/korenwolf
SPrequested attributes and name ID formatemail, office, groups; persistent ID

The certificate is the heart of it: the SP will believe any assertion signed with the matching private key. Certificates expire, and an unnoticed expiry stops every sign-in at once, so put the date in a calendar and agree how rollovers will work (most IdPs can publish the next certificate in advance).

5.3A sign-in, step by step

SAML moves messages through the user's browser, using redirects and auto-submitting HTML forms; the IdP and SP never talk directly during a sign-in. The usual flow starts at the application, so it's called SP-initiated:

Step ① has a name: home realm discovery, working out which IdP a person belongs to, here from the email domain. In step ② the SP remembers the request's ID; in step ⑥ it checks that the response answers that request (InResponseTo).

SAML can also start at the IdP: the person clicks a Larder tile in Korenwolf's app portal, and the IdP posts an unsolicited response straight to Larder. This IdP-initiated sign-in is convenient, but there's no request to match it to, so the SP can't tell a fresh sign-in from a response captured and replayed, or injected into someone else's browser. Prefer SP-initiated; if you must accept IdP-initiated, keep validity windows short and keep a replay cache.

5.4Inside an assertion

The SAML response carries an assertion: the IdP's signed statement about the person. Strip away the namespaces and it reads quite naturally:

Two details deserve attention:

  • The NameID identifies the person to this SP. Prefer a persistent format, an opaque ID that never changes for this person and this SP. An email address as NameID breaks when someone marries and changes their name, and can be reassigned to a new employee later. Together with the issuer, the NameID is the key for the account, like iss + sub in OIDC.
  • Attributes are whatever the IdP is configured to release: names, email, department, group memberships. The SP should ask for the minimum, and treat each one as a claim made by the IdP, which is only as trustworthy as the IdP's own data.

5.5Checking an assertion

A SAML response arrives as a form post from a browser, which anyone can make. Before trusting it, the SP must check:

  1. Signature: the assertion (or the whole response) is signed, the signature is valid, and it was made with the certificate from this IdP's metadata, not one embedded in the message.
  2. Issuer: exactly the IdP this connection trusts.
  3. Audience: your SP's entity ID.
  4. Recipient and destination: your ACS URL for this connection.
  5. Time: now is between NotBefore and NotOnOrAfter, with a small allowance for clock differences.
  6. InResponseTo: answers a request you sent and haven't seen answered.
  7. One use: the assertion ID hasn't been seen before (keep a cache until its expiry).
  8. Then your own rules about what this IdP may say (5.7), and map the attributes to the account.

Watch out: never write your own SAML validation

XML signatures sign a part of a document, identified by an ID. In signature wrapping attacks, the attacker keeps the validly signed part where the signature check finds it, and adds a second, unsigned assertion where the application reads its data. Code that checks one element and reads another is fooled. These bugs keep reappearing, even in mature libraries. Use a well-maintained SAML library or, better, a managed identity service, and keep it updated.

5.6SAML or OpenID Connect?

Both protocols federate sign-in, and most identity providers speak both. They differ in age and in what they're good at:

Table 5.2 · SAML 2.0 and OpenID Connect side by side
SAML 2.0OpenID Connect
Since20052014
FormatXML, XML signaturesJSON, JWTs
Transportthrough the browser (redirects and form posts)browser redirects plus a back channel for tokens
Mobile and native appsawkwarddesigned for them (Chapter 4)
Calling APIs afterwardsnot part of the standardOAuth access tokens, in the same flow
Where it's strongenterprise SaaS, older IdPs, cloud console federationnew apps, mobile, APIs, workload federation

For a new integration where both sides can choose, choose OpenID Connect: it's simpler to implement correctly and covers apps and APIs. Support SAML when your customers' identity providers require it, as Korenwolf's does: in enterprise sales it's often a checkbox you can't skip.

5.7How far to trust a partner's IdP

Here is the question that kept Eli busy. Once Larder trusts assertions signed by Korenwolf's IdP, those assertions can say anything: any email address, any group, any office. A misconfiguration at Korenwolf, a malicious administrator there, or an attacker who takes over their IdP could produce a perfectly signed assertion for eli@larder.example, or for an office manager at a different Larder business customer. The signature proves who said it, not that it's true.

Key idea

A federated IdP should only be able to vouch for its own people, inside its own organisation's account, with the attributes you need, and nothing more.

In practice, that means four rules on the SP side:

  1. Bind the connection to one tenant. Accounts created through Korenwolf's connection belong to Korenwolf's business account, always. No attribute in an assertion can move someone into another customer's account, or into Larder's staff tools.
  2. Bind it to the organisation's domains. Only email addresses at domains Korenwolf has proved it owns (korenwolf.example) are accepted from it. An assertion naming any other domain is rejected, however well signed.
  3. Key accounts on issuer + NameID, never on email alone, so an account can't be claimed by another IdP that asserts the same address.
  4. Ask the IdP to filter, and check anyway. Korenwolf assigns the Larder app only to its office managers' group, so other staff never get an assertion. Larder also requires that group in the assertion, so a mistake on one side isn't enough.

Then decide how accounts appear and disappear. With just-in-time provisioning, the SP creates the account at the first successful sign-in from the assertion's attributes; simple, but the SP never hears that someone left, only that they stopped signing in. With SCIM, the IdP pushes creations, changes and removals to the SP as they happen (Chapter 13). Either way, keep the SP's sessions short, because disabling someone at the IdP doesn't end a session they already have at the SP.

5.8Across providers

The clouds appear on both sides of federation: as IdPs for the apps you build, and as SPs that accept your company's IdP for console and CLI access.

Table 5.3 · SAML and federation at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Your workforce IdP into the cloudIAM Identity Center with an external IdP (SAML for sign-in, SCIM for users and groups); or per-account IAM SAML providers and AssumeRoleWithSAMLEntra ID is the directory; other IdPs federate into Entra IDCloud Identity / Workspace SSO with a third-party IdP; or workforce identity federation without Google accounts
Cloud as IdP for your SaaS appsIdentity Center applications (SAML)Entra ID enterprise apps (SAML or OIDC), with thousands of templatesWorkspace / Cloud Identity SAML apps
Partners' IdPs into your appCognito user pools: SAML and OIDC IdPs per customerEntra External ID: SAML / WS-Fed and OIDC federationIdentity Platform: SAML and OIDC providers, multi-tenant

On Azure

Inviting a partner's staff as B2B guests is Entra ID's own form of federation: the guest signs in with their home organisation's account, and lives in your tenant as a guest user you can add to groups and remove. Cross-tenant access settings decide which external tenants are allowed, and whether you trust their MFA.

On Google Cloud

Workforce identity federation lets people from an external IdP (SAML or OIDC) use the Google Cloud console and APIs without Google accounts at all; IAM policies grant roles to attributes from the assertion, such as a group. It's the closest equivalent to AWS's AssumeRoleWithSAML.

5.9At Larder: Korenwolf's office managers sign in

At Larder: solved

Larder's login service could already act as a SAML SP for each business customer, so the work was configuration and rules. Eli and Korenwolf IT exchanged metadata on 7 December; Korenwolf assigned the Larder app to a new group, Larder office managers, with nine people from the three offices; and Korenwolf's IdP releases four things: a persistent NameID, email, office and that group.

On Larder's side, the connection is bound to Korenwolf's business account and to korenwolf.example; accounts are keyed on issuer + NameID; the group is required; and the office attribute picks which delivery address an office manager orders for. Accounts are created just in time for now, with sessions of eight hours; SCIM is on the plan for when Korenwolf's portal launches in spring (Chapter 13). The first test sign-in failed because the audience in Korenwolf's configuration had a trailing slash; the second worked. It went live on 18 December, as planned.

The certificate in Korenwolf's metadata expires in December 2029. It's in Larder's on-call calendar for November 2029.

5.10Lab: be the service provider

Lab 5 · Read and check a SAML assertion

You'll parse a SAML response like Korenwolf's, run the service provider's checks from 5.5 and 5.7, and watch it reject a replay, a late response, the wrong audience and an IdP vouching for someone it shouldn't. You need Python 3. Cost: nothing. Important: the lab doesn't check the XML signature, which needs a dedicated library (such as python3-saml or pysaml2, built on xmlsec); 5.5 explains why you shouldn't write that yourself.

saml.pyPython 3.8+

# saml.py · read a SAML assertion and run a service provider's checks on it
# Teaching only: it does NOT verify the XML signature. Real code uses a library that does.
import xml.etree.ElementTree as ET
from datetime import datetime

NS = {"samlp": "urn:oasis:names:tc:SAML:2.0:protocol",
      "saml": "urn:oasis:names:tc:SAML:2.0:assertion"}

RESPONSE = """<samlp:Response xmlns:samlp="urn:oasis:names:tc:SAML:2.0:protocol"
    xmlns:saml="urn:oasis:names:tc:SAML:2.0:assertion" ID="_r1" Version="2.0"
    IssueInstant="2026-12-18T08:30:00Z" InResponseTo="_req-7f3a"
    Destination="https://login.larder.example/saml/acs/korenwolf">
  <saml:Issuer>https://sts.korenwolf.example/7c1d</saml:Issuer>
  <samlp:Status><samlp:StatusCode Value="urn:oasis:names:tc:SAML:2.0:status:Success"/></samlp:Status>
  <saml:Assertion ID="_a-91c2" Version="2.0" IssueInstant="2026-12-18T08:30:00Z">
    <saml:Issuer>https://sts.korenwolf.example/7c1d</saml:Issuer>
    <!-- <ds:Signature> over this Assertion goes here -->
    <saml:Subject>
      <saml:NameID Format="urn:oasis:names:tc:SAML:2.0:nameid-format:persistent">kw-48d1a0</saml:NameID>
      <saml:SubjectConfirmation Method="urn:oasis:names:tc:SAML:2.0:cm:bearer">
        <saml:SubjectConfirmationData InResponseTo="_req-7f3a" NotOnOrAfter="2026-12-18T08:35:00Z"
            Recipient="https://login.larder.example/saml/acs/korenwolf"/>
      </saml:SubjectConfirmation>
    </saml:Subject>
    <saml:Conditions NotBefore="2026-12-18T08:29:00Z" NotOnOrAfter="2026-12-18T08:35:00Z">
      <saml:AudienceRestriction><saml:Audience>https://login.larder.example/saml</saml:Audience></saml:AudienceRestriction>
    </saml:Conditions>
    <saml:AuthnStatement AuthnInstant="2026-12-18T08:29:58Z">
      <saml:AuthnContext><saml:AuthnContextClassRef>urn:oasis:names:tc:SAML:2.0:ac:classes:PasswordProtectedTransport</saml:AuthnContextClassRef></saml:AuthnContext>
    </saml:AuthnStatement>
    <saml:AttributeStatement>
      <saml:Attribute Name="email"><saml:AttributeValue>j.devries@korenwolf.example</saml:AttributeValue></saml:Attribute>
      <saml:Attribute Name="office"><saml:AttributeValue>Rotterdam</saml:AttributeValue></saml:Attribute>
      <saml:Attribute Name="groups"><saml:AttributeValue>Larder office managers</saml:AttributeValue></saml:Attribute>
    </saml:AttributeStatement>
  </saml:Assertion>
</samlp:Response>"""

# What Larder configured for this connection (from Korenwolf's metadata, and its own)
TRUST = {"issuer": "https://sts.korenwolf.example/7c1d",
         "audience": "https://login.larder.example/saml",
         "acs": "https://login.larder.example/saml/acs/korenwolf",
         "email_domain": "korenwolf.example",
         "required_group": "Larder office managers"}
PENDING = {"_req-7f3a"}            # AuthnRequest IDs we sent and haven't seen answered
SEEN = set()                       # assertion IDs already used (replay cache)

def t(text):
    return datetime.fromisoformat(text.replace("Z", "+00:00"))

def check(xml, now, skew=60):
    r = ET.fromstring(xml)
    a = r.find("saml:Assertion", NS)
    cond = a.find("saml:Conditions", NS)
    scd = a.find(".//saml:SubjectConfirmationData", NS)
    attrs = {x.get("Name"): x.findtext("saml:AttributeValue", namespaces=NS)
             for x in a.findall(".//saml:Attribute", NS)}
    problems = []
    if a.findtext("saml:Issuer", namespaces=NS) != TRUST["issuer"]:
        problems.append("issuer")
    if cond.findtext(".//saml:Audience", namespaces=NS) != TRUST["audience"]:
        problems.append("audience")
    if scd.get("Recipient") != TRUST["acs"] or r.get("Destination") != TRUST["acs"]:
        problems.append("recipient")
    if not (t(cond.get("NotBefore")).timestamp() - skew <= now <= t(cond.get("NotOnOrAfter")).timestamp() + skew):
        problems.append("time window")
    if scd.get("InResponseTo") not in PENDING:
        problems.append("not a response to our request")
    if a.get("ID") in SEEN:
        problems.append("replayed assertion")
    if not attrs.get("email", "").endswith("@" + TRUST["email_domain"]):
        problems.append("email outside korenwolf.example")
    if attrs.get("groups") != TRUST["required_group"]:
        problems.append("not in the Larder group")
    if problems:
        return "rejected: " + ", ".join(problems)
    SEEN.add(a.get("ID"))
    PENDING.discard(scd.get("InResponseTo"))
    name_id = a.findtext(".//saml:NameID", namespaces=NS)
    return f"ok: {TRUST['issuer']} | {name_id} | {attrs['email']} | office={attrs['office']}"

if __name__ == "__main__":
    NOW = t("2026-12-18T08:30:02Z").timestamp()
    print("as received     ", check(RESPONSE, NOW))
    print("same one again  ", check(RESPONSE, NOW + 10))
    PENDING.add("_req-7f3a"); SEEN.clear()
    print("ten minutes late", check(RESPONSE, NOW + 600))
    print("other audience  ", check(RESPONSE.replace("login.larder.example/saml<", "app.other.example/saml<"), NOW))
    print("a Larder email  ", check(RESPONSE.replace("j.devries@korenwolf.example", "eli@larder.example"), NOW))
  1. Run it with python3 saml.py: 
    as received      ok: https://sts.korenwolf.example/7c1d | kw-48d1a0 | j.devries@korenwolf.example | office=Rotterdam
same one again   rejected: not a response to our request, replayed assertion
ten minutes late rejected: time window
other audience   rejected: audience
a Larder email   rejected: email outside korenwolf.example
    Match each line to a check in 5.5 or 5.7. The last one is the important one: even if Korenwolf's IdP signed an assertion for eli@larder.example, Larder wouldn't accept it from this connection.
  2. Read the account key. The success line shows issuer and NameID first. Change the email in RESPONSE to a different @korenwolf.example address and run again: it's still the same account (kw-48d1a0), with a changed email, which is what should happen when someone changes their name.
  3. Break it on purpose. Comment out the domain check and run again: the assertion for a Larder employee is accepted. Then delete PENDING.add("_req-7f3a"); SEEN.clear() and run again: every later test now also fails with “not a response to our request”, because once a request has been answered, it can't be answered again.

Stretch goal. Look at a real IdP's metadata: in Microsoft Entra ID, every tenant publishes it at https://login.microsoftonline.com/<tenant-id>/federationmetadata/2007-06/federationmetadata.xml. Find the entity ID, the sign-in URL and the signing certificate's expiry (decode the certificate with openssl x509 -noout -enddate).

5.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Home-made SAML validationSignature wrapping and other subtle bypasses.A maintained library or managed identity service.
Trusting the certificate inside the messageAnyone can sign an assertion with their own key.Only the certificate from the IdP's metadata.
Letting a partner IdP assert any domain or tenantA partner, or whoever compromises them, can sign in as your staff or other customers.Bind each connection to a tenant and verified domains.
Email as the account keyTakeover when addresses change or are reused.Issuer + persistent NameID.
Accepting IdP-initiated responses freelyReplayed or injected sign-ins.SP-initiated; short windows and a replay cache if not.
Forgetting certificate expiryEvery federated sign-in fails on the same morning.Calendar it; agree a rollover with the IdP.
Long SP sessions with JIT accountsLeavers keep access until their session ends.Short sessions; SCIM deprovisioning.

✓Chapter summary

  • Federation: an SP accepts an IdP's word about who someone is. The person's own organisation keeps control of their account.
  • Trust is set up with metadata: entity IDs, URLs and the IdP's signing certificate.
  • SAML sign-in passes through the browser; SP-initiated flows can be matched to a request, IdP-initiated ones can't.
  • An assertion says who vouches, about whom, for which service, when, how they signed in, and some attributes.
  • Check signature (with the metadata's certificate), issuer, audience, recipient, time, InResponseTo and one-time use, with a library.
  • Limit what each IdP can vouch for: its own tenant, its own domains, required groups. Key accounts on issuer + NameID.
  • Prefer OpenID Connect for new integrations; support SAML where customers need it.

Key terms

Federation
Accepting another system's authentication of a person.
Identity provider (IdP)
Authenticates people and issues signed assertions about them.
Service provider (SP)
The application that relies on the IdP's assertions.
Metadata
An XML description of an IdP or SP: entity ID, endpoints and certificates.
Assertion
A signed SAML statement about a subject's authentication and attributes.
ACS URL
Assertion consumer service: the SP endpoint that receives SAML responses.
NameID
The identifier for the subject; persistent NameIDs never change for a person and SP.
Home realm discovery
Working out which IdP a person belongs to.
JIT provisioning
Creating an account at a person's first federated sign-in.

Check your understanding

  1. In the Larder–Korenwolf setup, which is the IdP, which is the SP, and where is the person's password checked? recall

    Show answer

    Korenwolf's Entra ID is the IdP and checks the password and MFA. Larder's login service is the SP; it never sees the password.

  2. Why does an SP keep the IDs of the AuthnRequests it sends, and of the assertions it has accepted? explain

    Show answer

    Request IDs let it check that a response answers a sign-in this SP started (InResponseTo). Assertion IDs let it reject a captured response that's replayed while still within its time window.

  3. An assertion is correctly signed by a partner's IdP and names finance@yourcompany.example. What should happen, and why? apply

    Show answer

    Reject it. The partner's connection may only vouch for its own verified domains and its own tenant; a valid signature proves who made the statement, not that the partner is entitled to make it.

  4. Korenwolf disables an office manager's account at 10:00. They had signed in to Larder at 09:00. When does their Larder access end, and how would you make it sooner? apply

    Show answer

    With just-in-time accounts, when Larder's session ends: up to eight hours after 09:00. Sooner: shorter sessions, and SCIM deprovisioning so Larder hears about the disabled account and ends its sessions at once.

  5. A start-up is adding enterprise SSO to its new web and mobile app. Should it start with SAML or OIDC? design

    Show answer

    Start with OpenID Connect, which suits mobile and APIs and is easier to get right; add SAML when an enterprise customer's IdP requires it, ideally through an identity service that handles both.

  6. Compare how a partner's staff could be given access to your app on Azure and on AWS. compare

    Show answer

    Azure: invite them as B2B guests in your Entra ID tenant, signing in with their home organisation, governed by cross-tenant access settings; or add their IdP to Entra External ID. AWS: add their IdP (SAML or OIDC) to a Cognito user pool, one IdP per partner, mapped to that partner's account in your app.

Going further

  • OASIS SAML 2.0 Technical Overview.
  • OWASP SAML Security Cheat Sheet.
  • AWS: Identity Center with an external IdP. Azure: SAML single sign-on protocol. Google Cloud: Workforce identity federation.

Part I · How access works

6

Access models: roles, attributes and relationships

Once you know who's asking, something has to decide what they may do. There are only a few ways to write that down: lists on each resource, roles, rules about attributes, and relationships between things. Each one fits some problems well and others badly. This chapter compares them on one company's real questions, and sets out the principles that keep any of them safe.

By the end you can

  • Describe access as a matrix, and explain ACLs and capabilities as its columns and rows.
  • Design roles, and recognise role explosion and privilege creep.
  • Write attribute-based rules, and say what they cost in auditability.
  • Model ownership and sharing as relationships.
  • Apply least privilege, separation of duties and object-level checks to any model.

Before you start

  • Principals, actions, resources and context; default deny (Chapter 1, 1.4).

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, Python 3, no cloud account
Main provider
provider-neutral, with AWS, Azure and Google Cloud in 6.8

At Larder

In December 2026, Joost's operations team starts planning the Utrecht store, and Eli is asked to set up its staff in the stock tool. The tool has 19 roles: a picker, stock clerk, store manager and operations role for each of the three stores, plus seven others that grew over two years. Roles are handed out from a spreadsheet. Adding Utrecht means four more, and the six-city plan means dozens.

While copying the spreadsheet, Eli notices that Tomás, who manages Noord, is also a store manager at Zuid. He covered there for two weeks in July while Zuid's manager was on holiday. Nobody removed the role, so for five months he could adjust any stock count at a store he didn't work in. Nothing bad happened. That's not the same as nothing being wrong. Section 6.9 shows what Larder changed.

6.1The access matrix

Every access model is a way of writing down one big table: principals down the side, resources across the top, and in each cell the actions that principal may perform on that resource. This is the access control matrix. Nobody stores it whole (it would be enormous and mostly empty), but every model is a compression of it.

6.2Access control lists

An access control list (ACL) stores the matrix by column: each resource carries a list of who may do what to it. File permissions are ACLs; so are sharing settings on a document (“Anna can edit, the team can comment”). ACLs are easy to understand for one resource, and answer “who can reach this file?” instantly.

They're hard to manage at scale. “What can Aylin reach?” means reading every ACL in the system, and when Aylin changes jobs, someone must find and edit each one. They suit cases where the owner of each resource genuinely decides who shares it, as in document sharing, and little else.

S3 ACLsS3 still supports per-object ACLs from its early days, but new buckets have them switched off (Object Ownership: “bucket owner enforced”). AWS's advice, and this book's, is to keep them off and use policies (Chapter 9).

6.3Roles

Role-based access control (RBAC) adds a layer: permissions are granted to roles named after jobs (“store manager”, “picker”), and people are assigned roles. When someone changes job, you change their roles, and every permission follows. RBAC is the most widely used model, and for good reason: roles match how organisations already think, and “who has the store manager role?” is an easy question to answer and review.

RBAC goes wrong in two familiar ways:

  • Role explosion. Roles start as jobs, then pick up a second dimension: a job at a store, in a team, for a customer. Every new store multiplies the roles. Larder's four jobs times three stores is 12 roles; times twelve stores it's 48, before any exceptions. Soon nobody knows what noord-lead-2 was for.
  • Privilege creep. People collect roles as they cover, move and help out, and roles are rarely taken away, because nobody notices extra access until it's misused. Tomás's Zuid role is a textbook case. Regular access reviews (Chapter 13) and assignments that expire are the cures.

Good role design keeps roles about what a job does, and moves where or for whom into something else: attributes or relationships.

6.4Attributes

Attribute-based access control (ABAC) writes rules over attributes of the principal, the resource, the action and the context:

allow if  action in permissions_of(principal.job)
     and  resource.store in principal.stores
     and  context.time is within the principal's shift

One rule replaces every “job at store” role. Add Utrecht, and nothing in the rules changes: Utrecht's staff get stores = {utrecht} from the HR system, and Utrecht's stock items carry store = utrecht. You've already seen ABAC in AWS: comparing a principal tag with a resource tag (Chapter 9, 9.6) is exactly this.

ABAC has its own costs:

  • Auditing gets harder. “Who can adjust Zuid's stock?” is no longer a list you can read; you have to evaluate the rule against every person's attributes.
  • Attributes become security data. Whoever can change someone's stores attribute, or a resource's tag, can change access. Attributes must come from a trustworthy source (the HR system, not a free-text field users can edit) and be protected like permissions.
  • Missing attributes need a safe default. A resource without a store attribute should be denied, not open to everyone.

6.5Relationships

Some access follows from how things relate. A Korenwolf office manager may see their office's orders; Korenwolf's Head of Workplace may see the orders of every office in her organisation; a document is visible to members of the folder it's in. Neither roles nor attributes say this naturally. Relationship-based access control (ReBAC) stores facts as relationships (“Rotterdam office has manager J. de Vries”, “order B-1042 belongs to the Rotterdam office”) and answers questions by following them.

ReBAC is how large sharing systems work: Google described its global authorisation system, Zanzibar, which stores trillions of such relationships for Drive, YouTube and Cloud; open-source systems such as OpenFGA and SpiceDB follow the same design. It suits multi-tenant products with organisations, teams, folders and sharing. It needs a place to store the relationships, kept in step with your application's data.

6.6Principles that apply to every model

  • Least privilege: each principal gets the access its job needs, and no more, for no longer than needed. Every model can express it; only discipline achieves it.
  • Default deny: no rule, no access (Chapter 1). Missing attributes and broken relationships deny.
  • Separation of duties: split powerful tasks so one person can't complete them alone. The person who adds a supplier shouldn't approve its payments; the engineer who writes a production change shouldn't be the only reviewer.
  • Deny overrides: a small number of explicit “never” rules (no one deletes backups; nothing leaves the EU) that win over any allow. AWS builds this into policy evaluation (Chapter 10).
  • Time limits: temporary needs get access that expires by itself. Tomás's July cover should have ended in July without anyone remembering.

Watch out: checking the action but not the object

The most common authorisation bug in web APIs isn't a wrong role; it's checking that a user may “view orders” and then returning whichever order ID they ask for. Change /orders/1042 to /orders/1043 and you see someone else's. OWASP ranks this, broken object-level authorisation, as the number one API security risk. Every request for a specific object must check that this principal may access this object, which is exactly the question ABAC and ReBAC ask.

6.7Choosing, and combining

Table 6.1 · Four ways to write down access
ACLsRBACABACReBAC
Grants are attached toeach resourceroles, assigned to peoplerules over attributesrelationships between objects
Good atowner-controlled sharingjobs; easy reviewsscaling across locations, teams, tagsownership, hierarchies, sharing
Struggles with“what can this person reach?”role explosion; creep“who can reach this?”; attribute hygienekeeping relationships in sync
Examplea shared documentan AWS permission setAWS tag conditionsGoogle Drive folders

Real systems combine them, and a good default is:

Key idea

Roles for what a job does; attributes for where and for whom; relationships for who owns what. Keep each one small.

Wherever the decision is made, make it in one place. Authorisation rules scattered through application code (if user.role == "admin" or user.id == order.owner_id …) can't be reviewed, tested or changed safely. Put them in a policy layer: a module, a library, or a policy engine such as Open Policy Agent (Rego), Cedar or OpenFGA, which takes a request and returns allow or deny (the policy decision point of Chapter 1).

6.8Across providers

Cloud permission systems mix the same models. Knowing which is which explains a lot of their design:

Table 6.2 · Access models in the clouds and for your own apps
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
RolesIAM roles and Identity Center permission sets (Chapter 12)Azure RBAC role definitions and assignmentspredefined and custom IAM roles
Attributestag conditions: aws:PrincipalTag, aws:ResourceTag (Chapter 9)role assignment conditions (Azure ABAC), on storage attributes and tagsIAM Conditions on resource tags, names and time
Relationshipsaccount and organisation structure (Chapter 20)management group → subscription → resource group scopesorganisation → folder → project inheritance
ACLsS3 ACLs (legacy; keep off)storage shared access signatures and container ACLs (data lake)Cloud Storage ACLs (prefer uniform bucket-level access)
For your own appsAmazon Verified Permissions (Cedar)Entra ID app roles and groups in tokensIAP and app-level policies; open-source engines

On Azure

Azure RBAC is role-based with a strong relationship flavour: a role assignment at a management group is inherited by every subscription, resource group and resource below it. Role assignment conditions add ABAC, for example letting a role read only blobs whose index tag project matches the principal's attribute.

On Google Cloud

Google Cloud's resource hierarchy makes inheritance central: a role granted on a folder applies to every project and resource in it. IAM Conditions narrow a binding by resource name, type, tags or time, which is how you grant temporary access that expires by itself.

6.9At Larder: four jobs, one rule

At Larder: solved

Eli and Joost rewrote the stock tool's access before Utrecht's staff were added. The 12 store roles became four jobs, and a person's job and home store now come from the HR system, which Joost's team already keeps up to date. One rule decides: the job's permissions, at the person's stores. Covering another store is a temporary store assignment with an end date, requested in the tool and approved by the store's manager; it disappears on the end date. The seven other roles shrank to three after nobody could explain the rest. Tomás's Zuid access went with the spreadsheet.

For Larder for Business, the portal that launches in spring 2027 is designed around relationships from the start: an office manager sees their office's orders, and a customer's administrator, such as Korenwolf's Hanna, sees every office in their organisation. The design says every order request checks the order's office against the person, not just their role. Whether every endpoint actually does is a question that comes back in October 2027 (Chapter 19).

6.10Lab: one question, three models

Lab 6 · RBAC, ABAC and ReBAC side by side

You'll answer Larder's access questions with roles, then with an attribute rule, then answer the business-order question with relationships, count roles as stores grow, and see privilege creep in action. You need Python 3. Cost: nothing.

models.pyPython 3.8+

# models.py · the same access questions answered three ways: RBAC, ABAC, ReBAC
JOBS = {                                   # what each job may do, anywhere it works
    "picker":        {"stock:view"},
    "stock-clerk":   {"stock:view", "stock:count"},
    "store-manager": {"stock:view", "stock:count", "stock:adjust", "rota:edit"},
    "operations":    {"stock:view", "rota:edit"},
}

# ---------- RBAC: a role per job per store, assigned by hand
def rbac_roles(stores):
    return {f"{job}@{store}": perms for job, perms in JOBS.items() for store in stores}

ROLES = rbac_roles(["noord", "zuid", "rotterdam"])
ASSIGNED = {"tomas": {"store-manager@noord", "store-manager@zuid"},   # covered Zuid in July...
            "aylin": {"picker@zuid"},
            "joost": {f"operations@{s}" for s in ["noord", "zuid", "rotterdam"]}}

def rbac(user, action, store):
    return any(action in ROLES[r] and r.endswith("@" + store) for r in ASSIGNED.get(user, ()))

# ---------- ABAC: one rule over attributes of the person and the resource
PEOPLE = {"tomas": {"job": "store-manager", "stores": {"noord"}},   # from the HR system
          "aylin": {"job": "picker", "stores": {"zuid"}},
          "joost": {"job": "operations", "stores": {"*"}}}

def abac(user, action, store):
    p = PEOPLE.get(user)
    return bool(p) and action in JOBS[p["job"]] and (store in p["stores"] or "*" in p["stores"])

# ---------- ReBAC: who may see a business order follows relationships
TUPLES = {("office:kw-rotterdam", "manager", "j.devries"),
          ("office:kw-amsterdam", "manager", "m.bakker"),
          ("org:korenwolf", "admin", "hanna"),
          ("office:kw-rotterdam", "parent", "org:korenwolf"),
          ("office:kw-amsterdam", "parent", "org:korenwolf"),
          ("order:B-1042", "office", "office:kw-rotterdam")}

def related(obj, rel):
    return {s for (o, r, s) in TUPLES if o == obj and r == rel}

def can_view_order(user, order):
    for office in related(order, "office"):
        if user in related(office, "manager"):
            return True
        if any(user in related(org, "admin") for org in related(office, "parent")):
            return True
    return False

if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(f"RBAC roles for 3 stores: {len(ROLES)}; for 12 stores: {len(rbac_roles(range(12)))}\n")
    questions = [("tomas", "stock:adjust", "noord"), ("tomas", "stock:adjust", "zuid"),
                 ("aylin", "stock:adjust", "zuid"), ("aylin", "stock:view", "zuid"),
                 ("joost", "rota:edit", "rotterdam"), ("joost", "stock:adjust", "rotterdam")]
    print(f"{'question':<34} RBAC   ABAC")
    for q in questions:
        print(f"{' '.join(q):<34} {str(rbac(*q)):<6} {abac(*q)}")
    print()
    for user in ["j.devries", "m.bakker", "hanna"]:
        print(f"{user:<10} may view order B-1042: {can_view_order(user, 'order:B-1042')}")
  1. Run it with python3 models.py: 
    RBAC roles for 3 stores: 12; for 12 stores: 48

question                           RBAC   ABAC
tomas stock:adjust noord           True   True
tomas stock:adjust zuid            True   False
aylin stock:adjust zuid            False  False
aylin stock:view zuid              True   True
joost rota:edit rotterdam          True   True
joost stock:adjust rotterdam       False  False

j.devries  may view order B-1042: True
m.bakker   may view order B-1042: False
hanna      may view order B-1042: True
    The two models agree everywhere except one row: RBAC still remembers Tomás's July cover. ABAC reads his store from HR, so it doesn't. The ReBAC answers follow the paths in Figure 6.2.
  2. Add a store. Add "utrecht" to the list in ROLES and count the roles. Then add a Utrecht picker in both models. In RBAC you add a role and an assignment; in ABAC, one person with "stores": {"utrecht"}, and no rule changes.
  3. Break it on purpose. In abac, change the store check to store in p.get("stores", {store}) and remove Aylin's "stores" key: a person with a missing attribute now gets access everywhere. That's why missing attributes must deny. Then, in TUPLES, add ("office:kw-amsterdam", "manager", "j.devries"), as if someone mistyped an office: she now sees Amsterdam's orders too. In ReBAC, the relationships are the permissions.

Stretch goals. (1) Give ABAC a temporary cover: "cover": {"store": "zuid", "until": "2026-07-19"}, and a today argument, so Tomás's July access ends by itself. (2) Add a relationship type viewer on an order, so an office manager can share one order with a colleague, and extend can_view_order.

6.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Roles that encode location, team or customerRole explosion; nobody knows what each role is for.Roles for jobs; attributes or relationships for scope.
Access without an end date for temporary needsPrivilege creep, as with Tomás.Time-limited assignments; regular reviews.
Checking the action but not the objectUsers read others' records by changing an ID.Check this principal against this object on every request.
Attributes users can edit themselvesAnyone can grant themselves access.Attributes from a trusted source, changed only by authorised people.
Missing attributes treated as “match anything”Untagged resources or people get broad access.Missing means deny.
Authorisation scattered through codeCan't review, test or change it safely.One policy layer or engine.

✓Chapter summary

  • Every model compresses the access matrix: ACLs store it by resource, capabilities by principal, roles and rules by pattern.
  • RBAC matches jobs and is easy to review, but suffers role explosion and privilege creep.
  • ABAC scales across stores, teams and tags with one rule, but makes “who can reach this?” harder and turns attributes into security data.
  • ReBAC follows relationships: ownership, hierarchies, sharing.
  • Combine them: roles for what, attributes for where, relationships for whose; decide in one policy layer.
  • Least privilege, default deny, separation of duties, deny overrides and time limits apply to every model. Always check the object, not just the action.

Key terms

Access control matrix
Principals × resources, with permitted actions in each cell.
ACL
Access control list: the permissions stored on one resource.
RBAC
Role-based access control: permissions granted to roles, roles assigned to principals.
ABAC
Attribute-based access control: rules over attributes of principal, resource, action and context.
ReBAC
Relationship-based access control: decisions follow relationships between objects.
Role explosion
Roles multiplying because they encode extra dimensions such as location.
Privilege creep
Access accumulating over time as people move and cover.
Least privilege
Only the access needed, for only as long as needed.
Separation of duties
Splitting sensitive tasks so no one person can complete them alone.
Policy engine
A component that evaluates authorisation rules and returns allow or deny.

Check your understanding

  1. Which model fits each: a shared slide deck; “store managers can adjust stock at their store”; “Korenwolf's admin sees all its offices' orders”? recall

    Show answer

    An ACL (owner-controlled sharing); ABAC (job plus a store attribute), or RBAC with attributes; ReBAC (organisation → office → order).

  2. Why did ABAC fix Tomás's leftover access, when RBAC didn't? explain

    Show answer

    ABAC reads his store from the HR system, which was updated when the cover ended; RBAC depended on someone remembering to remove a role assignment, which nobody did.

  3. An API checks that the caller has the orders:read scope, then returns /orders/{id} for any ID. What's wrong, and what's the fix? apply

    Show answer

    Broken object-level authorisation: any caller with the scope can read any order. Check that the caller is related to that order (owner, or manager of its office) before returning it.

  4. Give one example of separation of duties at Larder, and how you'd enforce it. design

    Show answer

    Refunds over €100: support staff can propose, a team lead must approve, and nobody can approve their own. Enforce it in the refund tool's policy: the approver must hold the lead role and differ from the requester.

  5. What new risks does ABAC introduce, and how do you control them? explain

    Show answer

    Whoever can change attributes or tags can change access, missing attributes can be mistaken for “match all”, and it's harder to list who can reach a resource. Source attributes from a trusted system, restrict who can change them, deny when they're missing, and evaluate rules regularly to produce access reports.

  6. Compare how inheritance works in Azure RBAC and Google Cloud IAM. compare

    Show answer

    Both inherit downwards: Azure from management groups to subscriptions, resource groups and resources; Google Cloud from organisation to folders, projects and resources. In both, a grant high up applies to everything below it, so grant at the lowest scope that works.

Going further

  • NIST SP 800-162, Guide to ABAC.
  • Zanzibar: Google's consistent, global authorization system.
  • OWASP API1: Broken Object Level Authorization.
  • Policy engines: Cedar, Open Policy Agent, OpenFGA.
  • AWS: ABAC with tags. Azure: Azure ABAC. Google Cloud: IAM Conditions.

Part II · IAM on AWS

7

The AWS account and its root user

Everything in AWS lives inside an account, and every account starts with one identity that can do anything in it, including closing it: the root user. This chapter explains what an account is as a security boundary, what the root user can do that nobody else can, and how to lock it away so well that you never use it, while still being able to get it back on the day you need it.

By the end you can

  • Explain what an AWS account isolates, and why it's the strongest boundary AWS offers.
  • List the tasks only the root user can do.
  • Secure a root user: no keys, strong MFA, a company-owned email and phone, monitored sign-ins.
  • Recover root access when the email, password or MFA device is lost.
  • Read a credential report and find the risky credentials in an account.

Before you start

  • Principals and credentials (Chapter 1); MFA and security keys (Chapter 2).
  • ARNs (Chapter 9, 9.4) help; this chapter recaps what it needs.

This chapter

Reading
about 35 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, AWS CLI and Python 3, free
Main provider
AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 7.7

At Larder

On Monday 23 November 2026, a week after the backup incident (Chapter 1), Eli starts his audit with the account itself: the one Jasper opened in March 2024, which still runs everything in production. The credential report has bad news on its first line. The root user has an active access key, created minutes after the account itself, last used in March 2025.

Then it gets worse. The root user's email address is jasper@larder.example, a mailbox that was switched off when Jasper left in August 2025. Its MFA device was an app on Jasper's phone, now wiped. Nobody knows the password. Larder's most powerful identity has a working key that nobody controls, and no working way in for anyone who should. Section 7.8 shows how Eli got it back.

7.1What an account is

An AWS account is a container for resources, identities and a bill. Each has a 12-digit account ID (111122223333 in this book's examples) and is isolated from every other account by default:

  • Resources: a bucket, database or VPC belongs to exactly one account.
  • Identities: IAM users and roles exist inside one account, and by default can only touch that account's resources.
  • Permissions: nothing crosses an account boundary unless both sides allow it (Chapter 10, Chapter 21).
  • Quotas and billing: limits (such as the number of VPCs) and costs are per account.

That makes the account AWS's strongest security boundary, which is why companies use many of them: one per environment, per team, per workload (Chapter 20). A mistake or a breach in the development account then can't reach production. Larder has two: Jasper's original account, which runs production (and still holds the network hub from Handbook 1), and a second account for development and staging that Eli opened in 2025. They aren't part of an organisation yet.

Is an account ID secret?No: it appears in every ARN, and AWS doesn't treat it as a secret. But it tells an attacker which account to target, so don't publish it needlessly. Never rely on its secrecy.

7.2The root user

When you create an account, you give an email address and a password. That pair is the root user: an identity with complete, unrestricted access to everything in the account. Three properties make it special:

  • IAM policies can't limit it. There's no policy you can attach to root. The only thing that restricts a root user is an organisation-level guardrail (an SCP) when the account is a member of an AWS Organization (Chapter 20).
  • It can do a few things no one else can (Table 7.1).
  • It's tied to an email address, and whoever controls that mailbox can reset the password.
Table 7.1 · Tasks that need the root user (at the time of writing)
TaskWhen you'd need it
Change the root email address or passwordhanding over or recovering the account
Create or delete root access keysonly ever to delete them
Close the accountend of life
Restore IAM permissionswhen the only IAM administrator has locked themselves out
Remove an S3 bucket policy or SQS queue policy that denies everyoneafter a policy mistake locks out all IAM principals
Activate IAM access to the Billing consoleonce, when setting up an account
Configure MFA delete on an S3 bucketprotecting critical buckets from deletion
Register as a seller in the Reserved Instance Marketplace; sign up for GovCloudrarely

That's the whole list. Everything else, from creating users to deleting databases, can and should be done by IAM principals with the right permissions. The root user is for emergencies and a handful of account-level chores.

7.3Locking it away

Because it can't be limited, the root user must be impossible to use casually and hard to steal. The checklist:

  1. No access keys. Root keys give unlimited programmatic access and can't be restricted. Delete any that exist, after checking when they were last used and by what.
  2. Phishing-resistant MFA, more than one device. Register two or more FIDO2 security keys or passkeys (an account's root user can have up to eight MFA devices), kept in different places. AWS now requires MFA for root users in most accounts.
  3. A long, random password in the company's password vault, ideally readable only by two people together.
  4. A company-owned email address that's a group, such as aws-root@larder.example, delivered to several trusted people. Never a personal address, and never one person's work mailbox, which disappears when they leave.
  5. A company-owned phone number as the account's primary contact, because AWS uses it to verify you during recovery (7.4).
  6. Alternate contacts for billing, operations and security, so AWS's security notices reach a team, not a former employee.
  7. Alerts on root sign-in. Any use of root should page someone (Chapter 23). If root is used, it should be because you planned to.

Key idea

Treat the root user like the deeds to a building: kept safe, owned by the company, needed rarely, and missed immediately if anyone touches them.

7.4Getting root back

Root access is lost more often than it's stolen: the person who set it up leaves, a phone is replaced, a mailbox is closed. AWS's recovery paths all depend on the account's contact details, which is why 7.3 insists they belong to the company:

  • Lost password: “Forgot password” on the root sign-in page sends a reset link to the root email address.
  • Lost MFA device: the sign-in page offers to sign in with alternative factors: AWS verifies the root email address, then calls the account's primary contact phone number.
  • Lost email or phone too: AWS Support can help, but expect identity checks and time.

Watch out: root on someone's personal email

If the root email is a founder's personal address, or a freelancer's, that person owns your account in every way that matters. They can reset the password, and you can't. Moving root to a company group address is one of the first jobs in any AWS account review.

7.5Seeing every credential: the credential report

The credential report is a CSV file listing every IAM user in the account, plus the root user, with the state of each credential: whether a password is set and when it was last used, whether MFA is on, and for each access key when it was created and last used, in which region and for which service. It's generated on request (at most once every four hours) and is the fastest way to see what's lying around in an account. The lab reads one.

Two other quick views help: aws iam get-account-summary reports whether the root user has MFA (AccountMFAEnabled) and access keys (AccountAccessKeysPresent); and the IAM console's dashboard shows the same as security recommendations.

7.6Root in an organisation

When accounts belong to an AWS Organization (Chapter 20), root works differently:

  • The management account, which pays for and governs the organisation, has a root user that nothing can restrict. Secure it most carefully of all, and run no workloads in that account.
  • The root users of member accounts are limited by service control policies, like every other principal there.
  • With centralised root access, the organisation can remove root credentials (password, keys, MFA) from member accounts entirely. When a root-only task is needed, such as unlocking a bucket policy that denies everyone, an administrator in the management account performs it through a short-lived, task-scoped root session (sts:AssumeRoot). New member accounts then never have root credentials at all.

7.7Across providers

Azure and Google Cloud have no single “root user” per subscription or project, but they have the same problem one level up: a few all-powerful administrator identities that must be locked away and never lost.

Table 7.2 · The all-powerful identities at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Boundaryaccounttenant (directory); subscriptions inside itorganisation; projects inside it
Most powerful identityroot user of each account (management account's is unrestricted)Global Administrator in Entra ID, who can elevate to manage every subscriptionsuper administrator in Cloud Identity / Workspace
Recommended practiceno keys, FIDO2 MFA, group email, centralised root access for memberstwo cloud-only emergency access accounts with FIDO2 keys, monitored; few Global Administratorsdedicated super admin accounts with security keys, not used day to day; a backup super admin

On Azure

A Global Administrator can turn on Elevate access to become User Access Administrator over every subscription in the tenant. Keep Global Administrators to a handful, give them separate admin accounts from their daily ones, and keep two emergency access (“break-glass”) accounts that don't depend on federation or a single MFA method, with alerts on every sign-in.

On Google Cloud

Super administrators of the Cloud Identity or Workspace account can grant themselves any role in the organisation. Google recommends using them only for administration, protecting them with security keys, keeping at least one backup, and granting the day-to-day Organization Administrator role to ordinary accounts instead.

7.8At Larder: getting root back

At Larder: solved

Larder still owns the domain, so the fix started with email: on 24 November, Larder's IT administrator restored jasper@larder.example as an alias delivering to a new group, aws-root@larder.example (Eli, Femke and Marco). A password reset link arrived within a minute. For MFA, Eli used sign in with alternative factors: AWS verified the email address, then called the account's primary contact number, which turned out to be Femke's mobile, from the week the founders opened the account. Femke read out the code.

Once signed in, Eli and Femke worked through 7.3 together. The root access key had last been used on 4 March 2025, by S3, which matched a nightly export script on an old virtual machine retired that month; the key was deleted. Two FIDO2 security keys were registered, one for the office safe and one kept by Femke. The root email became aws-root@larder.example, the primary phone a company number, and the alternate contacts the platform team's address. The new password went into the company vault. Root hasn't been used since. When Larder creates an organisation in January (Chapter 12), this account joins it as a member, and its root credentials can then be removed altogether (Chapter 20).

7.9Lab: audit an account's credentials

Lab 7 · Root status and the credential report

You'll check your account's root user from the CLI, download its credential report, and run a script that lists what needs fixing, first on a sample shaped like Larder's account, then on yours. You need the AWS CLI v2 with read access to IAM (the IAMReadOnlyAccess policy is enough) and Python 3. Cost: free; nothing is created.

  1. Check root from the CLI. 
    aws iam get-account-summary \
  --query 'SummaryMap.{RootMFA: AccountMFAEnabled, RootKeys: AccountAccessKeysPresent}'
    You want RootMFA to be 1 and RootKeys to be 0. If either isn't, fix it first, signed in as root, following 7.3.
  2. Check the contacts. aws account get-alternate-contact --alternate-contact-type SECURITY shows who receives AWS's security notices. An error saying none is set means nobody does.
  3. Save this script as credcheck.py:

    credcheck.pyPython 3.8+

    # credcheck.py · read an IAM credential report and list what needs fixing
import csv, sys
from datetime import datetime, timezone

TODAY = datetime(2026, 11, 23, 12, 0, tzinfo=timezone.utc)  # use datetime.now(timezone.utc) for real
STALE = 90                                                 # days

def age(value):
    """Days since a timestamp in the report, or None for N/A, not_supported, no_information."""
    try:
        return (TODAY - datetime.fromisoformat(value)).days
    except ValueError:
        return None

def findings(row):
    user, out = row["user"], []
    if user == "<root_account>":
        if row["access_key_1_active"] == "true" or row["access_key_2_active"] == "true":
            out.append("ROOT has an active access key: delete it")
        if row["mfa_active"] != "true":
            out.append("ROOT has no MFA")
        used = age(row["password_last_used"])
        if used is not None and used < STALE:
            out.append(f"root signed in {used} days ago: find out why")
        return out
    if row["password_enabled"] == "true" and row["mfa_active"] != "true":
        out.append("console password without MFA")
    if row["password_enabled"] == "true" and (age(row["password_last_used"]) or 0) > STALE:
        out.append(f"password unused for {age(row['password_last_used'])} days")
    for n in ("1", "2"):
        if row[f"access_key_{n}_active"] != "true":
            continue
        rotated, used = age(row[f"access_key_{n}_last_rotated"]), age(row[f"access_key_{n}_last_used_date"])
        out.append(f"access key {n}: {rotated} days old, "
                   + (f"last used {used} days ago" if used is not None else "never used"))
    return out

path = sys.argv[1] if len(sys.argv) > 1 else "sample-report.csv"
for row in csv.DictReader(open(path, newline="")):
    for f in findings(row):
        print(f"{row['user']:<15} {f}")
  4. Run it on Larder's sample. Save the six-line report below as sample-report.csv (real reports have the same 22 columns), then run python3 credcheck.py. 
    user,arn,user_creation_time,password_enabled,password_last_used,password_last_changed,password_next_rotation,mfa_active,access_key_1_active,access_key_1_last_rotated,access_key_1_last_used_date,access_key_1_last_used_region,access_key_1_last_used_service,access_key_2_active,access_key_2_last_rotated,access_key_2_last_used_date,access_key_2_last_used_region,access_key_2_last_used_service,cert_1_active,cert_1_last_rotated,cert_2_active,cert_2_last_rotated

    Output:

    Every line is a decision. The root key goes (7.3). ops and jasper are Chapter 8's problem. deploy-bot's two-and-a-half-year-old key is in daily use, so it can't just be deleted; Chapter 17 replaces it.
  5. Run it on your own account. Change TODAY to datetime.now(timezone.utc), then: 
    aws iam generate-credential-report             # repeat until "State": "COMPLETE"
aws iam get-credential-report --query Content --output text | base64 --decode > report.csv
python3 credcheck.py report.csv
    On macOS, use base64 -D. The report holds no secrets, only metadata, but it's a map of your account's weak spots: delete it when you're done.
  6. Find root's recent activity. Root actions in the last 90 days appear in CloudTrail under the user name root: 
    aws cloudtrail lookup-events --max-results 20 \
  --lookup-attributes AttributeKey=Username,AttributeValue=root \
  --query 'Events[].[EventTime, EventName]' --output table
    In a well-run account this is empty, or shows only what you did on purpose.

Stretch goal. Extend findings to flag users whose access key was never used but is older than seven days. Those are usually keys someone created “just in case”.

7.10Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Root access keysUnlimited, unrestrictable programmatic access for whoever finds them.None, ever.
Root email on one person's mailboxAccess leaves with them, or stays with them.A company group address.
One MFA device, on one phoneA lost phone locks you out; recovery depends on old contact details.Two or more FIDO2 keys in different places.
Using root for daily workEvery action has unlimited blast radius and no attribution.IAM roles through single sign-on; root only for Table 7.1.
No alternate contactsAWS's security warnings reach nobody.Security, operations and billing contacts set to team addresses.
Workloads in the management accountA compromise there controls the whole organisation.Keep it for governance only (Chapter 20).

✓Chapter summary

  • An account isolates resources, identities, permissions, quotas and billing. It's AWS's strongest boundary.
  • The root user can do everything and can't be limited by IAM policies; only SCPs restrict it, in member accounts.
  • Only a short list of tasks needs root. Do everything else with IAM principals.
  • Lock root away: no keys, two or more FIDO2 keys, a vaulted password, a company group email and phone, alternate contacts, alerts on use.
  • Recovery depends on the root email and the account's phone number. Make sure the company owns both.
  • The credential report lists every credential in the account; read it regularly.
  • In an organisation, member accounts can have no root credentials at all.

Key terms

AWS account
A container for resources, identities and billing, isolated from other accounts.
Account ID
The 12-digit number identifying an account, part of every ARN.
Root user
The account's original email-and-password identity, with unrestricted access.
Credential report
A CSV of every IAM user and root, with password, MFA and access key status.
Alternate contacts
Billing, operations and security contacts AWS uses for notices.
Management account
The account that creates and pays for an AWS Organization.
Centralised root access
Removing member accounts' root credentials and doing root tasks through scoped sessions.

Check your understanding

  1. Why can't you limit the root user of a standalone account with an IAM policy? recall

    Show answer

    IAM policies attach to users, groups and roles; the root user isn't one of them. Only an organisation's SCPs apply to a member account's root.

  2. An administrator applied a bucket policy that denies all actions to every principal. Every IAM user now gets “access denied”, including admins. How do you fix it? apply

    Show answer

    Sign in as the root user (or, in an organisation with centralised root access, use a task-scoped root session from the management account) and delete the bucket policy. That's one of the root-only tasks.

  3. The credential report shows a root access key last used yesterday. What do you do, in order? apply

    Show answer

    Treat it as a possible incident: find out from CloudTrail what it was used for and from where. If it's an unknown use, rotate root's password and MFA, and investigate. Then move whatever legitimately used it to a role, and delete the key.

  4. Explain why the root email address should be a group, not a person. explain

    Show answer

    Whoever receives that mail can reset the password and helps pass MFA recovery. A personal mailbox disappears or leaves with its owner; a group survives staff changes and can't be used by one person alone without others seeing.

  5. Design root security for a new company with one AWS account today and an organisation planned next year. design

    Show answer

    Today: group root email and company phone, two FIDO2 keys in separate places, vaulted password, no keys, alternate contacts, an alert on root sign-in. Next year: make this account the management account with no workloads, create members from it, and turn on centralised root access so members have no root credentials.

  6. Compare the AWS root user with Azure's Global Administrator. compare

    Show answer

    Root is one per account and can't be restricted there; Global Administrator is a role in an Entra ID tenant that several accounts can hold, and can elevate to manage every subscription. Both should be few, use phishing-resistant MFA, have emergency fallbacks and be monitored.

Going further

  • AWS: AWS account root user, tasks that require root, credential reports and centralised root access.
  • Azure: Manage emergency access accounts.
  • Google Cloud: Super administrator best practices.

Part II · IAM on AWS

8

Users, groups and roles

AWS IAM gives you three kinds of identity. Users are the oldest and easiest to understand, and the one to avoid. Roles are the one to build everything on: an identity with no password and no permanent key, which people, services and other accounts borrow for an hour at a time.
