This chapter explains how a role is put together, what happens when you assume one, and how to write the trust policy that decides who can.

By the end you can

  • Explain why IAM users are a legacy pattern, and when one is still acceptable.
  • Describe a role as a trust policy plus permission policies.
  • Assume a role and read the temporary credentials and session it produces.
  • Write trust policies for people, AWS services and other accounts, with conditions.
  • Control who can hand a role to a service, with iam:PassRole.

Before you start

  • Short-lived credentials (Chapter 3); the account and root user (Chapter 7).
  • Reading policies (Chapter 9). This chapter writes the other half: trust policies.

This chapter

Reading
about 50 minutes
Lab
25 minutes, AWS CLI, free
Main provider
AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 8.10

At Larder

The second page of Eli's credential report (Chapter 7) lists Larder's IAM users: eleven with console passwords. Eight are current engineers, each with read-only access and a personal access key or two. Three belong to people who've left: Jasper, and two contractors from 2024. Jasper's user still has an active access key. Then there's ops, the shared user with AdministratorAccess from Chapter 1, which engineers use whenever production needs fixing because their own users can only look.

Single sign-on (Chapter 12) will replace all of this, but that's weeks away. Eli wants something safe for production work this month, without a shared password. Section 8.11 shows what he built.

8.1IAM users

An IAM user is a long-lived identity inside one account. It can have a console password and up to two access keys for the CLI and SDKs. An access key is a pair: an access key ID starting with AKIA, and a secret access key, shown once when it's created. Two keys are allowed so you can rotate: create the new one, switch everything to it, then delete the old.

Users get permissions from policies attached to them directly or, better, to groups they belong to.

8.2Groups

An IAM group is a set of users that share policies: attach a policy to developers, and every user in it gets those permissions. Groups are simple, with three limits worth knowing: a group isn't a principal (you can't name it in a trust or resource policy, or sign in as it); groups can't contain other groups; and they only hold IAM users, not roles or federated people. Groups in your identity provider are a different thing, and much more useful (Chapter 12).

8.3Why users are a legacy pattern

IAM users were AWS's original answer to “how do people get in?”, and they still work. But they carry every problem from Part I:

  • Long-lived credentials. Passwords and access keys last until someone deletes them. Larder's credential report shows the result: a key two and a half years old, and three more belonging to people who've left (Chapter 3).
  • One account at a time. A user exists in one account. With ten accounts, a person needs ten users, ten passwords and ten MFA devices, or you build cross-account roles anyway.
  • A separate directory. Joiners and leavers in your company's directory don't reach IAM. Someone must remember to create and delete users by hand (Chapter 13).

The modern answer is: people sign in through IAM Identity Center or another identity provider and get temporary role sessions (Chapter 12); workloads on AWS use roles provided by the platform (Chapter 15); workloads outside AWS exchange their own platform's tokens for role sessions (Chapter 17). An IAM user is acceptable only where nothing else works: a third-party tool that can only take an access key, or an emergency break-glass account (Chapter 14), and then with a narrow policy, monitoring and rotation.

8.4Roles

An IAM role is an identity with permissions but no credentials of its own: no password, no access keys. Instead, trusted principals assume it and receive temporary credentials for a session. A role is defined by two kinds of policy, and keeping them apart is most of the battle:

  • The trust policy is a resource-based policy on the role itself. Its Principal names who may call sts:AssumeRole (or its web identity and SAML variants) on this role, and its conditions say under what circumstances.
  • The permission policies are ordinary identity-based policies (Chapter 9): what a session of this role may do.

Roles serve every kind of principal from Chapter 1: people (through single sign-on or, as at Larder this month, from IAM users), AWS services acting for you (an ECS task, a Lambda function), pipelines outside AWS, and principals in other accounts.

8.5Assuming a role

Assuming a role is an API call to AWS STS, the Security Token Service:

aws sts assume-role \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/prod-operator \
  --role-session-name eli \
  --source-identity eli

STS checks the trust policy and the caller's own permissions (Chapter 10), and returns temporary credentials:

{
  "Credentials": {
    "AccessKeyId": "ASIAQEXAMPLE7RTHUNA",
    "SecretAccessKey": "…",
    "SessionToken": "IQoJb3JpZ2luX2VjE…",
    "Expiration": "2026-12-07T10:42:13+00:00"
  },
  "AssumedRoleUser": {
    "Arn": "arn:aws:sts::111122223333:assumed-role/prod-operator/eli"
  },
  "SourceIdentity": "eli"
}

Things to notice:

  • Temporary keys start with ASIA, long-lived ones with AKIA. Seeing AKIA in a log or a leak tells you a long-lived key is involved.
  • All three parts are needed: key ID, secret and session token. Tools pass them as AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID, AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY and AWS_SESSION_TOKEN.
  • The session expires: by default after one hour; you can ask for 15 minutes up to the role's maximum session duration, which you set between 1 and 12 hours.
  • The session has its own ARN: assumed-role/role-name/session-name. That's what CloudTrail records, so choose session names that identify the person or job.
  • Source identity is a value set when the role is first assumed, which can't be changed afterwards and is recorded in CloudTrail for every action in the session, even across further role assumptions. It's how you keep Chapter 1's accountability when people share a role.
Role chainingUsing a role session to assume another role is called role chaining. AWS limits chained sessions to one hour, whatever the maximum session duration says.

8.6Writing trust policies

A trust policy has the same grammar as any policy (Chapter 9), with a Principal and an action from STS. The principal says what kind of thing may assume the role:

Table 8.1 · Who can be trusted to assume a role
PrincipalExampleMeaning
A specific user or role{"AWS": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:user/eli"}exactly that identity
An account{"AWS": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:root"}any principal in that account whose own policies allow it; the account's administrators decide who
An AWS service{"Service": "ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com"}that service, acting for you (8.7)
A federated provider{"Federated": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:oidc-provider/token.actions.githubusercontent.com"}people or workloads authenticated by that SAML or OIDC provider (Chapter 5, Chapter 17)

Despite the name, arn:aws:iam::111122223333:root in a principal doesn't mean the root user. It means “the account”, handing the decision to that account's IAM policies. Conditions then narrow the trust:

prod-operator-trust.jsontrust policy

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "EngineersWithMfaAndTheirOwnName",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "AWS": [
      "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:user/ben",
      "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:user/eli"
    ] },
    "Action": ["sts:AssumeRole", "sts:SetSourceIdentity"],
    "Condition": {
      "Bool": { "aws:MultiFactorAuthPresent": "true" },
      "StringEquals": { "sts:SourceIdentity": "${aws:username}" }
    }
  }]
}

Read aloud: “Allow Ben's and Eli's users to assume this role, only if they signed in with MFA, and only if they set the source identity to their own user name.” The last condition stops anyone from recording a colleague's name. Other conditions you'll meet: sts:ExternalId for third parties (Chapter 21), aws:SourceAccount and aws:SourceArn for services (8.7), and the sub and aud claims of OIDC tokens (Chapter 17).

Watch out: trust that's too wide

"Principal": {"AWS": "*"} without strong conditions lets any AWS account in the world try to assume your role. Trusting another company's whole account trusts every administrator they have. And trusting a federated provider without conditions on which identities (such as which GitHub repository) lets anyone that provider authenticates in. A role's permissions are only as safe as its trust policy.

8.7Roles for AWS services, and iam:PassRole

Many services act for you: an ECS task reads from S3, a Lambda function writes to DynamoDB, CloudFormation creates resources. Each does it by assuming a role you give it:

  • A service role is an ordinary role whose trust policy names the service (ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com, lambda.amazonaws.com). You write its permissions.
  • An instance profile is the wrapper that lets an EC2 instance use a role (Chapter 15).
  • A service-linked role is created and managed by a service for its own needs, with a fixed policy. You can see it but not edit it.

Handing a role to a service is itself a permission: iam:PassRole. Whoever can launch a task and pass any role can run code with that role's permissions, including an administrator role. That's one of the classic ways to escalate privileges in AWS. Limit it to the roles a person or pipeline actually needs to pass, and to the service that should receive them:

{
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Action": "iam:PassRole",
  "Resource": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/dispatch-task",
  "Condition": { "StringEquals": { "iam:PassedToService": "ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com" } }
}

Service role trust policies should also say which of your resources may use them, with aws:SourceAccount or aws:SourceArn, so a service acting for someone else can't be tricked into using your role (the “confused deputy” of Chapter 21).

8.8How tools find credentials

The AWS CLI and SDKs look for credentials in a fixed order, the credential provider chain, and use the first they find. Knowing the order explains most “why is it using those credentials?” puzzles:

Table 8.2 · Where the CLI and SDKs look, roughly in order
SourceExampleTypical use
Command-line options--profile prodchoosing a profile per command
Environment variablesAWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID, AWS_SESSION_TOKEN, AWS_PROFILEscripts, CI, temporary sessions
Web identity token fileAWS_WEB_IDENTITY_TOKEN_FILE + AWS_ROLE_ARNKubernetes pods (Chapter 16)
Config and credentials files~/.aws/config: SSO profiles, role_arn + source_profile, credential_processpeople on laptops
Container credentialsAWS_CONTAINER_CREDENTIALS_RELATIVE_URIECS tasks
Instance metadataIMDSv2 on EC2EC2 instances (Chapter 15)

When in doubt, aws sts get-caller-identity tells you who you are right now, and works whatever your permissions.

8.9Changing roles from the console

People with IAM users can also switch role in the AWS console: the console assumes the role for them, with MFA if the trust policy requires it, and shows the role's name in the top bar. A CLI profile does the same:

[profile prod-operator]
role_arn = arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/prod-operator
source_profile = eli
mfa_serial = arn:aws:iam::111122223333:mfa/eli-phone
role_session_name = eli
duration_seconds = 3600

The CLI then asks for a six-digit code before assuming the role. One limit to know: MFA for API and CLI calls accepts only one-time-code devices (an authenticator app or a hardware TOTP token), not FIDO2 security keys or passkeys, which work for console sign-in only. With single sign-on (Chapter 12), this whole step disappears: people sign in once, with whatever factor the identity provider requires, choose an account and a permission set, and get a role session directly.

8.10Across providers

Table 8.3 · People, workloads and borrowed identities at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
PeopleIdentity Center users or federated people; IAM users (legacy)Entra ID usersGoogle accounts (Cloud Identity / Workspace) or federated identities
Workloadsroles assumed by servicesmanaged identities; service principalsservice accounts
Borrowing an identitysts:AssumeRole → temporary credentialsno assume step: roles are assigned to the identity directly; Privileged Identity Management activates roles for a timeservice account impersonation (roles/iam.serviceAccountTokenCreator) → short-lived tokens
Long-lived keys to avoidIAM user access keysservice principal client secretsservice account keys (JSON)

On Azure

Azure has no “assume role”: you assign roles directly to a user, group or managed identity at a scope. Temporary elevation comes from Privileged Identity Management (PIM), where an eligible person activates a role for a few hours, with justification and optional approval (Chapter 14).

On Google Cloud

The closest thing to assuming a role is impersonating a service account: a principal with the Token Creator role on a service account can get short-lived tokens for it, for example with gcloud --impersonate-service-account. Avoid downloading service account keys, which are long-lived like IAM user access keys; organisations can forbid them with an organisation policy.

8.11At Larder: ops becomes a role

At Larder: solved

Eli did it in three steps in the first week of December. First, the leavers: he deactivated the access keys of Jasper's and the two contractors' users, removed their console passwords, waited a week to see whether anything broke (nothing did), and deleted the users. Second, he created a role, prod-operator, with ops's old powers minus IAM changes, a one-hour maximum session, and the trust policy from 8.6, listing the six engineers on the on-call rota (8.6 shows two of them). Each of them registered two MFA devices on their own IAM user, their new security key for the console and an authenticator app for the CLI, and got a CLI profile like the one in 8.9. Third, he removed ops's password and detached its policies; the user itself goes in Chapter 12, along with every other IAM user.

The difference shows in CloudTrail. A production change now reads assumed-role/prod-operator/ben, with sourceIdentity: ben, and Ben had to pass MFA to make it. Had the backup change from Chapter 1 happened this way, the first line of the investigation would have named a person.

8.12Lab: create, assume and inspect a role

Lab 8 · A role, a session and a source identity

You'll create a read-only role, assume it with a source identity, look at the temporary credentials and the session's ARN, find the limits of what the session can do, and see the assumption in CloudTrail. You need the AWS CLI v2, signed in to a sandbox account as an administrator (Appendix C), and a Unix shell. Cost: free.

  1. Create the role. It trusts your account (so your administrator permissions decide whether you may assume it) and allows setting a source identity. 
    ACCOUNT=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
cat > trust.json <<EOF
{ "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{ "Effect": "Allow",
                  "Principal": { "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::$ACCOUNT:root" },
                  "Action": ["sts:AssumeRole", "sts:SetSourceIdentity"] }] }
EOF
aws iam create-role --role-name iam-lab-reader --assume-role-policy-document file://trust.json
aws iam attach-role-policy --role-name iam-lab-reader \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/AmazonS3ReadOnlyAccess
    Wait about ten seconds for IAM to catch up.
  2. Assume it for 15 minutes and load the session into your shell: 
    read -r AK SK ST EXP <<< "$(aws sts assume-role \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::$ACCOUNT:role/iam-lab-reader \
  --role-session-name lab-$USER --source-identity $USER --duration-seconds 900 \
  --query 'Credentials.[AccessKeyId,SecretAccessKey,SessionToken,Expiration]' --output text)"
export AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID=$AK AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=$SK AWS_SESSION_TOKEN=$ST
echo "$AK expires $EXP"
aws sts get-caller-identity --query Arn --output text
    The key starts with ASIA, expires in 15 minutes, and you are now arn:aws:sts::…:assumed-role/iam-lab-reader/lab-you.
  3. Find the session's edges. Predict each result first: 
    aws s3 ls                                                      # a
aws iam list-users                                             # b
aws sts assume-role --role-arn arn:aws:iam::$ACCOUNT:role/iam-lab-reader \
  --role-session-name again                                    # c
    a works (the role reads S3). b is denied: nothing in the role's permission policies allows iam:ListUsers, and the error message says so. c is denied too: the trust policy would allow it, but the session's own permissions don't include sts:AssumeRole. Both sides must agree, which is Chapter 10's subject.
  4. Return to yourself with unset AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY AWS_SESSION_TOKEN, and check with get-caller-identity.
  5. Find it in CloudTrail. After a few minutes (CloudTrail can take up to about 15 to show events): 
    aws cloudtrail lookup-events --max-results 5 \
  --lookup-attributes AttributeKey=EventName,AttributeValue=AssumeRole \
  --query 'Events[].CloudTrailEvent' --output text |
python3 -c 'import sys, json
for line in sys.stdin:
    e = json.loads(line); p = e.get("requestParameters") or {}
    print(e["eventTime"], e["userIdentity"].get("arn"), "→", p.get("roleSessionName"), p.get("sourceIdentity"))'
    You'll see who assumed the role, the session name and the source identity.
  6. Clean up. 
    aws iam detach-role-policy --role-name iam-lab-reader \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/AmazonS3ReadOnlyAccess
aws iam delete-role --role-name iam-lab-reader
rm trust.json

Stretch goals. (1) Add the MFA condition from 8.6 to the trust policy, then try step 2 again: it fails until you pass --serial-number and --token-code. (2) Set the role's maximum session duration to two hours with aws iam update-role --max-session-duration 7200 and assume it with --duration-seconds 7200.

8.13Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
IAM users for peopleLong-lived passwords and keys; leavers keep access.Single sign-on with role sessions.
Access keys for workloads on AWSKeys in config files, images and Git history.Service roles (Chapter 15).
Wide trust ("AWS": "*", whole accounts, unconditioned providers)Strangers or partners' admins can assume the role.Named principals plus conditions.
iam:PassRole on *Anyone who can launch a service gets any role's permissions.Specific role ARNs and iam:PassedToService.
Meaningless session namesCloudTrail can't tell sessions apart.Session names and source identity that name the person or job.
Long maximum session durations “for convenience”Stolen sessions stay useful for hours.One hour for powerful roles; longer only where needed.

✓Chapter summary

  • IAM users have long-lived passwords and keys (AKIA…) in one account; they're a legacy pattern. Groups bundle users' policies and aren't principals.
  • A role has no credentials; trusted principals assume it and get temporary ones (ASIA…) from STS.
  • A role = a trust policy (who may assume it, when) + permission policies (what a session may do).
  • Sessions last 15 minutes to 12 hours (chained ones, one hour); session names and source identity keep accountability.
  • Trust specific principals with conditions; an account principal delegates to that account's policies.
  • Services act through service roles; iam:PassRole decides who may hand them one, so restrict it.
  • The CLI and SDKs use the first credentials in the provider chain; get-caller-identity tells you which.

Key terms

IAM user
A long-lived identity with a password and/or access keys in one account.
IAM group
A set of IAM users sharing policies; not a principal.
IAM role
An identity without credentials, assumed for temporary sessions.
Trust policy
The resource-based policy on a role that says who may assume it.
STS
AWS Security Token Service, which issues temporary credentials.
Role session
A period of use of a role, with its own ARN and temporary credentials.
Source identity
An unchangeable value recorded for every action in a session and its chained sessions.
Service role
A role an AWS service assumes to act for you.
iam:PassRole
The permission to give a role to an AWS service.
Credential provider chain
The order in which tools look for credentials.

Check your understanding

  1. You find AKIAQEXAMPLE… in a public repository. What kind of credential is it, and why does that make it worse than ASIA…? recall

    Show answer

    A long-lived IAM user (or root) access key. It works until deleted, whereas an ASIA key is part of a temporary session that expires within hours. Deactivate it now, then investigate its use in CloudTrail.

  2. A trust policy names arn:aws:iam::111122223333:root. Can every user in that account assume the role? explain

    Show answer

    Only those whose own identity policies allow sts:AssumeRole on this role. Naming the account delegates the choice to that account's administrators; it doesn't mean the root user, and it doesn't mean everyone.

  3. Write a trust policy for a role that ECS tasks in your account use. apply

    Show answer

    {"Effect": "Allow", "Principal": {"Service": "ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com"}, "Action": "sts:AssumeRole", "Condition": {"StringEquals": {"aws:SourceAccount": "111122223333"}}}, optionally with aws:SourceArn limited to your cluster's tasks.

  4. A developer may create Lambda functions and has iam:PassRole on *. Explain how they could become an administrator. explain

    Show answer

    They create a function, pass it an existing administrator role, and invoke it: the function's code runs with administrator permissions and can, for example, attach AdministratorAccess to the developer. Restrict PassRole to specific roles.

  5. Six engineers share a powerful role. Design it so that CloudTrail still shows which of them did what, and nobody can pretend to be another. design

    Show answer

    Require sts:SetSourceIdentity with sts:SourceIdentity equal to ${aws:username} (or the IdP's user attribute with SSO), require MFA, and keep sessions to an hour. Every event then carries a source identity that the person couldn't choose freely.

  6. Compare how a person gets temporary admin rights on AWS and on Azure. compare

    Show answer

    AWS: they assume a role (directly or through an SSO permission set) and get a time-limited session. Azure: they're made eligible for a role in Privileged Identity Management and activate it for a few hours, with justification or approval; there's no separate identity to assume.

Going further

  • AWS: IAM roles, source identity, passing roles to services, and the SDKs' credential providers.
  • Azure: Managed identities. Google Cloud: Service account impersonation.

Part II · IAM on AWS

9

Reading and writing IAM policies

Almost everything you're allowed to do on AWS comes down to a few lines of JSON. Most people copy those lines from somewhere and hope. This chapter takes a policy apart field by field, so you can read any statement aloud as a plain sentence, write one that does exactly one job, and check it before it ships.

By the end you can

  • Read any IAM policy statement as a plain sentence: who may do what, to what, and when.
  • Write an identity policy scoped to one bucket prefix and one secret.
  • Narrow a policy with conditions, variables and tags.
  • Choose between AWS managed, customer managed and inline policies.
  • Validate a policy with IAM Access Analyzer before you attach it.

Before you start

  • Principals, actions and resources (Chapter 1).
  • Least privilege and default deny (Chapter 6).
  • What a role is (Chapter 8). This chapter recaps the little it needs.

This chapter

Reading
about 50 minutes
Lab
30 minutes, AWS, well under $0.01
Main provider
AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 9.8

At Larder

In November 2026, after the network work of Handbook 1, Eli, Larder's platform tech lead, audits who can do what in the production account. Most of what he finds can wait a week. One thing can't. Dispatch, the service that assigns couriers and plans the vans' routes, runs on the managed container service with a role whose only policy was copied from a blog post during the move in April:

{ "Effect": "Allow", "Action": "s3:*", "Resource": "*" }

Anyone who gets code running inside a Dispatch container can read, overwrite or delete every bucket in the account, including the nightly order exports. Dispatch actually needs three things. Section 9.9 writes the policy that gives it exactly those.

9.1What a policy is

An IAM policy is a JSON document that allows or denies actions on AWS resources. Every request to AWS (an API call from the console, the CLI, an SDK or another AWS service) is checked against the policies that apply to it, and AWS answers allow or deny before anything else happens.

Policies come in two main families, depending on what they're attached to:

  • An identity-based policy is attached to a user, a group or a role, and says what that identity may do. “Dispatch's role may read objects under config/.” Most of this chapter is about these.
  • A resource-based policy is attached to a resource, such as an S3 bucket, a KMS key or an SQS queue, and says who may use it. “This bucket may be read by the Dispatch role.” These matter most when access crosses accounts (Chapter 21).

Both use the same grammar. Two rules decide what an unlucky combination of them means, and they're worth learning before anything else:

Key idea

Nothing is allowed until a statement allows it, and an explicit deny beats any allow.

There are more policy types (permission boundaries, session policies, and the organisation-wide guardrails called SCPs and RCPs), and the full order in which AWS combines them is the subject of Chapter 10. For now, one identity and its policies are enough.

9.2Anatomy of a statement

A policy is a Version and a list of statements. Each statement is one rule. Here is the smallest useful policy, with one statement:

read-config.jsonidentity-based policy

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "ReadConfig",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "s3:GetObject",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::larder-dispatch-prod/config/*",
      "Condition": { "Bool": { "aws:SecureTransport": "true" } }
    }
  ]
}

Field by field:

  • Version is the version of the policy language, not of your policy. Always write "2012-10-17". The only older value, "2008-10-17", doesn't understand policy variables (9.6), and leaving Version out means the old one.
  • Sid (statement ID) is an optional label. Use it: ReadConfig tells the next reader why the statement exists, and it shows up in some tools' output.
  • Effect is Allow or Deny.
  • Action is one action or a list of them (9.3).
  • Resource is one ARN or a list of them (9.4).
  • Condition is optional and narrows when the statement applies (9.5).
  • Principal appears only in resource-based policies and trust policies, because an identity policy's principal is whoever it's attached to.
Reading habitBefore you approve any policy change, read each statement aloud in the form “Allow / Deny actions on resources, when conditions.” Vague sentences (“allow everything on S3”) are the bugs.

When a policy has several statements, they are not checked in order. Every statement that matches the request counts, and the two rules from 9.1 combine them: any matching Deny wins; otherwise any matching Allow allows; otherwise the request is denied.

9.3Actions

An action names one API operation, written service:Operation: s3:GetObject, secretsmanager:GetSecretValue, ec2:StartInstances. The prefix is the service's short name, and action names are case-insensitive (s3:getobject works, but nobody will thank you for it).

You can use * and ? as wildcards. s3:Get* matches every S3 action that starts with Get, and s3:* matches all of them. Wildcards look tidy, but they age badly: when AWS adds a new action whose name happens to match, your policy grants it without anyone reviewing it. s3:Get* already covers dozens of actions today, including reading bucket policies and object ACLs. Prefer listing exact actions; it's a few more lines and every one of them is a decision someone made.

Where do the names come from? The Service Authorization Reference (linked in Going further) lists, for every service, each action, the resource types it accepts, and the condition keys it supports. Keep it open while you write policies. Two surprises it will show you:

  • One API call can need several actions. Copying an object needs s3:GetObject on the source and s3:PutObject on the destination. Reading a secret encrypted with your own KMS key needs secretsmanager:GetSecretValue and kms:Decrypt (Chapter 18).
  • Action names don't always match API names. The S3 API call ListObjectsV2 is authorised by the action s3:ListBucket, and HeadObject by s3:GetObject.

Watch out: NotAction with Allow

NotAction means “every action except these”. In an Allow statement, "NotAction": "iam:*" with "Resource": "*" grants every action in every other service, including all the ones AWS launches next year. It has a narrow legitimate use in Deny statements (“deny everything except these, outside the approved regions”), which you'll meet in Chapter 20. In an Allow, treat it as a bug.

9.4Resources and ARNs

Every AWS resource has an Amazon Resource Name (ARN), a string that identifies it uniquely across all of AWS. The Resource element lists the ARNs a statement applies to, with the same * and ? wildcards as actions. Unlike actions, ARNs are case-sensitive.

Table 9.1 · Six ARNs taken apart
ARNWhat it namesNotice
arn:aws:s3:::larder-dispatch-proda bucketno region or account
arn:aws:s3:::larder-dispatch-prod/config/*every object under config/the bucket is a different resource from its objects
arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/dispatch-taskan IAM roleIAM is global: no region
arn:aws:secretsmanager:eu-central-1:111122223333:secret:dispatch/prod/maps-api-key-??????one secretAWS adds six random characters to every secret's name; match them with ??????
arn:aws:dynamodb:eu-central-1:111122223333:table/slotsa DynamoDB tableits indexes are table/slots/index/*
arn:aws:ecs:eu-central-1:111122223333:service/prod/dispatchan ECS servicecluster, then service name

The most common mistake in all of IAM lives in the first two rows. A bucket and the objects in it are different resources, and each S3 action works on one of them:

  • s3:ListBucket (list the keys) acts on the bucket: arn:aws:s3:::larder-dispatch-prod.
  • s3:GetObject, s3:PutObject and s3:DeleteObject act on objects: arn:aws:s3:::larder-dispatch-prod/* or a narrower path.

Put ListBucket on the object ARN, or GetObject on the bucket ARN, and the statement simply never matches. AWS doesn't complain when you save it; the request is just denied later.

403, not 404Without s3:ListBucket, asking for an object that doesn't exist returns AccessDenied instead of NoSuchKey: S3 won't tell you what's missing if you aren't allowed to list. That's why “access denied” sometimes really means “typo in the key”.

Finally, some actions don't work on a specific resource at all. s3:ListAllMyBuckets, ec2:DescribeInstances and sts:GetCallerIdentity only accept "Resource": "*"; the Service Authorization Reference shows a blank resource type for them. That's the one legitimate reason to write "*" in a Resource element. If an action does support resource ARNs, use them.

9.5Conditions

Actions and resources say what. A condition says when: only from this network, only over TLS, only in these regions, only for resources tagged with my team. A condition block has three levels: a condition operator, then a condition key (a value AWS knows about the request), then the value or values to compare it with.

Condition keys come in two kinds:

  • Global keys start with aws: and exist for most requests: aws:SourceIp, aws:SecureTransport, aws:RequestedRegion, aws:PrincipalTag/key, aws:ResourceTag/key, aws:PrincipalOrgID (the data perimeter key of Chapter 22).
  • Service keys start with the service's prefix and exist only for some of its actions: s3:prefix for ListBucket, ec2:InstanceType for RunInstances. The Service Authorization Reference lists which keys each action sends.
Table 9.2 · The condition operators you'll use most
OperatorComparesExample
StringEquals, StringNotEqualsexact text (case-sensitive)"aws:PrincipalTag/team": "dispatch"
StringLiketext with * and ?"s3:prefix": ["config/*", "routes/*"]
Bool"true" or "false""aws:SecureTransport": "true"
IpAddress, NotIpAddressan address against CIDR ranges"aws:SourceIp": "203.0.113.0/24"
DateLessThan, DateGreaterThantimes"aws:CurrentTime": "2027-01-31T23:59:59Z"
ArnLike, ArnEqualsARNs"aws:SourceArn": "arn:aws:sns:…"
Nullwhether a key is present"aws:ResourceTag/team": "false" (the tag must exist)

A missing key fails the check. If a statement has StringEquals on aws:ResourceTag/team and the resource has no team tag, the condition is false and the statement doesn't match. Add IfExists to the operator (StringEqualsIfExists) to mean “check it if it's there, otherwise pass”. Use that carefully: in an Allow, it lets untagged resources through.

Keys with several values. A few keys carry a list rather than one value, such as the tag keys in a tagging request (aws:TagKeys). For them, prefix the operator with ForAnyValue: (at least one value in the request matches) or ForAllValues: (every value in the request matches).

Watch out: ForAllValues and the empty list

ForAllValues is true when every value matches, and if the request has no values at all, every one of none matches: it's true. So an Allow meant as “only these tag keys” also allows a request with no tags. Pair it with a Null check that the key is present, or write the rule as a Deny with ForAnyValue:StringNotEquals.

9.6Policy variables and tags

A policy variable is a placeholder, written ${…}, that AWS fills in from the request when it evaluates the policy. Variables let one policy serve many identities. They work in Resource and in condition values (and need "Version": "2012-10-17").

The classic example gives each IAM user a home folder:

{ "Effect": "Allow", "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject"],
  "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::larder-scratch/home/${aws:username}/*" }

aws:username only exists for IAM users, and you'll stop using IAM users in Chapter 8 and Chapter 12. With roles, the useful variables are tags. A principal tag is a key-value pair on the role (or passed in from your identity provider when someone signs in), and a resource tag is one on the resource. Compare the two, and one statement covers every team:

team-secrets.jsonattribute-based access control

{
  "Sid": "ReadOwnTeamsSecrets",
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Action": "secretsmanager:GetSecretValue",
  "Resource": "*",
  "Condition": {
    "StringEquals": { "aws:ResourceTag/team": "${aws:PrincipalTag/team}" }
  }
}

Read aloud: “Allow reading any secret whose team tag equals my own team tag.” Dispatch's role is tagged team=dispatch, so it reads secrets tagged team=dispatch and nothing else; a new team needs a tag, not a new policy. This is attribute-based access control (ABAC) from Chapter 6, and Chapter 12 uses it with single sign-on. It has one catch: whoever can change tags can change access. Protect tagging itself (only the platform team may set team), or ABAC becomes a polite suggestion.

9.7Managed, inline, and how many

The same policy JSON can live in three places:

Table 9.3 · Where a policy can live
AWS managedCustomer managedInline
Written byAWSyouyou
Reusableyes, attach anywhereyes, attach anywhereno, part of one user, group or role
VersionsAWS updates it for youup to five kept; roll back with one callnone: edits replace it
Sizen/a6,144 characters each10,240 characters for all of a role's inline policies together
Use it forread-only and job-function access to start with, and in sandboxesmost real permissionsa strict one-to-one link, deleted with its role
LimitsAt the time of writing. A role can have 10 managed policies attached by default, and the quota can be raised a little; if you're near it, the role is probably doing too many jobs.

AWS managed policies are convenient and well maintained, but they're written for everyone, so they're broad: AmazonS3ReadOnlyAccess reads every bucket. Use them where breadth is the point (a ReadOnlyAccess permission set for engineers, or the job-function policies like ViewOnlyAccess) and write your own for workloads. You can't edit an AWS managed policy; to start from one, copy its JSON into a customer managed policy and cut it down.

Watch out: the “full access” family

AdministratorAccess, PowerUserAccess and the …FullAccess policies (AmazonS3FullAccess, AmazonDynamoDBFullAccess…) on a production workload mean that one bug or one leaked credential owns the service, or the account. Larder's deploy-bot user had AdministratorAccess; Chapter 17 replaces it. If you see one of these on a role that runs code, it's a finding.

9.8Across providers

The three big providers agree on the ideas (principals, actions, resources, conditions) but package them differently. AWS attaches permission documents to principals. Azure and Google Cloud define roles (named sets of permissions) and bind a role to a principal at a scope, such as a subscription, a project or one bucket. Where you grant it is part of the grant.

Table 9.4 · Writing permissions at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Unit of permissionaction, s3:GetObjectoperation, Microsoft.Storage/…/blobs/readpermission, storage.objects.get
What you writea policy: statements with effect, actions, resources, conditionsa role definition: Actions, NotActions, DataActions, NotDataActions, assignable scopesa custom role: a list of permissions
How it's grantedattach the policy to a user, group or rolea role assignment: principal + role + scopean allow policy on a resource: role bound to principals
Which resourcesARNs in the policythe assignment's scope (management group, subscription, resource group or resource)the resource the binding is on, and everything below it in the hierarchy
Conditionscondition block, many keysrole assignment conditions (ABAC), mainly for storage dataIAM Conditions written in CEL
Explicit denyany Deny statementdeny assignments (limited; mostly set by Azure itself)separate IAM deny policies

On Azure

Built-in roles such as Storage Blob Data Reader cover most needs; a custom role is a JSON role definition whose DataActions cover data (reading blobs) and Actions cover management (creating the storage account). You then assign it to a user, group or managed identity at the narrowest scope that works: the one storage account, not the subscription. Narrowing to a folder of blobs uses an assignment condition, for example on the blob path.

On Google Cloud

You grant a role such as roles/storage.objectViewer by adding a binding to a resource's allow policy: on the bucket for one bucket, on the project for every bucket in it. Bindings inherit downwards through organisation, folders and projects, and can't be taken away lower down by an allow policy. Narrowing within a bucket uses an IAM Condition such as resource.name.startsWith("projects/_/buckets/larder-dispatch-prod/objects/config/").

9.9Writing a least-privilege policy: a method

Least privilege (Chapter 6) is easy to agree with and hard to write. This method gets you there without guessing:

  1. List what the code does, in plain words, from the code and its owners: “reads its config file”, “writes tonight's route plans”.
  2. Map each to actions with the Service Authorization Reference. Remember the hidden extras (a kms:Decrypt, a ListBucket).
  3. Narrow the resources to exact ARNs or prefixes. "*" only for actions that accept nothing else.
  4. Add conditions where they're cheap and meaningful: a prefix, a tag, the organisation.
  5. Validate with IAM Access Analyzer (in the lab), which catches typos, impossible combinations and risky patterns before anything is attached.
  6. Test the real workload in a non-production environment, including the things it should not be able to do.
  7. Watch CloudTrail for AccessDenied from the role for a few days after rollout (Chapter 11 shows how).

At Larder: solved

Eli walks the method through Dispatch with Ben's team. Step 1 gives three sentences: Dispatch reads its zone and depot settings from config/; Engine A writes each evening's van route plans to routes/; and Dispatch reads the maps provider's API key. Step 2 adds s3:ListBucket, because the settings loader lists config/ to find its files. The result, attached to the dispatch-task role:

dispatch-task-policy.jsoncustomer managed, attached to role dispatch-task

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "ListOwnPrefixes",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "s3:ListBucket",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::larder-dispatch-prod",
      "Condition": { "StringLike": { "s3:prefix": ["config/*", "routes/*"] } }
    },
    {
      "Sid": "ReadConfig",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "s3:GetObject",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::larder-dispatch-prod/config/*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "WriteRoutePlans",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "s3:PutObject",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::larder-dispatch-prod/routes/*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "ReadMapsKey",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "secretsmanager:GetSecretValue",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:eu-central-1:111122223333:secret:dispatch/prod/maps-api-key-??????"
    }
  ]
}

Read aloud, it's four sentences, and each one maps to something Dispatch does. Things worth noticing:

  • No delete. Dispatch overwrites route plans but never deletes them, so a bug or an intruder can't empty the bucket. Old versions are kept by bucket versioning, set on the bucket.
  • Listing is limited to two prefixes by the s3:prefix condition. Listing the whole bucket, or orders/, is denied.
  • The secret's random suffix is matched with ?????? rather than *, so a secret later named maps-api-key-old isn't included by accident.
  • The secret uses the default AWS managed key for Secrets Manager, so no kms:Decrypt statement is needed. With a customer managed key it would be (Chapter 18).
  • TLS is enforced on the bucket, not here: a bucket policy that denies any request with aws:SecureTransport false protects the data from every principal, not only Dispatch.

At Larder: rollout

The new policy goes to staging first, where the team runs a full evening of route planning and checks that writing to config/ and reading orders/ are both refused. In production, Eli attaches it alongside the old one, watches CloudTrail for a day to confirm nothing Dispatch does depends on the old policy alone, and then detaches s3:*. The account audit has 40 more findings, and Chapter 8 and Chapter 17 pick up the two worst.

9.10Lab: give a role exactly one job

Lab 9 · A Dispatch-style role, tested and validated

You'll build a bucket shaped like Dispatch's, a role with the policy from 9.9, then predict and test six calls as that role, break the policy twice, and let IAM Access Analyzer review a sloppy policy. You need the AWS CLI v2, signed in to a sandbox account with administrator access (Appendix C), and a Unix shell. Cost: IAM and policy validation are free; the bucket holds two tiny files, well under $0.01. Clean-up is step 7.

  1. Set up names, a bucket and two files. Bucket names are global, so the name gets a random suffix. orders/ stands in for data Dispatch must never read. 
    ACCOUNT=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
B=iam-lab-dispatch-$RANDOM$RANDOM
aws s3 mb s3://$B --region eu-central-1
echo '{"zones": 3}' > zones.json
aws s3 cp zones.json s3://$B/config/zones.json
aws s3 cp zones.json s3://$B/orders/export.json
echo "bucket: $B"
  2. Create the role. Its trust policy (the subject of Chapter 8) lets identities in your own account assume it, if their own policies allow that, which an administrator's do. 
    cat > trust.json <<EOF
{ "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{ "Effect": "Allow", "Action": "sts:AssumeRole",
                  "Principal": { "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::$ACCOUNT:root" } }] }
EOF
aws iam create-role --role-name iam-lab-dispatch \
  --assume-role-policy-document file://trust.json
  3. Give it the Dispatch policy (the S3 part of 9.9, for your bucket), as an inline policy to keep clean-up simple. 
    cat > policy.json <<EOF
{ "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    { "Sid": "ListOwnPrefixes", "Effect": "Allow", "Action": "s3:ListBucket",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::$B",
      "Condition": { "StringLike": { "s3:prefix": ["config/*", "routes/*"] } } },
    { "Sid": "ReadConfig", "Effect": "Allow", "Action": "s3:GetObject",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::$B/config/*" },
    { "Sid": "WriteRoutePlans", "Effect": "Allow", "Action": "s3:PutObject",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::$B/routes/*" } ] }
EOF
aws iam put-role-policy --role-name iam-lab-dispatch --policy-name dispatch-s3 \
  --policy-document file://policy.json
  4. Make a CLI profile that assumes the role, then wait about ten seconds: IAM changes take a moment to reach every region. If you normally use a named profile, put its name instead of default. 
    aws configure set profile.lab.role_arn arn:aws:iam::$ACCOUNT:role/iam-lab-dispatch
aws configure set profile.lab.source_profile ${AWS_PROFILE:-default}
aws sts get-caller-identity --profile lab     # the Arn should end in assumed-role/iam-lab-dispatch/…
  5. Predict, then test. For each call, write down “works” or “denied”, and which statement decides, before you run it. 
    aws s3 ls s3://$B/config/ --profile lab                          # a
aws s3 cp s3://$B/config/zones.json - --profile lab              # b
aws s3 cp zones.json s3://$B/routes/plan.json --profile lab      # c
aws s3 cp zones.json s3://$B/config/zones.json --profile lab     # d
aws s3 cp s3://$B/orders/export.json - --profile lab             # e
aws s3 ls s3://$B/ --profile lab                                 # f
    Answers: a, b and c work (ListOwnPrefixes, ReadConfig, WriteRoutePlans). d is denied: there's no PutObject on config/. e is denied: nothing allows orders/. f is denied too: listing the top of the bucket sends an empty prefix, which matches neither pattern.
  6. Break it on purpose. In policy.json, change the ReadConfig resource to arn:aws:s3:::$B/config (drop /*; the variable has already been filled in, so edit the real bucket name), run put-role-policy again, wait, and repeat call b: denied, because the ARN now names one object called config. Put it back. Then change the ListOwnPrefixes resource to arn:aws:s3:::$B/* and repeat call a: denied, because ListBucket acts on the bucket, not on objects. Put that back too.
  7. Let Access Analyzer review a sloppy policy. This one has a typo, a wildcard PassRole, and the bucket/object mix-up: 
    cat > sloppy.json <<EOF
{ "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    { "Effect": "Allow", "Action": ["s3:GetObjects", "s3:ListBucket"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::$B/config/*" },
    { "Effect": "Allow", "Action": "iam:PassRole", "Resource": "*" } ] }
EOF
aws accessanalyzer validate-policy --policy-type IDENTITY_POLICY \
  --policy-document file://sloppy.json \
  --query 'findings[].[findingType, issueCode]' --output table
    You should see an ERROR for the action that doesn't exist and a SECURITY_WARNING for passing any role, plus whatever it says about ListBucket on an object ARN. Fix the policy until the only findings left are ones you can explain, then run the same check on your policy.json: no findings.
  8. Clean up. 
    aws s3 rb s3://$B --force
aws iam delete-role-policy --role-name iam-lab-dispatch --policy-name dispatch-s3
aws iam delete-role --role-name iam-lab-dispatch
rm zones.json trust.json policy.json sloppy.json
    Then delete the [profile lab] section from ~/.aws/config.

Stretch goals. (1) Write the ReadMapsKey statement for a secret in your account and validate it without creating the secret: Access Analyzer checks the grammar, not whether the resource exists. (2) Use the role for a few minutes, then, in the IAM console, open the role and choose Generate policy: Access Analyzer builds a policy from the role's CloudTrail activity. Compare it with yours (Chapter 24 uses this at scale).

9.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Bucket ARN for object actions, or object ARN for ListBucketThe statement never matches; requests are denied.bucket for ListBucket, bucket/* (or a prefix) for objects.
NotAction in an AllowGrants everything else, including future services.List the actions; keep NotAction for guardrail denies.
Wildcard actions such as s3:Get*The grant grows whenever AWS adds a matching action.Exact actions from the Service Authorization Reference.
"Resource": "*" for actions that support ARNsAccess to every resource of that type in the account.Exact ARNs or prefixes; * only where nothing else is accepted.
A condition on a key the action doesn't sendThe statement never matches (or always does, with IfExists).Check the action's condition keys in the reference.
*FullAccess or AdministratorAccess on a workloadOne bug or leaked credential controls the service or the account.A customer managed policy per workload, built with the 9.9 method.
Inline policies scattered over many rolesNobody can find or review what's granted.Customer managed policies, managed as code (Chapter 24).

✓Chapter summary

  • A policy is a list of statements. Each one says: Allow or Deny these actions on these resources, when these conditions hold. Read each one aloud.
  • Default deny; an explicit deny beats any allow; statements aren't ordered.
  • Actions are service:Operation. Prefer exact actions to wildcards, and never NotAction in an allow.
  • Resources are ARNs. A bucket and its objects are different resources.
  • Conditions: values in a list are OR, everything else is AND. Missing keys fail unless you use IfExists; watch ForAllValues on empty lists.
  • Variables and tags let one policy serve many identities (ABAC), as long as tagging is protected.
  • Customer managed policies for workloads; AWS managed ones where breadth is the point; inline rarely.
  • List, map, narrow, condition, validate, test, watch: least privilege without guessing.

Key terms

Policy
A JSON document of statements that allow or deny actions on resources.
Statement
One rule in a policy: effect, actions, resources and optional conditions.
Identity-based policy
A policy attached to a user, group or role.
Resource-based policy
A policy attached to a resource, naming the principals that may use it.
Action
One API operation, written service:Operation.
ARN
Amazon Resource Name: the unique identifier of an AWS resource.
Condition key
A value AWS knows about a request, compared in a condition block.
Policy variable
A ${…} placeholder filled in from the request at evaluation time.
Principal tag / resource tag
Key-value pairs on an identity or a resource, used for ABAC.
Managed / inline policy
A standalone, reusable policy, versus one embedded in a single identity.
IAM Access Analyzer
The AWS tool that validates policies and generates them from activity.

Check your understanding

  1. Read this aloud as one sentence: {"Effect": "Deny", "Action": "s3:DeleteObject", "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::larder-dispatch-prod/routes/*"}. recall

    Show answer

    “Deny deleting any object under routes/ in the bucket larder-dispatch-prod.” Because it's an explicit deny, no other policy can allow it.

  2. A role has s3:ListBucket on arn:aws:s3:::reports/*, and aws s3 ls s3://reports/ is denied. Why, and what's the fix? apply

    Show answer

    ListBucket acts on the bucket, but the resource names objects, so the statement never matches. Use arn:aws:s3:::reports, optionally with an s3:prefix condition.

  3. Why is "Action": "s3:Get*" riskier than it looks, even if every Get action today seems harmless? explain

    Show answer

    It already includes reading bucket policies, ACLs and other configuration, and it will include any matching action AWS adds in future, without anyone reviewing the change.

  4. Write a statement that lets a role read only secrets tagged with the same team as the role. What must you also control for it to be safe? design

    Show answer

    Allow secretsmanager:GetSecretValue on "*" with StringEquals "aws:ResourceTag/team": "${aws:PrincipalTag/team}". You must also control who can set the team tag on roles and secrets, or anyone who can tag can grant themselves access.

  5. A condition block has StringEquals on aws:RequestedRegion with two regions, and Bool on aws:SecureTransport. A request comes from eu-west-1 over plain HTTP. Does the statement apply? apply

    Show answer

    No. The region matches one of the two values (OR), but the two operators must both hold (AND), and the request didn't use TLS.

  6. Compare how you'd let one service read one storage bucket on AWS and on Google Cloud. compare

    Show answer

    AWS: write a policy with the read actions and the bucket's ARNs, and attach it to the service's role. Google Cloud: bind a role such as roles/storage.objectViewer to the service account in the bucket's allow policy; the scope of the binding decides which resource it covers.

Going further

  • AWS: IAM JSON policy element reference, Service Authorization Reference, global condition keys and Access Analyzer policy validation.
  • Azure: Understand role definitions.
  • Google Cloud: IAM overview and IAM Conditions.

Part II · IAM on AWS

10

How AWS decides: policy evaluation logic

A single request to AWS can be touched by up to seven kinds of policy: the caller's own, the resource's, a permissions boundary, a session policy, and the organisation's guardrails. AWS combines them in a fixed order, and once you know that order, every “why was this allowed?” and “why was this denied?” has an answer. This chapter teaches the order, one policy type at a time, with a flowchart you'll use for the rest of your career.

By the end you can

  • Name every policy type that can affect a request, and say which can grant and which can only limit.
  • Walk a request through AWS's evaluation order and predict the result.
  • Explain why an explicit deny beats AdministratorAccess, and use that for guardrails.
  • Say what both sides must allow for cross-account access.
  • Use permissions boundaries to let teams create roles safely.

Before you start

  • Reading policies (Chapter 9) and roles and trust policies (Chapter 8).

This chapter

Reading
about 50 minutes
Lab
25 minutes, Python 3; optional AWS CLI step, free
Main provider
AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 10.9

At Larder

Chapter 1 left Eli with a promise: nobody should be able to shorten the orders database's backups, “whoever asks”. In the first week of December he writes a policy that denies changing or deleting the orders database, and deleting any database backup, and attaches it to the engineers' group and to the new prod-operator role (Chapter 8).

The next morning, Ben tries a harmless change to the database's maintenance window as prod-operator, a role with full production powers, and gets “access denied”. “I'm an admin,” he writes in the team chat, “how can anything deny me?” A week later Marco, the CFO, asks a sharper question: “So now it's impossible to shorten the backups?” It isn't, and section 10.10 explains why not.

10.1One request, many policies

Chapter 9 looked at one policy at a time. In reality, every request is judged against all the policies that apply to it at once. There are seven kinds, and they divide into two groups: policies that can grant access, and policies that can only limit it.

Table 10.1 · The policy types AWS evaluates
TypeAttached toCan grant?Purpose
Identity-based policya user, group or roleyeswhat this identity may do (Chapter 9)
Resource-based policya resource: bucket, key, queue, role trustyeswho may use this resource
Permissions boundarya user or roleno: a ceilingthe most an identity may ever have
Session policyone role session, passed when it's createdno: a ceilingnarrowing a session below its role
Service control policy (SCP)an organisation, OU or member accountno: a ceilingthe most any principal in those accounts may do
Resource control policy (RCP)an organisation, OU or member accountno: a ceilingthe most anyone may do to resources in those accounts
ACLssome older resources (S3, VPC endpoints for some services)yeslegacy cross-account grants; avoid (Chapter 6)

Two rules from Chapter 9 still hold across all of them: nothing is allowed unless something allows it, and an explicit deny in any of them wins.

10.2The order of evaluation

For a request within one account, AWS asks these questions in order. The first one that decides, decides:

Read it as two kinds of question. Steps ①, ②, ③, ⑥ and ⑦ can only say no: they're filters. Steps ④ and ⑤ are where a yes comes from. A request is allowed only if something grants it and nothing filters it out.

SimplifiedThe full rules have more detail, especially about how boundaries and session policies interact with resource-based policies that name a role rather than a session. AWS's documentation has the complete flowchart; this one is right for the cases you'll meet most.

10.3Explicit deny: guardrails

Step ① is why Ben, with full production powers, was denied. AdministratorAccess is just an identity-based policy that allows * on *; it answers step ⑤. An explicit deny in any attached policy is checked first, at step ①, and nothing later can undo it. That makes explicit deny the tool for guardrails: rules that hold however generous someone's other permissions are.

protect-orders-db.jsoncustomer managed policy, Larder

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "OnlyDbChangeMayAlterOrdersDb",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": ["rds:ModifyDBInstance", "rds:DeleteDBInstance"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:rds:eu-central-1:111122223333:db:orders-prod",
      "Condition": { "StringNotLike": { "aws:PrincipalArn": [
        "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/db-change",
        "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/break-glass-*"
      ] } }
    },
    {
      "Sid": "NobodyDeletesBackups",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": ["rds:DeleteDBSnapshot", "rds:DeleteDBInstanceAutomatedBackup"],
      "Resource": "*"
    }
  ]
}

Read aloud: “Deny changing or deleting the orders database, unless you're the db-change role or a break-glass role. Deny deleting any database snapshot or automated backup, for everyone.” The pattern in the first statement (deny, except for named principals through aws:PrincipalArn) is the standard way to write a guardrail with a controlled exception. Use aws:PrincipalArn rather than the session ARN, because it gives the role's ARN for any session of that role.

Key idea

Allows add up; one deny ends the discussion. Grant with allows, and draw lines that nobody may cross with denies.

Watch out: a guardrail only guards what it's attached to

An identity-based deny protects only from the identities it's attached to. Every user, group and role you forget, including the next one somebody creates, is outside it. That's the gap Marco's question found. Guardrails that must hold for everyone in an account belong one level up, in an SCP (10.8, Chapter 20), or, for resources that support them, in a resource-based policy.

10.4Resource-based policies in the same account

Step ④ is easy to miss. Within one account, if a resource-based policy (a bucket policy, a KMS key policy, a role's trust policy) allows a specific principal, that's enough: the principal doesn't also need an identity-based allow. That's why a trust policy that names user/eli lets Eli assume the role, while one that names the whole account (…:root) also needs Eli's own policies to allow sts:AssumeRole (Chapter 8, 8.6).

It also means resource policies deserve the same review as identity policies: a bucket policy that grants s3:* to a role gives that role full access to the bucket, whatever its identity policies say.

KMS is differentKMS keys are stricter: a key policy must allow access, either directly or by delegating to IAM policies with a statement for the account. Without that, even an administrator can't use the key (Chapter 18).

10.5Across accounts: both sides must agree

When the principal and the resource are in different accounts, there's no short cut at step ④. Each account controls its own side, and both must allow:

Assuming a role in another account works the same way: the caller's own account must allow sts:AssumeRole on that role's ARN, and the role's trust policy in the other account must trust the caller. Once assumed, the session lives in the role's account and only its permissions there count (Chapter 21).

10.6Permissions boundaries: safe delegation

A permissions boundary is a managed policy set on a user or role as its ceiling: the identity can do only what both its own policies and the boundary allow. It grants nothing by itself.

Boundaries exist to solve one problem well: letting teams create their own roles without letting them create roles more powerful than they should. Without a boundary, anyone allowed to create roles and attach policies can create an administrator role and assume it. With one, you allow role creation only if the new role carries the boundary:

{
  "Sid": "CreateTeamRolesOnlyWithTheBoundary",
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Action": ["iam:CreateRole", "iam:AttachRolePolicy", "iam:PutRolePolicy"],
  "Resource": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/team-*",
  "Condition": { "StringEquals": {
    "iam:PermissionsBoundary": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:policy/team-boundary" } }
}

Pair it with a deny on iam:DeleteRolePermissionsBoundary and iam:PutRolePermissionsBoundary, and on editing the boundary policy itself, or the ceiling can simply be removed. Now a team can create team-reporting with AdministratorAccess attached and it still can only do what team-boundary allows. Put your guardrail denies inside the boundary too, and every role created this way carries them.

10.7Session policies

A session policy is passed when a role session is created (aws sts assume-role --policy … or --policy-arns …). The session gets the intersection of the role's permissions and the session policy. It's how a broker service can hand out one shared role, narrowed per request: “this session may only read tenant-42/”. People rarely write session policies by hand, but they explain some surprising denials, and IAM Identity Center and other services use them behind the scenes.

10.8SCPs and RCPs: the organisation's ceiling

When accounts belong to an AWS Organization (Chapter 20), two more ceilings apply, set centrally:

  • Service control policies limit what principals in member accounts may do, including those accounts' root users. A deny in an SCP applies to every role and user in the account, however created, which closes the gap from 10.3.
  • Resource control policies limit what anyone may do to resources in member accounts, including principals from outside the organisation. They support a growing list of services, starting with S3, STS, KMS, SQS and Secrets Manager.

Neither ever grants anything; by default each account has a policy allowing everything (FullAWSAccess, RCPFullAWSAccess), and you add denies or replace the default with an allow-list. Two exceptions to remember: SCPs and RCPs don't apply to the management account, and SCPs don't restrict service-linked roles.

10.9Across providers

Table 10.2 · How the big three combine permissions
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Grants combine byunion of allows, after filtersunion of role assignments at the scope and aboveunion of allow-policy bindings on the resource and its ancestors
Explicit denyany Deny statement, anywheredeny assignments, created by Azure for managed applications and deployment stacks, not written directlyIAM deny policies, evaluated before allow policies
Organisation ceilingsSCPs (principals), RCPs (resources)Azure Policy (resource configuration, not permissions); management group RBACdeny policies at organisation or folder; organisation policy constraints (configuration)
Delegation ceilingpermissions boundariesrole assignment conditions limiting which roles a delegate may assignconditions on who may grant which roles

On Azure

Azure RBAC is additive: a principal's permissions are the union of its role assignments at every scope above the resource, and there's no way for you to write a general-purpose deny. Guardrails come from elsewhere: Azure Policy stops non-compliant resources being created or changed (no public storage accounts, only these regions), and resource locks (CanNotDelete, ReadOnly) protect critical resources from deletion by anyone, owners included.

On Google Cloud

Google Cloud evaluates IAM deny policies first, then the union of allow-policy bindings inherited down the resource hierarchy. Deny policies attach to the organisation, folders or projects and can exempt principals, which makes them the nearest equivalent to an AWS guardrail with an exception. Configuration rules (such as “no external IPs”) are organisation policy constraints, a separate system.

10.10At Larder: who can still shorten the backups?

At Larder: solved

Ben's answer was step ①: an explicit deny in protect-orders-db beats the allow in his administrator policy. His maintenance-window change went through the new db-change role, which only Larder's two database owners may assume, with MFA, and whose sessions CloudTrail records like any other.

Marco's answer took Eli an afternoon with the lab's evaluator. The guardrail was attached to the engineers' group and prod-operator, but not to deploy-bot, the pipeline's IAM user with AdministratorAccess, which could still change the database. Nor would it be attached to any role created next month. Eli attached it to deploy-bot the same day, and added a rule for the team: every new role gets the team-boundary permissions boundary, which contains the same denies, and only roles with it can be created by anyone but the platform team. He told Marco the honest version: it's now very hard, not impossible, and it becomes impossible when the accounts join an organisation and the guardrail moves into an SCP (Chapter 20).

10.11Lab: an evaluator you can read

Lab 10 · Figure 10.1 in a hundred lines

You'll run a small implementation of AWS's evaluation order on eight of Larder's requests, predict each result first, then change policies and see which step decides. Optionally, you'll check one case against AWS's own policy simulator. You need Python 3; for the last step, the AWS CLI. Cost: free. The evaluator supports only the policy features this chapter uses; it's for learning, not for checking real policies.

evaluate.pyPython 3.8+

# evaluate.py · AWS policy evaluation logic for one request, simplified for learning
from fnmatch import fnmatchcase

def as_list(x):
    return x if isinstance(x, list) else [x]

def statement_matches(st, req, check_principal=False):
    if not any(fnmatchcase(req["action"].lower(), a.lower()) for a in as_list(st.get("Action", []))):
        return False
    if not any(fnmatchcase(req["resource"], r) for r in as_list(st.get("Resource", "*"))):
        return False
    if check_principal:
        allowed = as_list(st.get("Principal", {}).get("AWS", []))
        account_root = f"arn:aws:iam::{req['principal_account']}:root"
        names = {req["principal"], req["context"]["aws:PrincipalArn"], account_root, "*"}
        if not names & set(allowed):
            return False
    for op, tests in st.get("Condition", {}).items():
        for key, want in tests.items():
            have = req["context"].get(key)
            if op == "StringEquals" and have not in as_list(want):
                return False
            if op == "StringNotLike" and (have is None or any(fnmatchcase(have, w) for w in as_list(want))):
                return False
            if op == "Bool" and str(have).lower() != str(want).lower():
                return False
    return True

def decide(req, identity=(), resource=None, boundary=None, session=None, scp=None, rcp=None):
    policies = {"identity-based policy": identity, "resource-based policy": resource or [],
                "permissions boundary": boundary or [], "session policy": session or [],
                "SCP": scp or [], "RCP": rcp or []}
    def hits(kind, effect):
        return any(st["Effect"] == effect and statement_matches(st, req, kind == "resource-based policy")
                   for st in policies[kind])
    for kind in policies:                                         # 1. any explicit deny wins
        if hits(kind, "Deny"):
            return f"DENY   explicit deny in {kind}"
    if rcp is not None and not hits("RCP", "Allow"):              # 2. RCPs must allow
        return "DENY   no RCP allows it"
    if scp is not None and not hits("SCP", "Allow"):              # 3. SCPs must allow
        return "DENY   no SCP allows it"
    same_account = req["principal_account"] == req["resource_account"]
    by_resource = hits("resource-based policy", "Allow")
    if same_account and by_resource:                              # 4. same account: resource policy is enough
        return "ALLOW  by resource-based policy"
    if not hits("identity-based policy", "Allow"):                # 5. identity policy must allow
        return "DENY   no identity-based policy allows it"
    if boundary is not None and not hits("permissions boundary", "Allow"):
        return "DENY   permissions boundary doesn't allow it"     # 6. boundary caps it
    if session is not None and not hits("session policy", "Allow"):
        return "DENY   session policy doesn't allow it"           # 7. session policy caps it
    if not same_account:                                          # cross-account needs both sides
        return ("ALLOW  identity- and resource-based policies both allow it" if by_resource
                else "DENY   cross-account: no resource-based policy allows it")
    return "ALLOW  by identity-based policy"

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- Larder's policies
PROD, STOCKPILOT = "111122223333", "999988887777"
ADMIN = [{"Effect": "Allow", "Action": "*", "Resource": "*"}]
GUARDRAIL = [{"Effect": "Deny",
              "Action": ["rds:ModifyDBInstance", "rds:DeleteDBInstance"],
              "Resource": f"arn:aws:rds:eu-central-1:{PROD}:db:orders-prod",
              "Condition": {"StringNotLike": {"aws:PrincipalArn": [f"arn:aws:iam::{PROD}:role/db-change",
                                                                   f"arn:aws:iam::{PROD}:role/break-glass-*"]}}},
             {"Effect": "Deny",
              "Action": ["rds:DeleteDBSnapshot", "rds:DeleteDBInstanceAutomatedBackup"], "Resource": "*"}]
DEV_BOUNDARY = [{"Effect": "Allow", "Action": ["s3:*", "logs:*", "dynamodb:*"], "Resource": "*"}]
BUCKET = [{"Effect": "Allow", "Principal": {"AWS": f"arn:aws:iam::{STOCKPILOT}:role/stock-sync"},
           "Action": "s3:GetObject", "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::larder-stock-exports/*"}]
READ_EXPORTS = [{"Effect": "Allow", "Action": "s3:GetObject", "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::larder-stock-exports/*"}]
SCP_ALLOW_LIST = [{"Effect": "Allow", "Action": ["s3:*", "ec2:*", "ecs:*", "logs:*"], "Resource": "*"}]

def principal_arn(arn):
    """A role session's aws:PrincipalArn is its role's ARN; anything else is itself."""
    if ":assumed-role/" not in arn:
        return arn
    return f"arn:aws:iam::{arn.split(':')[4]}:role/{arn.split('/')[1]}"

def req(principal, action, resource, resource_account=PROD):
    return {"principal": principal, "action": action, "resource": resource,
            "principal_account": principal.split(":")[4], "resource_account": resource_account,
            "context": {"aws:PrincipalArn": principal_arn(principal)}}

DB = f"arn:aws:rds:eu-central-1:{PROD}:db:orders-prod"
cases = [
    ("1 admin changes the orders DB, no guardrail",
     req(f"arn:aws:sts::{PROD}:assumed-role/prod-operator/ben", "rds:ModifyDBInstance", DB), dict(identity=ADMIN)),
    ("2 same, guardrail attached",
     req(f"arn:aws:sts::{PROD}:assumed-role/prod-operator/ben", "rds:ModifyDBInstance", DB), dict(identity=ADMIN + GUARDRAIL)),
    ("3 the db-change role, guardrail attached",
     req(f"arn:aws:sts::{PROD}:assumed-role/db-change/pipeline", "rds:ModifyDBInstance", DB), dict(identity=ADMIN + GUARDRAIL)),
    ("4 deploy-bot: guardrail forgotten",
     req(f"arn:aws:iam::{PROD}:user/deploy-bot", "rds:ModifyDBInstance", DB), dict(identity=ADMIN)),
    ("5 developer role inside a boundary",
     req(f"arn:aws:sts::{PROD}:assumed-role/team-tool/dee", "iam:CreateUser", f"arn:aws:iam::{PROD}:user/x"),
     dict(identity=ADMIN, boundary=DEV_BOUNDARY)),
    ("6 StockPilot reads an export, no bucket policy",
     req(f"arn:aws:sts::{STOCKPILOT}:assumed-role/stock-sync/s1", "s3:GetObject", "arn:aws:s3:::larder-stock-exports/today.csv"),
     dict(identity=READ_EXPORTS)),
    ("7 same, with the bucket policy",
     req(f"arn:aws:sts::{STOCKPILOT}:assumed-role/stock-sync/s1", "s3:GetObject", "arn:aws:s3:::larder-stock-exports/today.csv"),
     dict(identity=READ_EXPORTS, resource=BUCKET)),
    ("8 admin, under an allow-list SCP",
     req(f"arn:aws:sts::{PROD}:assumed-role/prod-operator/eli", "rds:ModifyDBInstance", DB), dict(identity=ADMIN, scp=SCP_ALLOW_LIST)),
]
for name, r, pols in cases:
    print(f"{name:<48} {decide(r, **pols)}")
  1. Predict, then run. Cover the output below, write down ALLOW or DENY and the step from Figure 10.1 for each case, then run python3 evaluate.py: 
    1 admin changes the orders DB, no guardrail      ALLOW  by identity-based policy
2 same, guardrail attached                       DENY   explicit deny in identity-based policy
3 the db-change role, guardrail attached         ALLOW  by identity-based policy
4 deploy-bot: guardrail forgotten                ALLOW  by identity-based policy
5 developer role inside a boundary               DENY   permissions boundary doesn't allow it
6 StockPilot reads an export, no bucket policy   DENY   cross-account: no resource-based policy allows it
7 same, with the bucket policy                   ALLOW  identity- and resource-based policies both allow it
8 admin, under an allow-list SCP                 DENY   no SCP allows it
    Cases 1 and 2 are Ben's morning. Case 4 is Marco's question. Case 8 previews Chapter 20: even an administrator can't use a service the organisation hasn't allowed.
  2. Close the gap. Add GUARDRAIL to case 4's identity policies. Then add a case for a brand-new role with AdministratorAccess and no guardrail: it's allowed. Finally, give that role boundary=DEV_BOUNDARY + GUARDRAIL, the 10.10 fix, and run again.
  3. Break it on purpose. In the guardrail's exception list, change role/break-glass-* to role/*, a common slip, and run case 2 again: every role in the account is now an exception, and the guardrail protects nothing. In case 7, change the bucket policy's principal to StockPilot's whole account (arn:aws:iam::999988887777:root): still allowed, but now any identity there whose own policies allow it can read Larder's exports.
  4. Optional: ask AWS. Save the GUARDRAIL statements as guardrail.json (a full policy, with Version), then run AWS's simulator, which is free: 
    aws iam simulate-custom-policy \
  --policy-input-list file://guardrail.json '{"Version":"2012-10-17","Statement":[{"Effect":"Allow","Action":"*","Resource":"*"}]}' \
  --action-names rds:ModifyDBInstance \
  --resource-arns arn:aws:rds:eu-central-1:111122223333:db:orders-prod \
  --context-entries "ContextKeyName=aws:PrincipalArn,ContextKeyValues=arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/prod-operator,ContextKeyType=string" \
  --query 'EvaluationResults[].[EvalActionName, EvalDecision]' --output text
    Expect explicitDeny. Change the context value to …:role/db-change and expect allowed.

10.12Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
“Admins can do anything”Surprise at guardrails; or guardrails never written.Explicit deny for lines nobody may cross.
Guardrails attached identity by identityEvery forgotten or new identity bypasses them.Permissions boundaries for new roles; SCPs and RCPs for everyone.
Exceptions with wide wildcards (role/*)The exception swallows the rule.Exact role names or a narrow, reserved prefix.
Expecting a boundary or SCP to grantAccess still denied after adding one.Ceilings only filter; grant with identity or resource policies.
Reviewing identity policies onlyBucket, key and trust policies grant access unnoticed.Review resource-based policies too; use Access Analyzer (Chapter 23).
Granting cross-account to a whole accountEvery admin in the other account gets in.Name specific roles; add conditions.

✓Chapter summary

  • Seven policy types can apply. Identity and resource policies grant; boundaries, session policies, SCPs and RCPs only limit.
  • Order: explicit deny anywhere → RCPs → SCPs → resource policy (same account) → identity policy → boundary → session policy.
  • An explicit deny beats every allow, including AdministratorAccess: use it for guardrails, with exceptions via aws:PrincipalArn.
  • In one account, a resource policy naming a principal is enough; across accounts, both sides must allow.
  • Permissions boundaries let teams create roles safely, if the boundary can't be removed.
  • Identity-attached guardrails cover only what they're attached to; organisation-wide ones belong in SCPs and RCPs.

Key terms

Explicit deny
A matching Deny statement in any policy; always wins.
Implicit deny
The default result when nothing allows a request.
Guardrail
A deny that holds regardless of other permissions.
Permissions boundary
A managed policy that caps an identity's maximum permissions.
Session policy
A policy passed at session creation that narrows a role session.
SCP
Service control policy: an organisation ceiling on principals in member accounts.
RCP
Resource control policy: an organisation ceiling on access to resources in member accounts.
aws:PrincipalArn
The ARN of the calling user or role (a role session's role), used for exceptions.

Check your understanding

  1. Which policy types can grant access, and which can only take it away? recall

    Show answer

    Identity-based and resource-based policies (and legacy ACLs) can grant. Permissions boundaries, session policies, SCPs and RCPs can only limit.

  2. A role has AdministratorAccess and a permissions boundary allowing only s3:*. Can it create an IAM user? Can it delete a bucket? apply

    Show answer

    No: the boundary doesn't allow iam:CreateUser. Yes: both the identity policy and the boundary allow s3:DeleteBucket, unless something denies it explicitly.

  3. A bucket in account A has a policy allowing a role in account B to read it. The role's own policies allow nothing. Can it read the bucket? What if the role were in account A? apply

    Show answer

    From account B, no: across accounts both sides must allow, and B's side doesn't. In account A, yes: within one account a resource-based policy naming the principal is enough.

  4. Why is an explicit deny in an identity policy a weaker guardrail than the same deny in an SCP? explain

    Show answer

    The identity policy protects only from the identities it's attached to; any other or new identity bypasses it. An SCP applies to every principal in the member account, however it was created, including the root user.

  5. Design a way for Larder's product teams to create roles for their own services without being able to give themselves more power than the platform team allows. design
