    Show answer

    A team-boundary policy with the services teams may use and the guardrail denies. Teams may create and modify roles named team-* only with that boundary (condition on iam:PermissionsBoundary), may pass only team-* roles, and are denied removing or changing boundaries and editing the boundary policy itself.

  6. Compare how you'd stop anyone deleting a production database on AWS and on Azure. compare

    Show answer

    AWS: an explicit deny on the delete action (in an SCP to cover everyone, with an exception for a break-glass role), plus RDS deletion protection. Azure: a CanNotDelete resource lock, which blocks deletion even by owners until someone with permission removes the lock, and Azure Policy for configuration rules.

Going further

  • AWS: Policy evaluation logic (the full flowchart), cross-account evaluation, permissions boundaries, RCPs.
  • Azure: resource locks. Google Cloud: IAM deny policies.

Part II · IAM on AWS

11

Debugging “access denied”

Every engineer who works with AWS meets “access denied” weekly. Most people respond by adding permissions until it goes away, which is how s3:* on * ends up in production. This chapter gives you a method instead: find out exactly who asked for exactly what, read what AWS tells you about why it said no, confirm it in CloudTrail, and fix the one policy that's actually responsible.

By the end you can

  • Follow a seven-step method from an error message to the policy that caused it.
  • Read the reason in an AWS access-denied message, and decode an encoded one.
  • Find denied calls in CloudTrail, including calls one service made for another.
  • Tell real permission problems from look-alikes: expired tokens, wrong accounts, clock skew.
  • Fix a denial with the smallest correct change.

Before you start

  • The evaluation order (Chapter 10), roles and sessions (Chapter 8), reading policies (Chapter 9).

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
30 minutes, Python 3 and AWS CLI, free
Main provider
AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 11.8

At Larder

On Tuesday 8 December 2026, Dispatch's new least-privilege policy from Chapter 9 went to staging for its trial evening. At 18:00, Engine A started planning tomorrow's van routes, read its settings from config/ without trouble, and then failed to write a single route plan to routes/: AccessDenied, for every van.

The policy plainly allowed s3:PutObject on routes/*. Ben's first suggestion in the chat was to put s3:* back “just for staging”. Eli asked for twenty minutes instead. Section 11.9 shows what he found, and it wasn't in the policy anyone was looking at.

11.1A method

“Access denied” is a symptom, and like any symptom it has several possible causes. Guessing (adding permissions until it works) usually fixes it by granting far more than needed, and sometimes doesn't fix it at all, because the cause was never a missing permission. Work through it in order:

  1. Who am I? Confirm the exact principal making the call: which role, which session, which account (11.2).
  2. What exactly was asked? The action, the resource ARN, the region. Not “S3”: s3:PutObject on arn:aws:s3:::larder-dispatch-staging/routes/2026-12-09/van-1.json.
  3. Read the error all the way to the end. It increasingly names the type of policy that said no (11.3).
  4. Find it in CloudTrail, including any calls made on your behalf by another service (11.4).
  5. Walk the evaluation order from Chapter 10 for that request: deny, RCP, SCP, resource policy, identity policy, boundary, session policy.
  6. Rule out look-alikes: expired credentials, the wrong account or region, clock skew, a missing object (11.6).
  7. Fix the one responsible policy with the smallest change, validate it, and test the original request again (11.7).

Key idea

Find out exactly who asked for exactly what, then let AWS tell you which policy said no. Don't add a permission until you know which one is missing.

11.2Who am I, really?

A surprising share of permission problems are identity problems: the call wasn't made by who you think. Leftover environment variables from an earlier session, a default profile pointing at another account, a container that picked up the node's role instead of its own (Chapter 8, 8.8). Always start with:

aws sts get-caller-identity
{
    "UserId": "AROAEXAMPLEID:engine-a-7f9c",
    "Account": "444455556666",
    "Arn": "arn:aws:sts::444455556666:assumed-role/dispatch-task/engine-a-7f9c"
}

This call always works, whatever your permissions. For code, log the same thing at start-up: the SDK has the same call. Check the account number as carefully as the role name: production and staging roles often share names.

11.3Read the error

AWS's access-denied messages have become much more helpful. Most services now say who was denied, what they tried, on which resource, and which type of policy is responsible:

Table 11.1 · What the reason says, and where to look
The message saysLook at
because no identity-based policy allowsthe user's or role's own policies (and its groups')
with an explicit deny in an identity-based policya Deny statement attached to the identity, such as a guardrail (Chapter 10)
because no resource-based policy allows / with an explicit deny in a resource-based policythe bucket, key, queue or trust policy; for cross-account calls, the other account's side
because no permissions boundary allowsthe identity's permissions boundary
because no session policy allowswhatever created the session: a broker, a federation setup, an SSO permission set
…service control policy / …resource control policythe organisation's guardrails; ask the platform team (Chapter 20)
…VPC endpoint policythe policy on the VPC endpoint the call went through (Handbook 1, Chapter 12)
a bare Access Deniedcommon from S3 data calls: bucket policy, Block Public Access, object ownership, KMS, or a missing object (11.6)

Some services, notably EC2, return an encoded authorization failure message instead of the full detail, because it can reveal information about your policies. Someone with sts:DecodeAuthorizationMessage can decode it:

aws sts decode-authorization-message --encoded-message "$MSG" \
  --query DecodedMessage --output text | python3 -m json.tool

The result shows whether the request was allowed, whether an explicit deny matched, any matched statements, and the request's full context: principal, action, resource and condition keys.

11.4Find it in CloudTrail

CloudTrail records API calls made in your account: who, what, when, from where, and whether it failed. Denied calls are recorded with an errorCode such as AccessDenied or Client.UnauthorizedOperation and, usually, the same errorMessage the caller saw. What to know before you go looking:

  • Management events (creating, changing and deleting resources, and many reads) are recorded by default and searchable for 90 days in the console's Event history or with aws cloudtrail lookup-events, per region.
  • Data events (S3 object reads and writes, Lambda invocations, DynamoDB item calls) are not recorded unless a trail is configured for them, because there are so many. Larder records S3 data events for its production buckets (Chapter 23).
  • Global services such as IAM and STS log in us-east-1 (or the region of the STS endpoint used).
  • Events appear after a delay, typically a few minutes and occasionally up to about 15.

The most useful field for puzzles like Larder's is userIdentity.invokedBy. When S3 needs a KMS key to encrypt an object you upload, S3 calls KMS as you, and CloudTrail records that KMS call with invokedBy: s3.amazonaws.com. If that call is denied, your upload fails with an error from S3, but the cause lives in a different service's event.

11.5Tools that walk the flowchart for you

  • IAM policy simulator (aws iam simulate-principal-policy, or the console): evaluates an existing user's or role's policies for given actions, resources and context keys, and says which statement allowed or denied. It covers identity policies, boundaries and SCPs; resource policies only if you pass them in.
  • IAM Access Analyzer policy validation: catches impossible policies before you blame AWS: misspelled actions, actions that don't support the resource you named (Chapter 9).
  • Last accessed information (in IAM's Access Advisor tab): shows which services a role has actually used, which helps when narrowing a policy without breaking it.

11.6It isn't always a permission

Table 11.2 · Errors that look like permission problems
You seeUsually meansCheck
ExpiredTokenthe session endedsign in again; for long jobs, refresh credentials (Chapter 8)
InvalidClientTokenIdthe access key doesn't exist (deleted, mistyped, or from another partition)get-caller-identity; which credentials the chain found
SignatureDoesNotMatchwrong secret key, or the request was alteredthe secret that goes with this key ID; proxies rewriting requests
RequestTimeTooSkewed or “signature expired”the machine's clock is more than about 15 minutes offtime sync on the host
S3 AccessDenied on GetObjectpossibly a missing object, if you lack s3:ListBucket (Chapter 9, 9.4)the exact key; list the prefix with someone who can
“not found” for something that existswrong region or wrong account--region, AWS_REGION, the account in get-caller-identity
AccessDeniedException from KMSthe key's own policy, not IAMthe key policy and grants (Chapter 18)

11.7Fixing it well

Once you know which policy said no, the fix is usually one statement. Keep it small:

  • Add the exact action on the exact resource, not a wildcard. If the message says kms:GenerateDataKey on one key, that's what to allow, on that key.
  • Fix it where it belongs. If a guardrail blocked something legitimate, change the guardrail's exception deliberately, or use the approved path (such as Larder's db-change role), rather than removing the guardrail.
  • Sometimes the fix isn't a permission at all. A staging setting that doesn't match production, a request to the wrong region, a missing object.
  • Validate, then repeat the original request. Also try one request that should still fail, so you know you didn't open more than you meant to.
  • Write it down in the commit message or ticket: which call, which reason, which statement changed. The next person will thank you.

Watch out: “just for now” wildcards

A wildcard added to make an error go away under pressure is almost never removed: the error is gone, so nobody looks again. Larder's s3:* on * started exactly that way in April. If you must unblock something urgently, add the narrowest statement you can, with a Sid that says it's temporary and a ticket to replace it.

11.8Across providers

Table 11.3 · Debugging denied access at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
The errorAccessDenied, with principal, action, resource and policy typeAuthorizationFailed: the client's object ID, the action (Microsoft.Storage/…/read) and the scope403 PERMISSION_DENIED, naming the missing permission (storage.objects.get)
The logCloudTrail (data events optional)Activity log for management; resource logs for dataCloud Audit Logs (Data Access logs must be enabled)
Explaining a decisionpolicy simulator; decoded authorization messagesCheck access in the portal: a principal's role assignments at a scopePolicy Troubleshooter: why a principal does or doesn't have a permission, including deny policies

On Azure

Role assignments can take several minutes to take effect, and a cached token may not show a new group membership until it's refreshed. When a correct-looking assignment “doesn't work”, sign out and in again (or get a new token) before changing anything. For storage data, remember that management roles such as Contributor don't include data access: you need a data role such as Storage Blob Data Reader.

On Google Cloud

Google's errors name the exact permission that was missing, which makes the first step easy. Policy Troubleshooter then shows every binding on the resource and its ancestors that could grant it, any deny policy that blocks it, and whether conditions matched. Like AWS, it can only explain what's in IAM; organisation policy constraints and VPC Service Controls produce their own, differently worded errors.

11.9At Larder: the error was in another service

At Larder: solved

Eli's twenty minutes went like this. Engine A's logs showed the identity was right (dispatch-task, in the staging account). The upload's error ended with the reason in Figure 11.1: the denied action was kms:GenerateDataKey, not anything in S3. CloudTrail in the staging account confirmed it: one denied KMS call per van, each invokedBy: s3.amazonaws.com. The staging bucket, it turned out, had been set in 2025 to encrypt new objects by default with a customer managed KMS key, during an experiment nobody remembered. Production's bucket uses S3-managed keys. The config/ files read fine because the deploy pipeline uploads them with S3-managed encryption explicitly; Engine A's uploads took the bucket's default.

So the policy was right, and staging was wrong. Eli changed the staging bucket's default encryption to match production, the team re-ran the evening's planning, and every route plan was written. Ben's s3:* was never needed. Two follow-ups went on the list: compare staging and production bucket settings automatically, and decide properly whether Larder wants customer managed keys at all (Chapter 18), in which case Dispatch's policy will need kms:GenerateDataKey and kms:Decrypt on that one key.

11.10Lab: from error to cause

Lab 11 · Find, read and decode denials

You'll run a script that finds denied calls in CloudTrail events and says why, first on Larder's staging evening, then on denials you cause yourself; decode an encoded EC2 message; and ask the policy simulator. You need Python 3; for steps 3–7, the AWS CLI v2 signed in to a sandbox account as an administrator, and the role from Lab 8. Cost: free; no instances are created (the EC2 call is a dry run).

denied.pyPython 3.8+

# denied.py · find the denied calls in CloudTrail events and say why
import json, sys
from collections import Counter

def events(path):
    text = open(path).read().strip()
    if text.startswith("["):                             # aws cloudtrail lookup-events ... --output json
        return [json.loads(x) if isinstance(x, str) else x for x in json.loads(text)]
    if '"Records"' in text[:200]:
        return json.loads(text)["Records"]               # a CloudTrail log file from S3
    return [json.loads(line) for line in text.splitlines() if line.strip()]   # one event per line

def who(e):
    u = e["userIdentity"]
    name = u.get("sessionContext", {}).get("sessionIssuer", {}).get("arn") or u.get("arn", u.get("type"))
    return name + (f" (via {u['invokedBy']})" if "invokedBy" in u else "")

def why(e):
    msg = e.get("errorMessage", "")
    if " because " in msg:
        return msg.split(" because ", 1)[1].split(". Encoded")[0]
    if " with an explicit deny " in msg:
        return "explicit deny " + msg.split(" with an explicit deny ", 1)[1]
    if "Encoded authorization failure message" in msg:
        return "encoded: run aws sts decode-authorization-message"
    return msg or "(no message)"

DENIED = ("AccessDenied", "UnauthorizedOperation", "Forbidden")
counts, first = Counter(), {}
for e in events(sys.argv[1] if len(sys.argv) > 1 else "sample-events.jsonl"):
    if not any(d in e.get("errorCode", "") for d in DENIED):
        continue
    key = (who(e), f"{e['eventSource'].split('.')[0]}:{e['eventName']}", why(e))
    counts[key] += 1
    first.setdefault(key, e["eventTime"])

for (principal, action, reason), n in sorted(counts.items(), key=lambda kv: first[kv[0]]):
    print(f"{first[(principal, action, reason)]}  {n}×  {action}\n    who: {principal}\n    why: {reason}")
  1. Save Larder's staging evening as sample-events.jsonl: five events from the staging account, one per line, trimmed to the fields the script reads. 
    {"userIdentity": {"arn": "arn:aws:sts::444455556666:assumed-role/team-tool/dee", "sessionContext": {"sessionIssuer": {"arn": "arn:aws:iam::444455556666:role/team-tool"}}}, "eventTime": "2026-12-08T15:12:40Z", "eventSource": "ec2.amazonaws.com", "eventName": "RunInstances", "errorCode": "Client.UnauthorizedOperation", "errorMessage": "You are not authorized to perform this operation. User: arn:aws:sts::444455556666:assumed-role/team-tool/dee is not authorized to perform: ec2:RunInstances on resource: arn:aws:ec2:eu-central-1:444455556666:instance/* because no permissions boundary allows the ec2:RunInstances action. Encoded authorization failure message: Ws3Lb8EXAMPLE"}
{"userIdentity": {"arn": "arn:aws:sts::444455556666:assumed-role/dispatch-task/engine-a-7f9c", "sessionContext": {"sessionIssuer": {"arn": "arn:aws:iam::444455556666:role/dispatch-task"}}}, "eventTime": "2026-12-08T18:00:02Z", "eventSource": "s3.amazonaws.com", "eventName": "GetObject"}
{"userIdentity": {"arn": "arn:aws:sts::444455556666:assumed-role/dispatch-task/engine-a-7f9c", "sessionContext": {"sessionIssuer": {"arn": "arn:aws:iam::444455556666:role/dispatch-task"}}, "invokedBy": "s3.amazonaws.com"}, "eventTime": "2026-12-08T18:00:04Z", "eventSource": "kms.amazonaws.com", "eventName": "GenerateDataKey", "errorCode": "AccessDenied", "errorMessage": "User: arn:aws:sts::444455556666:assumed-role/dispatch-task/engine-a-7f9c is not authorized to perform: kms:GenerateDataKey on resource: arn:aws:kms:eu-central-1:444455556666:key/1234abcd-12ab-34cd-56ef-1234567890ab because no identity-based policy allows the kms:GenerateDataKey action"}

{"userIdentity": {"arn": "arn:aws:sts::444455556666:assumed-role/dispatch-task/engine-a-7f9c", "sessionContext": {"sessionIssuer": {"arn": "arn:aws:iam::444455556666:role/dispatch-task"}}, "invokedBy": "s3.amazonaws.com"}, "eventTime": "2026-12-08T18:00:07Z", "eventSource": "kms.amazonaws.com", "eventName": "GenerateDataKey", "errorCode": "AccessDenied", "errorMessage": "User: arn:aws:sts::444455556666:assumed-role/dispatch-task/engine-a-7f9c is not authorized to perform: kms:GenerateDataKey on resource: arn:aws:kms:eu-central-1:444455556666:key/1234abcd-12ab-34cd-56ef-1234567890ab because no identity-based policy allows the kms:GenerateDataKey action"}
  2. Run it with python3 denied.py: 
    2026-12-08T15:12:40Z  1×  ec2:RunInstances
    who: arn:aws:iam::444455556666:role/team-tool
    why: no permissions boundary allows the ec2:RunInstances action
2026-12-08T18:00:04Z  3×  kms:GenerateDataKey
    who: arn:aws:iam::444455556666:role/dispatch-task (via s3.amazonaws.com)
    why: no identity-based policy allows the kms:GenerateDataKey action
    The successful GetObject is skipped. Three uploads become one line, attributed to Dispatch's role but made via S3: Eli's answer in one glance. The other line is Dee, earlier that afternoon, discovering the team-boundary from Chapter 10 doesn't include EC2.
  3. Cause denials of your own. Recreate Lab 8's iam-lab-reader role (S3 read-only) and assume it, as in Lab 8, steps 1–2. As the role, run: 
    aws ec2 describe-vpcs                                  # denied, with a readable reason
MSG=$(aws ec2 run-instances --dry-run --instance-type t3.micro \
  --image-id resolve:ssm:/aws/service/ami-amazon-linux-latest/al2023-ami-kernel-default-x86_64 \
  2>&1 | sed -n 's/.*Encoded authorization failure message: //p')
echo "${MSG:0:60}…"
  4. Decode the message as yourself (unset the role's variables first, since the role can't decode): 
    unset AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY AWS_SESSION_TOKEN
aws sts decode-authorization-message --encoded-message "$MSG" \
  --query DecodedMessage --output text | python3 -m json.tool | head -30
    Find "allowed": false, "explicitDeny": false (an implicit deny: nothing allowed it), and the action and resource in context.
  5. Find your denial in CloudTrail, after a few minutes: 
    aws cloudtrail lookup-events --max-results 20 \
  --lookup-attributes AttributeKey=EventName,AttributeValue=DescribeVpcs \
  --query 'Events[].CloudTrailEvent' --output json > mine.json
python3 denied.py mine.json
  6. Ask the simulator what the role may do: 
    ACCOUNT=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
aws iam simulate-principal-policy \
  --policy-source-arn arn:aws:iam::$ACCOUNT:role/iam-lab-reader \
  --action-names ec2:DescribeVpcs s3:ListAllMyBuckets \
  --query 'EvaluationResults[].[EvalActionName, EvalDecision]' --output text
    Expect implicitDeny for EC2 and allowed for S3.
  7. Clean up the role as in Lab 8, step 6, and delete mine.json.

Stretch goal. Extend denied.py to print sourceIPAddress and the session name, then run it over a day of real events from an account you work in (lookup-events with --start-time). Denials you don't recognise are worth a look: they're either a broken workload or someone exploring.

11.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Adding wildcards until it worksOver-broad permissions that never get removed.Find the exact action, resource and policy type first.
Not checking who's callingHours spent fixing the wrong role, or the wrong account.aws sts get-caller-identity, every time.
Stopping at “AccessDenied”The reason at the end of the message is missed.Read the whole message; decode encoded ones.
Looking only at the service you calledDenials in KMS, STS or another service on your behalf are missed.Search CloudTrail for invokedBy and related services.
Expecting S3 object calls in Event history“It's not in CloudTrail”, when data events were never recorded.A trail with data events for important buckets.
Staging that differs from productionDenials (or successes) that don't happen in production.Keep environments' settings the same, and compare them automatically.

✓Chapter summary

  • Work in order: who, what, read the error, CloudTrail, the evaluation order, look-alikes, smallest fix.
  • aws sts get-caller-identity settles “who am I?”, whatever your permissions.
  • Access-denied messages name the principal, action, resource and usually the type of policy responsible; EC2's encoded messages can be decoded.
  • CloudTrail records denials; data events need a trail; invokedBy reveals calls made for you by other services.
  • Expired tokens, wrong accounts or regions, clock skew and missing objects look like permission problems.
  • Fix the one responsible policy with the narrowest change, and test both what should and shouldn't work.

Key terms

CloudTrail
AWS's record of API calls in an account.
Management event
A control-plane call (create, change, delete, many reads); recorded by default.
Data event
A data-plane call such as S3 object access; recorded only if a trail asks for it.
invokedBy
The service that made a call on a principal's behalf.
Encoded authorization message
A denial's details in encoded form, decoded with sts:DecodeAuthorizationMessage.
Policy simulator
AWS's tool for evaluating a principal's policies against chosen requests.
Last accessed information
Which services and actions an identity has actually used, and when.

Check your understanding

  1. What's the first command to run when a script gets “access denied”, and why? recall

    Show answer

    aws sts get-caller-identity (or its SDK equivalent from inside the script): it shows which principal, in which account, is really making the calls, and works regardless of permissions.

  2. An error ends “with an explicit deny in a service control policy”. Will adding the action to the role's policy help? apply

    Show answer

    No. An explicit deny wins over any allow, and an SCP is set by the organisation. The fix is to use an approved path or ask the platform team for a deliberate exception.

  3. An upload to S3 fails with “access denied”, and the role clearly has s3:PutObject on the prefix. Name two causes outside the role's S3 permissions. explain

    Show answer

    The bucket encrypts with a customer managed KMS key, and the role lacks kms:GenerateDataKey on it (or the key policy doesn't allow it); a bucket policy, VPC endpoint policy, SCP or RCP denies the request; or the request comes from a different identity or account than expected.

  4. You can't find a denied S3 GetObject in CloudTrail's Event history. Why might that be? explain

    Show answer

    GetObject is a data event, which isn't in Event history and is recorded only by a trail configured for S3 data events on that bucket. It might also simply not have arrived yet, or be in another region's history.

  5. Design a weekly report that would have caught Larder's staging problem before the trial evening. design

    Show answer

    Run a denial summary like denied.py over each account's CloudTrail events daily and post new principal/action/reason combinations to the team channel; and compare key settings (bucket encryption, public access, versioning) between staging and production buckets, flagging differences.

  6. Compare what you'd look at first for a denied call on AWS and on Google Cloud. compare

    Show answer

    AWS: the error's reason (which policy type) and the CloudTrail event, then the simulator. Google Cloud: the missing permission named in the error, then Policy Troubleshooter for that principal, permission and resource, which shows bindings and deny policies.

Going further

  • AWS: Troubleshoot access denied error messages, CloudTrail userIdentity element, IAM policy simulator.
  • Azure: Troubleshoot Azure RBAC. Google Cloud: Policy Troubleshooter.

Part III · Access for people

12

Single sign-on with IAM Identity Center

This is the chapter where people stop having IAM users. With IAM Identity Center, everyone signs in once, with strong MFA, and picks an account and a job; AWS hands them a role session for exactly that. Access is granted to groups, in one place, across every account. This chapter explains how the pieces fit, how to design permission sets and assignments, and what changes in the console, the CLI and CloudTrail.

By the end you can

  • Explain instances, identity sources, permission sets and account assignments, and how they become IAM roles.
  • Design a small set of permission sets by job, and assign them to groups.
  • Sign in to the console and the CLI through the AWS access portal.
  • Read Identity Center sessions in CloudTrail, and write guardrails that refer to them.
  • Retire IAM users safely.

Before you start

  • Roles and sessions (Chapter 8); access models (Chapter 6); federation (Chapter 5).
  • Guardrails and aws:PrincipalArn (Chapter 10).

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
30 minutes, Python 3; AWS steps need a sandbox organisation, free
Main provider
AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 12.9

At Larder

By January 2027, Larder's AWS access is safer than it was in November, and much more awkward. The eight engineers who use AWS have IAM users in the production account, and most of them a second one in the staging account; Larder's other ten engineers have no AWS access at all, and ask a colleague when they need to look at something. The six on call assume prod-operator with a code from an authenticator app, because the security keys Eli bought in November don't work for the CLI (Chapter 8). A new joiner needs two users, two passwords and two MFA devices before lunch; a leaver needs someone to remember both.

Eli's January goal is simple to state: by the end of the month, nobody at Larder has an IAM user. Section 12.10 shows how it went.

12.1Why single sign-on for the cloud

Everything in Part I points the same way: one identity per person, strong and phishing-resistant authentication, short-lived credentials, access granted to groups and removed in one place. IAM Identity Center is AWS's service for exactly that. It's where people sign in, it's connected to your company directory, and it turns “Ben is on the on-call team” into a one-hour role session in the production account, with nothing long-lived in between.

For people, it replaces IAM users completely. Workloads still use roles directly (Chapter 15); Identity Center is for humans.

12.2The pieces

  • An instance of Identity Center. To give access to AWS accounts you need an organisation instance, which lives in an AWS Organization's management account (or is administered from a delegated member account). An account instance in a standalone account can only serve applications, not AWS account access.
  • An identity source: where users and groups come from (12.4).
  • A permission set: a template for a role: AWS managed policies, references to customer managed policies, an inline policy, an optional permissions boundary, and a session duration (1 to 12 hours).
  • An account assignment: a user or group, a permission set, and an account. For each assignment, Identity Center creates an IAM role named AWSReservedSSO_PermissionSetName_suffix in that account, with a trust policy that only Identity Center can use. You don't edit these roles; you change the permission set, and Identity Center updates them everywhere.
  • The AWS access portal (https://your-alias.awsapps.com/start): where people sign in and choose an account and a permission set.

12.3It needs an organisation

Because account access needs an organisation instance, single sign-on is usually the moment a company creates an AWS Organization. Chapter 20 covers organisations properly; for now, three decisions matter:

  • Create it from a new, empty account. The management account can't be restricted by SCPs and controls every member, so it should run nothing else. Turning an existing production account into the management account means living with that forever.
  • Invite the existing accounts as members. Their resources, IAM users and roles are untouched by joining.
  • Choose Identity Center's region carefully; the instance lives in one region. Pick the one your team works in (Larder: Frankfurt, eu-central-1).

12.4Where people come from: identity sources

Table 12.1 · Identity Center's identity sources
SourceHow people sign inHow users and groups arriveUse it when
Identity Center directorya password and MFA stored in Identity Centeryou create them (console, CLI, API)no company IdP yet; small teams
Active DirectoryAD credentials, through AWS Managed Microsoft AD or AD Connectorsynced from ADan existing Windows estate
External IdPat your IdP (Entra ID, Google, Okta…), via SAML 2.0pushed by the IdP with SCIMyou have a company IdP: almost always the goal

You can start with the built-in directory and switch to an external IdP later; users are matched by user name, and assignments to groups carry over if the groups are recreated with the same names. That's Larder's path: the directory now, the company IdP in May (Chapter 13).

With the built-in directory, set MFA to be required at every sign-in, and allow only FIDO2 authenticators (security keys and passkeys) if you can. Unlike MFA for IAM users' CLI calls (Chapter 8), these work everywhere, CLI included, because the CLI signs in through the browser.

12.5Designing permission sets

Permission sets are roles by job (Chapter 6): describe what a kind of work needs, and let assignments say where. A handful usually covers a company of Larder's size:

Table 12.2 · Larder's permission sets
Permission setPoliciesSessionWho, where
ViewOnlyAWS managed ViewOnlyAccess: see configuration, not data8 hall engineers, prod
DeveloperPowerUserAccess, boundary team-boundary8 hall engineers, dev-staging
ProdOperatorcustomer managed prod-operator (from Chapter 8)1 hon-call group, prod
DbChangecustomer managed db-change1 hthe two database owners, prod
PlatformAdminAdministratorAccess1 hplatform team, every account
BillingAWS managed Billing job-function policy8 hfinance, management account

Two design notes:

  • ViewOnlyAccess rather than ReadOnlyAccess. ReadOnlyAccess reads data, including every object in every bucket and every secret's metadata; ViewOnlyAccess shows resources and configuration without their contents. For “all engineers in production”, that difference is everything.
  • Customer managed policy references attach a policy by name, which must exist in each account where the permission set is assigned. That lets the policy differ per account (with the right ARNs) while the permission set stays the same. Create the policy first, or provisioning fails.

12.6Assignments: groups, never people

An assignment can name a user or a group. Assign to groups, always. A group assignment is a policy you can review (“the on-call team operates production”); a user assignment is an exception nobody remembers. When someone joins, moves or leaves, you change their group memberships, ideally in the company directory (Chapter 13), and every assignment follows.

Key idea

Permission sets describe jobs; groups describe people; assignments say where. Nobody gets access as an individual.

Together, groups × permission sets × accounts are an access matrix (Chapter 6), and the lab turns them into one so you can read who can do what, where. Review it whenever it changes, and look for two things above all: assignments to individuals, and powerful permission sets held permanently in production. The second is Chapter 14's subject.

12.7Signing in: console, CLI and CloudTrail

For the CLI, configure a profile per account and permission set, sharing one sign-in session:

[sso-session larder]
sso_start_url = https://larder.awsapps.com/start
sso_region = eu-central-1
sso_registration_scopes = sso:account:access

[profile prod-operator]
sso_session = larder
sso_account_id = 111122223333
sso_role_name = ProdOperator
region = eu-central-1

aws sso login --sso-session larder opens the browser (the authorization code flow with PKCE from Chapter 4; --use-device-code for remote machines), and afterwards aws s3 ls --profile prod-operator just works, with credentials that are fetched and refreshed for you and never written to ~/.aws/credentials.

In CloudTrail, a session shows the role and the person:

"userIdentity": {
  "type": "AssumedRole",
  "arn": "arn:aws:sts::111122223333:assumed-role/AWSReservedSSO_ProdOperator_3f9a8c1e2d4b5a60/ben@larder.example",
  "sessionContext": { "sessionIssuer": {
    "arn": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/aws-reserved/sso.amazonaws.com/eu-central-1/AWSReservedSSO_ProdOperator_3f9a8c1e2d4b5a60" } }
}

Watch out: guardrails that name roles

Identity Center's roles have a generated suffix and a path, /aws-reserved/sso.amazonaws.com/, and the suffix changes if a permission set is deleted and recreated. A guardrail exception that names role/db-change (Chapter 10) stops matching once people work through Identity Center. Match on the stable part with a wildcard, arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/aws-reserved/sso.amazonaws.com/*AWSReservedSSO_DbChange_*, and test it.

12.8Attributes, not just groups

Identity Center can pass attributes from the identity source into every session as session tags (attributes for access control): a person's team, cost centre or department. A policy can then compare aws:PrincipalTag/team with a resource's team tag, which is the ABAC pattern from Chapter 9 (9.6), with the attribute coming from the company directory instead of being set by hand on a role. One permission set can then serve every team, each seeing only its own resources.

12.9Across providers

Table 12.3 · Workforce single sign-on at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Where people sign inIAM Identity Center (its directory, AD, or an external IdP)Entra ID, nativelyGoogle accounts (Cloud Identity / Workspace), or workforce identity federation
A job's permissionspermission setrole definitionIAM role
Granting itaccount assignment: group + permission set + accountrole assignment: group + role + scopeallow-policy binding: group + role on a resource
What the person getsa role session per accounttheir own identity, with the role at that scopetheir own identity, with the role on that resource
CLI sign-inaws sso loginaz logingcloud auth login

On Azure

On Azure, single sign-on isn't an extra service: Entra ID is the sign-in for the portal, CLI and every subscription, and people keep one identity everywhere. The equivalent design rule is the same: assign Azure RBAC roles to Entra ID groups at the narrowest scope, not to users.

On Google Cloud

People sign in with Google accounts managed in Cloud Identity or Workspace, usually federated to the company IdP, and groups are Google Groups, which you bind to roles on the organisation, folders or projects. Workforce identity federation lets people from an external IdP use Google Cloud without Google accounts at all, the closest match to Identity Center with an external IdP.

12.10At Larder: no more IAM users

At Larder: solved

On 5 January 2027, Eli created a new, empty account, larder-management (777788889999), and secured its root user by the checklist in Chapter 7, with the same group email pattern and two more security keys. From it he created Larder's AWS Organization and invited the production and staging accounts, which accepted the same day. No SCPs yet: that's Chapter 20. He enabled IAM Identity Center in Frankfurt with its own directory, required MFA at every sign-in, and allowed only security keys and passkeys.

The permission sets and groups are Table 12.2's. Before go-live, he ran the lab's assignment check: it found one assignment to an individual, Dee's PlatformAdmin in staging, left over from setting up her team's sandbox, which became a time-limited request instead (Chapter 14). It also flagged the platform team's standing PlatformAdmin in production, which went on Chapter 14's list. The guardrail's exceptions were rewritten for Identity Center's role names (12.7) and tested.

Engineers moved over in the week of 11 January. Their IAM users were disabled on 18 January and deleted on 1 February, with ops, the prod-operator role and its authenticator-app MFA devices. The security keys from November finally work for everything. The only IAM user left in either account is deploy-bot, which isn't a person, and whose turn comes in Chapter 17.

12.11Lab: read the access matrix

Lab 12 · Assignments to access, and a real sign-in

You'll turn Larder's groups, permission sets and assignments into a per-person view and find the two kinds of risky assignment; then, in a sandbox organisation, create a permission set, a group and an assignment and sign in to the CLI with them. You need Python 3; for steps 3–6, an AWS Organization sandbox with IAM Identity Center enabled (Appendix C), the AWS CLI v2, and your own Identity Center user. Cost: free.

assignments.pyPython 3.8+

# assignments.py · who can do what, in which account, through IAM Identity Center
ACCOUNTS = {"777788889999": "management", "111122223333": "prod", "444455556666": "dev-staging"}

PERMISSION_SETS = {                       # name: (policies, session hours)
    "ViewOnly":      (["ViewOnlyAccess"], 8),
    "Developer":     (["PowerUserAccess", "boundary: team-boundary"], 8),
    "ProdOperator":  (["prod-operator"], 1),
    "DbChange":      (["db-change"], 1),
    "PlatformAdmin": (["AdministratorAccess"], 1),
    "Billing":       (["Billing"], 8),
}

GROUPS = {
    "engineers": {"ben", "cho", "dee", "eli"},
    "on-call":   {"ben", "cho", "eli"},
    "db-owners": {"ben", "eli"},
    "platform":  {"eli"},
    "finance":   {"marco"},
}

ASSIGNMENTS = [                           # (principal, permission set, account)
    ("group:engineers", "ViewOnly",      "111122223333"),
    ("group:engineers", "Developer",     "444455556666"),
    ("group:on-call",   "ProdOperator",  "111122223333"),
    ("group:db-owners", "DbChange",      "111122223333"),
    ("group:platform",  "PlatformAdmin", "777788889999"),
    ("group:platform",  "PlatformAdmin", "111122223333"),
    ("group:platform",  "PlatformAdmin", "444455556666"),
    ("group:finance",   "Billing",       "777788889999"),
    ("user:dee",        "PlatformAdmin", "444455556666"),   # left over from setting up staging
]

def members(principal):
    kind, name = principal.split(":")
    return GROUPS[name] if kind == "group" else {name}

def effective_access():
    access = {}
    for principal, pset, account in ASSIGNMENTS:
        for person in members(principal):
            access.setdefault(person, {}).setdefault(ACCOUNTS[account], set()).add(pset)
    return access

def findings():
    for principal, pset, account in ASSIGNMENTS:
        if principal.startswith("user:"):
            yield f"direct assignment: {principal} → {pset} in {ACCOUNTS[account]} (use a group)"
        if "AdministratorAccess" in PERMISSION_SETS[pset][0] and ACCOUNTS[account] == "prod":
            yield f"standing admin in prod: {principal} → {pset} (make it just-in-time, Chapter 14)"

if __name__ == "__main__":
    for person, accounts in sorted(effective_access().items()):
        line = "  ".join(f"{acct}: {', '.join(sorted(sets))}" for acct, sets in sorted(accounts.items()))
        print(f"{person:<6} {line}")
    print()
    for f in findings():
        print("!", f)
  1. Run it with python3 assignments.py: 
    ben    dev-staging: Developer  prod: DbChange, ProdOperator, ViewOnly
cho    dev-staging: Developer  prod: ProdOperator, ViewOnly
dee    dev-staging: Developer, PlatformAdmin  prod: ViewOnly
eli    dev-staging: Developer, PlatformAdmin  management: PlatformAdmin  prod: DbChange, PlatformAdmin, ProdOperator, ViewOnly
marco  management: Billing

! standing admin in prod: group:platform → PlatformAdmin (make it just-in-time, Chapter 14)
! direct assignment: user:dee → PlatformAdmin in dev-staging (use a group)
    Nine assignments become one line per person. Eli's line is the one to worry about: four ways into production, one of them unlimited.
  2. Make a change and review it. Move Cho from on-call to db-owners (a “mover”, Chapter 13) and run again: one membership change updates every account at once. Then add a fourth account, "555566667777": "data", and give engineers ViewOnly there.
  3. In your sandbox, find the instance (as an administrator of the management or delegated administrator account): 
    INSTANCE=$(aws sso-admin list-instances --query 'Instances[0].InstanceArn' --output text)
STORE=$(aws sso-admin list-instances --query 'Instances[0].IdentityStoreId' --output text)
ACCOUNT=<a member account ID>
  4. Create a permission set, a group and an assignment. 
    PS=$(aws sso-admin create-permission-set --instance-arn $INSTANCE --name LabViewOnly \
  --session-duration PT1H --query PermissionSet.PermissionSetArn --output text)
aws sso-admin attach-managed-policy-to-permission-set --instance-arn $INSTANCE \
  --permission-set-arn $PS --managed-policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/job-function/ViewOnlyAccess
G=$(aws identitystore create-group --identity-store-id $STORE --display-name lab-viewers \
  --query GroupId --output text)
ME=$(aws identitystore get-user-id --identity-store-id $STORE --alternate-identifier \
  '{"UniqueAttribute": {"AttributePath": "userName", "AttributeValue": "YOUR-USER-NAME"}}' \
  --query UserId --output text)
aws identitystore create-group-membership --identity-store-id $STORE --group-id $G --member-id UserId=$ME
aws sso-admin create-account-assignment --instance-arn $INSTANCE --target-id $ACCOUNT \
  --target-type AWS_ACCOUNT --permission-set-arn $PS --principal-type GROUP --principal-id $G
    Within a minute, the access portal shows the new account and LabViewOnly.
  5. Sign in from the CLI. Run aws configure sso, choose the account and LabViewOnly, name the profile lab-view, then: 
    aws sts get-caller-identity --profile lab-view --query Arn --output text
aws s3 ls --profile lab-view                                     # works: ViewOnly can list buckets
aws s3 cp s3://SOME-BUCKET/SOME-KEY - --profile lab-view         # denied: it can't read data
    The ARN ends in assumed-role/AWSReservedSSO_LabViewOnly_…/your user name. As an administrator in the member account, aws iam list-roles --path-prefix /aws-reserved/sso.amazonaws.com/ --query 'Roles[].Arn' shows the role Identity Center created.
  6. Clean up: aws sso-admin delete-account-assignment with the same arguments as the create, then aws sso-admin delete-permission-set --instance-arn $INSTANCE --permission-set-arn $PS, then aws identitystore delete-group --identity-store-id $STORE --group-id $G. The role in the member account disappears with the assignment.

12.12Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Keeping IAM users “just in case” after SSOLong-lived credentials and a second way in nobody reviews.Delete them; keep only designed break-glass access (Chapter 14).
An existing workload account as the management accountA workload compromise controls the organisation; SCPs can't restrict it.A new, empty management account.
Assigning permission sets to individualsExceptions pile up unseen.Groups only; review the matrix.
ReadOnlyAccess for everyoneEveryone can read all data and secrets' metadata.ViewOnlyAccess, plus narrow data access where needed.
Long sessions for powerful permission setsStolen or forgotten sessions stay useful for hours.One hour for admin and production change; longer for viewing.
Guardrails naming exact SSO role namesExceptions break when permission sets are recreated.Match AWSReservedSSO_Name_* with wildcards, and test.

✓Chapter summary

  • IAM Identity Center gives people one sign-in, strong MFA and short role sessions in every account; it replaces IAM users for humans.
  • Account access needs an organisation instance; create the organisation from a new, empty management account.
  • People come from an identity source: Identity Center's directory, AD, or an external IdP with SAML and SCIM.
  • A permission set describes a job; an account assignment gives it to a group in an account; Identity Center creates AWSReservedSSO_… roles.
  • Assign to groups, never individuals; review the resulting matrix.
  • The console and CLI share one sign-in; CloudTrail shows role and person; guardrails must match SSO role names with wildcards.

Key terms

IAM Identity Center
AWS's workforce sign-in service for accounts and applications.
Organisation instance
An Identity Center instance in an organisation, able to grant AWS account access.
Identity source
Where Identity Center's users and groups come from.
Permission set
A template of policies and session length that becomes a role in each assigned account.
Account assignment
A user or group, a permission set and an account.
AWS access portal
The page where people sign in and choose an account and permission set.
Attributes for access control
Identity attributes passed into sessions as tags, for ABAC.

Check your understanding

  1. What three things does an account assignment connect, and what does Identity Center create as a result? recall

    Show answer

    A user or group, a permission set and an AWS account. It creates (and maintains) an IAM role named AWSReservedSSO_PermissionSet_suffix in that account, trusted only by Identity Center.

  2. Why does single sign-on for AWS accounts usually start with creating an organisation, and why from a new account? explain

    Show answer

    Only an organisation instance of Identity Center can grant access to AWS accounts. The management account can't be restricted by SCPs and controls all members, so it should hold no workloads; a new, empty account is the simplest way to guarantee that.

  3. An engineer needs to read one bucket's objects in production for a week. How do you grant it without changing ViewOnly for everyone? apply

    Show answer

    Create (or reuse) a narrow permission set for that bucket, assign it to a small group in production, add the engineer to the group with an end date, and remove them after the week, ideally through the time-limited process of Chapter 14.

  4. After moving to Identity Center, a guardrail's exception for role/db-change no longer lets the database owners make changes. Why, and what's the fix? apply

    Show answer

    Their sessions now come from AWSReservedSSO_DbChange_suffix under the /aws-reserved/sso.amazonaws.com/ path. Change the exception to match that pattern with wildcards, and test it with the policy simulator or a real session.

  5. Design the permission sets and groups for a company with three product teams, a platform team, finance and support, and four accounts. design

    Show answer

    Roughly: ViewOnly for all engineers in production; Developer (with a boundary) for each team in development and staging; ProdOperator for on-call, time-limited; PlatformAdmin for platform, time-limited in production; Billing for finance in the management account; support gets application tools, not AWS access. All assigned to groups, ideally with a team attribute for ABAC.

  6. Compare how a person gets admin rights in one subscription on Azure with one account on AWS. compare

    Show answer

    Azure: their Entra ID group gets a role assignment (such as Owner or Contributor) at the subscription scope; they use their own identity. AWS: their group gets an account assignment of an admin permission set; they sign in through the portal and receive a session of the role Identity Center created in that account.

Going further

  • AWS: What is IAM Identity Center?, permission sets, CLI with IAM Identity Center, attributes for access control.
  • Azure: Azure RBAC best practices. Google Cloud: Workforce identity federation.

Part III · Access for people

13

Joiners, movers and leavers

Access is easy to grant and easy to forget. People join and need the right tools on day one; they change teams and quietly keep their old access; they leave and, in too many companies, stay in a few systems for weeks. This chapter connects the HR system, the identity provider and every app so that access follows the person automatically, and sets up the reviews that catch whatever automation misses.

By the end you can

  • Describe the joiner–mover–leaver lifecycle, with HR as the source of truth.
  • Design birthright access from department and role, and requests for everything else.
  • Explain SCIM provisioning and connect an identity provider to IAM Identity Center.
  • Run a leaver process that ends sessions, not just accounts.
  • Run access reviews that people actually complete.

Before you start

  • Single sign-on with Identity Center (Chapter 12); SAML and just-in-time accounts (Chapter 5).
  • Sessions and revocation (Chapter 3); privilege creep (Chapter 6).

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, Python 3, no cloud account
Main provider
provider-neutral and AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 13.8

At Larder

In May 2027, Lena, Larder's data protection officer, reviews who can see customer data. Most of the answers are reassuring. One isn't: Pim, a customer support agent who left on 30 April, could still sign in to the helpdesk and to the billing tool, where support agents issue refunds, on 21 May. Three weeks, with access to customers' order histories and the power to send money.

AWS wasn't the problem: since January, Larder's engineers sign in through Identity Center (Chapter 12), and Eli removes leavers the same day. But Larder has 14 staff tools, and each has its own accounts, created and deleted by whoever remembers. Pim's manager had told IT. IT had closed the email account. Nobody owned the other twelve. Section 13.9 shows what Larder changed.

13.1The lifecycle

Every person's access goes through three kinds of event: they join, they move (change team, role, location or manager), and they leave. The aim of lifecycle management is that each event changes access everywhere, automatically, from one place. That place should be where the event is first recorded: the HR system, which knows about a new hire before IT does, and about a resignation before anyone touches an account.

Two different protocols are involved, and both are needed. SAML or OpenID Connect handles sign-in: the app trusts the IdP to say who's there (Chapter 4, Chapter 5). SCIM handles provisioning: the IdP tells the app who exists, which groups they're in, and when they're gone (13.5). Sign-in alone stops a leaver logging in again; it doesn't remove their account, their group memberships or their existing sessions.

13.2Joiners: birthright access, and requests for the rest

Split access into two kinds:

  • Birthright access: what everyone in a department or role needs from day one, derived automatically from HR attributes. Every support agent gets the helpdesk and the billing tool's refund role; every engineer gets ViewOnly in production and Developer in staging (Chapter 12). Rules, not decisions.
  • Requested access: anything beyond that, asked for by the person, approved by an owner, and ideally time-limited (Chapter 14).

Keep birthright access small. Every item on it is given to everyone, forever, without anyone deciding. Day-one readiness then comes from HR entering the start date early: the account exists, with the right groups, before the person arrives.

13.3Movers: the forgotten event

Joiners get attention because they can't work without access. Leavers get attention because auditors ask. Movers get neither, so they're where privilege creep comes from (Chapter 6): someone moves from support to operations, gains the stock tool, and keeps the refund role. Nobody complains, because extra access never stops anyone working.

With birthright access derived from attributes, a move is just an attribute change: the old department's groups go, the new one's arrive. Allow a short, deliberate overlap if handovers need it (a week, with an end date), never an open one. Requested access should be re-confirmed on a move, by the new manager.

13.4Leavers: ending sessions, not just accounts

Disabling someone at the identity provider stops new sign-ins everywhere it's used. It does not immediately stop everything they already have:

  • Sessions already open in each app last until they expire: a browser session, a refresh token on a phone (Chapter 3), an Identity Center role session of up to its permission set's duration.
  • Accounts in apps without SCIM stay active. Pim's helpdesk and billing accounts were exactly this.
  • Secrets the person knew or made: shared passwords, API keys they created, keys on their laptop.
  • Things they owned: scheduled jobs, resources, the only copy of a runbook.
  1. HR records the leave date; the IdP disables the account at the end of the last day, automatically.
  2. SCIM deactivates the person in every connected app within minutes.
  3. Revoke active sessions where it matters: in the IdP, in apps that support it, and in Identity Center, whose console lists a user's sessions and can end them. That stops the access portal from issuing new credentials; role credentials it already issued still work until they expire. For an urgent case, AWS's documented method is a deny-everything statement in your permission sets (or an SCP) with a condition on the person's identitystore:UserId, which takes effect for sessions already running.
  4. Tickets for the rest: each app without SCIM gets a task, owned by that app's owner, due the same day.
  5. Rotate what they knew, transfer what they owned, recover devices.
  6. Verify the next day: a report of the person's status in every app. Anything still active is an incident.

Watch out: a hostile departure

When someone leaves on bad terms, the order flips: disable and revoke sessions first, at the moment they're told, then do the rest. Short session durations for powerful access (Chapter 12) are what make this fast.

13.5SCIM

SCIM (System for Cross-domain Identity Management, RFC 7643 and 7644) is a small REST API that apps expose so an identity provider can manage their users and groups. The IdP calls it whenever something changes:

  • POST /Users creates a user; POST /Groups a group.
  • PATCH /Users/{id} changes attributes, including deactivation; PATCH /Groups/{id} adds or removes members.
  • DELETE removes a user or group entirely; many IdPs deactivate first and delete later.

deactivating a leaverSCIM 2.0 request

PATCH /scim/v2/Users/9067729b3d-94f1e0b3-c394-48d5-8ab1-2c122a167074
Authorization: Bearer <SCIM access token>
Content-Type: application/scim+json

{ "schemas": ["urn:ietf:params:scim:api:messages:2.0:PatchOp"],
  "Operations": [ { "op": "replace", "path": "active", "value": false } ] }

To connect an IdP to IAM Identity Center, you change Identity Center's identity source to external identity provider, exchange SAML metadata (Chapter 5), and turn on automatic provisioning. Identity Center then gives you a SCIM endpoint and an access token for the IdP to use. Three things to know:

  • The SCIM token expires after a year. When it does, provisioning stops silently: new joiners get nothing and, worse, leavers aren't removed. Put the date in a calendar and rotate it early.
  • Provision groups, not just users, and keep assignments group-based (Chapter 12): then HR changes flow all the way through to AWS accounts.
  • Usernames must match when switching from Identity Center's own directory, so existing users are taken over rather than duplicated.
Table 13.1 · Getting accounts into an app
ManualJust-in-time (Chapter 5)SCIM
Account createdwhen someone remembersat first sign-inwhen HR says so, before day one
Groups kept currentrarelyat each sign-incontinuously
Leaver removedwhen someone remembersnever: they just stop signing inwithin minutes
Use fornothing importantlow-risk apps, partners' userseverything that supports it

13.6Access reviews

Automation handles the rules. Reviews catch what the rules don't: requested access that outlived its reason, an assignment made by hand, a group that grew. An access review asks the person who knows, usually the manager or the resource owner, to confirm or remove each item, and records the answer.

Key idea

Automate what follows from HR; review what doesn't. A review that lists everyone's birthright access teaches people to click “approve all”.

Reviews people actually complete are short and pointed:

  • Review exceptions, not rules. Show requested and hand-made access, not what every support agent has.
  • Privileged access more often: monthly for production admin, quarterly for the rest.
  • Show usage. “Last used 140 days ago” makes the decision for the reviewer. IAM Access Analyzer's unused access findings and Identity Center's sign-in records give this for AWS (Chapter 23).
  • Default to removal when a reviewer doesn't answer, after a reminder. Unanswered reviews that keep access teach everyone that reviews don't matter.
Machines leave tooService accounts, API keys and roles have human owners. When an owner leaves or moves, ownership must move with the team, or the identity becomes an orphan nobody dares delete. Tag every workload identity with an owning team, not a person.

13.7Partners' people

The same lifecycle applies to people from customers and suppliers who use your systems. You can't see their HR system, so let their identity provider drive: federation for sign-in (Chapter 5) and, where they support it, SCIM from their IdP into yours, so their leavers disappear from your app when they leave their company. Where they don't, keep their sessions short and run an access review with the partner's own contact.

13.8Across providers

Table 13.2 · Lifecycle tooling at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
HR-driven accountsnone built in: from your IdPEntra ID inbound provisioning from HR systems; lifecycle workflows for joiner, mover, leaver tasksfrom your IdP or directory sync (Google Cloud Directory Sync)
Provisioning into AWS / the cloudIdentity Center as a SCIM targetnative (Entra ID is the directory)native (Cloud Identity) or SCIM from your IdP
Provisioning out to SaaS appsIdentity Center isn't a SCIM source; use your IdPEntra ID app provisioning (SCIM)Workspace / Cloud Identity auto-provisioning for SAML apps
Access reviewsIAM Access Analyzer unused access; reviews in your IdPEntra ID Governance access reviewsIAM Recommender and Policy Intelligence; reviews in your IdP

On Azure

Entra ID can take hires and leavers straight from HR systems (inbound provisioning), run lifecycle workflows (send a welcome, add to groups before day one, remove access on the last day) and schedule access reviews of groups, apps and privileged roles, with automatic removal when reviewers don't respond. These features are part of Entra ID Governance licensing.

On Google Cloud

If your source of truth is elsewhere (Entra ID, Okta, an LDAP directory), users and groups reach Cloud Identity by SCIM from the IdP or by Google Cloud Directory Sync; suspended users lose access to the console and APIs. The IAM role recommender suggests removing permissions a principal hasn't used in 90 days, which is good evidence for reviews.

13.9At Larder: one identity per person

At Larder: solved

Lena's finding became ADR 0021, accepted on 24 May 2027: one identity per person. Larder's company directory, which until then had mostly run email, became its identity provider for every staff tool, fed by the HR system. Departments and roles in HR now decide birthright groups. Eleven of the 14 tools support SAML or OpenID Connect plus SCIM, and are connected; the other three get an automatic leaver ticket, owned by each tool's owner, due the same day. Pim's two accounts were closed the afternoon Lena found them, and the billing tool's log showed they hadn't been used.

On 7 June, Eli switched Identity Center's identity source from its own directory to the IdP, with SCIM; usernames matched, so nobody noticed except the new joiners, who had AWS access on their first morning. The SCIM token's expiry is in the platform team's calendar. Managers now review requested and privileged access every quarter, and production admin monthly. Two more changes followed from the same decision: business customers' staff are federated from their own IdPs (as Korenwolf's office managers have been since December, Chapter 5), and pickers get personal badges by July instead of shared logins on store tablets.

13.10Lab: reconcile apps with HR

Lab 13 · What SCIM provisioning would do

You'll compare what HR says with what four apps contain, and produce the SCIM requests that would fix every difference: a joiner, a leaver, a mover and a group granted by hand. You need Python 3. Cost: nothing.

jml.pyPython 3.8+

# jml.py · joiners, movers and leavers: reconcile apps with HR, the way SCIM provisioning does
from datetime import date

TODAY = date(2027, 5, 21)

HR = {   # the source of truth: who works here, in which department, until when
    "ben":   {"dept": "engineering", "left": None},
    "noor":  {"dept": "support",     "left": None},                 # joined 17 May
    "pim":   {"dept": "support",     "left": date(2027, 4, 30)},    # left
    "tomas": {"dept": "operations",  "left": None},
    "yara":  {"dept": "operations",  "left": None},                 # moved from support on 3 May
}

RULES = {  # department → the groups each app should give them
    "engineering": {"aws": {"engineers"}},
    "support":     {"helpdesk": {"agents"}, "billing": {"refunds"}},
    "operations":  {"stock-tool": {"store-staff"}},
}

APPS = {   # what each app has today: user → groups
    "aws":        {"ben": {"engineers"}},
    "helpdesk":   {"pim": {"agents"}, "yara": {"agents"}},
    "billing":    {"pim": {"refunds"}, "yara": {"refunds"}},
    "stock-tool": {"tomas": {"store-staff", "rota-admin"}},     # rota-admin: granted by hand in March
}

def desired(app):
    want = {}
    for user, rec in HR.items():
        if rec["left"] is None or rec["left"] > TODAY:
            groups = RULES.get(rec["dept"], {}).get(app)
            if groups:
                want[user] = groups
    return want

def reconcile(app):
    have, want = APPS[app], desired(app)
    for user in sorted(want.keys() - have.keys()):
        yield f"POST   /Users                  {app}: create {user}@larder.example, groups {sorted(want[user])}"
    for user in sorted(have.keys() - want.keys()):
        rec = HR.get(user)
        why = (f"left {rec['left']}, {(TODAY - rec['left']).days} days ago" if rec and rec["left"]
               else "moved" if rec else "not in HR")
        yield f"PATCH  /Users/{user:<17} {app}: active=false  ({why})"
    for user in sorted(want.keys() & have.keys()):
        for g in sorted(want[user] - have[user]):
            yield f"PATCH  /Groups/{g:<16} {app}: add {user}"
        for g in sorted(have[user] - want[user]):
            yield f"PATCH  /Groups/{g:<16} {app}: remove {user}"

for app in APPS:
    for action in reconcile(app):
        print(action)
  1. Predict, then run python3 jml.py: 
    POST   /Users                  helpdesk: create noor@larder.example, groups ['agents']
PATCH  /Users/pim               helpdesk: active=false  (left 2027-04-30, 21 days ago)
PATCH  /Users/yara              helpdesk: active=false  (moved)
POST   /Users                  billing: create noor@larder.example, groups ['refunds']
PATCH  /Users/pim               billing: active=false  (left 2027-04-30, 21 days ago)
PATCH  /Users/yara              billing: active=false  (moved)
POST   /Users                  stock-tool: create yara@larder.example, groups ['store-staff']
PATCH  /Groups/rota-admin       stock-tool: remove tomas
    Noor is a joiner who has nothing yet; Pim is Lena's finding; Yara moved to operations but still holds the refund role; Tomás has a group the rules don't explain.
  2. Decide about the last line. If Tomás really needs rota-admin, it isn't creep: it's a rule nobody wrote. Add "rota-admin" for operations store managers (you'll need a role attribute in HR and a rule that uses it) and run again until only real changes remain.
  3. Break it on purpose. Set TODAY to date(2027, 4, 29): Pim is still employed, so the script wants to keep his access. Now imagine the HR system lags a week behind resignations: what would that mean for leavers? (This is why HR's leave date, entered when notice is given, is the trigger, not someone's last-day email.)

Stretch goals. (1) Make the mover overlap explicit: give HR a moved date and keep the old department's groups for seven days after it. (2) Turn each output line into the real SCIM JSON body, as in 13.5.

13.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
IT, not HR, as the triggerJoiners wait; leavers are missed when nobody tells IT.HR records drive the IdP.
Sign-in federation without provisioningLeavers' accounts and groups stay in apps.SCIM wherever supported; tickets elsewhere.
Ignoring moversPrivilege creep.Attribute-driven groups; old access removed on a move.
Disabling the account but not the sessionsLeavers keep working until sessions expire.Revoke sessions; short session lengths for powerful access.
Forgetting the SCIM token's expiryProvisioning silently stops; leavers stay.Calendar it; monitor provisioning errors.
Reviews of everything, every quarterRubber-stamping.Review exceptions and privileged access, with usage data.

✓Chapter summary

  • Access changes at three events: join, move, leave. HR, where they're first recorded, should drive them.
  • Birthright access follows from department and role; everything else is requested, approved and time-limited.
  • Movers are where privilege creep comes from; derive groups from attributes so a move removes old access.
  • A leaver process must end sessions and handle apps without SCIM, secrets and ownership, then verify.
  • SCIM lets the IdP create, change and deactivate users and groups in apps, including IAM Identity Center; its token expires.
  • Access reviews catch what rules don't; keep them short, show usage, default to removal.

Key terms

Joiner, mover, leaver
The three lifecycle events that change a person's access.
Source of truth
The system whose record decides; for people, usually HR.
Birthright access
Access granted automatically from attributes such as department.
Provisioning
Creating, updating and removing accounts and group memberships in apps.
SCIM
A standard REST API for provisioning users and groups.
Deprovisioning
Deactivating or deleting a person's accounts and access.
Access review
A periodic confirmation of who has access, by someone who knows.

Check your understanding

  1. What's the difference between what SAML does and what SCIM does for an app? recall

    Show answer

    SAML (or OIDC) signs people in: the app trusts the IdP to say who's there. SCIM provisions: the IdP creates, updates and deactivates the person's account and group memberships in the app.

  2. An engineer is disabled in the IdP at 17:00. They have an Identity Center PlatformAdmin session from 16:45, with a one-hour duration. When does their AWS access end, and how could you end it sooner? apply

    Show answer

    The role session's credentials work until about 17:45. Sooner: end the user's sessions in Identity Center (so no new credentials are issued), and add a deny statement conditioned on their identitystore:UserId to the permission sets or an SCP, which blocks the credentials already issued. The AWSReservedSSO_ role itself can't be edited.

  3. Why are movers more dangerous than joiners for least privilege? explain

    Show answer

    Joiners complain when access is missing; movers never complain about access they no longer need. Without automatic removal on a move, old access accumulates silently.

  4. Your access review shows 400 items per manager and everyone clicks “approve all”. Redesign it. design

    Show answer

    Remove birthright access from the review (it's governed by rules); show only requested, hand-made and privileged access; add last-used dates; review privileged access monthly and the rest quarterly; remove access that isn't confirmed after a reminder.

  5. Identity Center provisioning stopped a month ago and nobody noticed. What probably happened, and what would have caught it? apply

    Show answer

    The SCIM access token expired after a year. A calendar reminder before expiry, alerts on provisioning errors in the IdP, and a daily comparison of HR leavers with Identity Center's active users.

  6. Compare how a leaver loses access to Azure and to AWS when the company's IdP is Entra ID. compare

    Show answer

    Azure: disabling the Entra ID account stops sign-in to the portal and every subscription at once; refresh tokens can be revoked, and Continuous Access Evaluation cuts many sessions quickly. AWS: Entra ID's SCIM deactivates the user in Identity Center, which stops new sign-ins; existing role sessions run until they expire unless revoked.

Going further

  • RFC 7644, SCIM Protocol and RFC 7643, SCIM Core Schema.
  • AWS: Automatic provisioning in IAM Identity Center and sessions in Identity Center.
  • Azure: Lifecycle workflows and access reviews.

Part III · Access for people

14

Privileged access and break-glass

The most dangerous permissions in any company are also the ones its best people need, at the worst moments: during an outage, at night, in a hurry. This chapter shows how to give admin and production-change rights only when they're needed, for as long as they're needed, with someone else's say-so; and how to keep an emergency way in that still works when single sign-on itself is broken.

By the end you can

  • Explain standing versus just-in-time access, and why the difference is about exposure, not trust.
  • Design an elevation process: eligibility, approval, duration, incident rules and review.
  • Implement time-limited elevation on AWS with IAM Identity Center.
  • Design, store, monitor and test break-glass access.

Before you start

  • Identity Center and permission sets (Chapter 12); guardrails (Chapter 10); the root user (Chapter 7).

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
30 minutes, Python 3; optional AWS step, pennies
Main provider
AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 14.7

At Larder

On Friday 18 December 2026, at 18:05, in the middle of the Christmas peak, the orders database failed over and checkout was down for 38 minutes. Cho was on call. He assumed prod-operator with an authenticator code from a phone at 4 % battery, and watched the failover complete on its own.

The post-incident review on 21 December found nothing wrong with access that night, and a lot that could have been. Had the database needed a change, only the db-change role could make one (Chapter 10), and both of its owners were unreachable: Ben on a plane, Eli at the office Christmas dinner without a laptop. Had Cho's phone died, nobody else on the rota was awake. And the break-glass-* roles in the guardrail's exception didn't exist yet. Section 14.8 shows what Larder built.

14.1Standing access and just-in-time access

Standing access is privilege you hold all the time, whether you're using it or not. Just-in-time (JIT) access is privilege you're eligible for, but activate only when needed, for a limited time, usually with a reason and an approval. The difference isn't about trusting people less. It's about exposure: how much of the year a powerful credential exists that a phishing page, a stolen laptop or a malicious browser extension could use.

Key idea

Nobody should hold production admin rights while they sleep. Be eligible, not permanently entitled.

14.2What counts as privileged

Privileged access is anything that could cause serious, hard-to-reverse harm, or that could be used to grant more access. In an AWS estate, that's at least:

  • Administrator access in any account, and anything at all in the management account.
  • Changing production: deployments outside the pipeline, database changes, network and firewall rules.
  • IAM and Identity Center: creating roles, editing policies, changing assignments, iam:PassRole on powerful roles (Chapter 8).
  • Reading sensitive data in bulk: customer exports, secrets, backups.
  • Security tooling and logs: anything that could switch off CloudTrail or an alarm.

Everything else, viewing configuration, working in development, reading logs, can usually be standing access through birthright groups (Chapter 13).

14.3Designing elevation

An elevation process answers five questions for each privileged permission set:

Table 14.1 · Larder's elevation rules
Permission setWho may askWho approvesLongest grantIn an incident
ProdOperatoron-call rotaany tech lead1 hon-call self-approves, citing the incident; reviewed next morning
DbChangeon-call rota and database ownersa database owner (not yourself)1 hthe incident lead may approve
PlatformAdmin (prod)platform team and tech leadsplatform team (not yourself)2 hbreak-glass if nobody can approve (14.5)
PlatformAdmin (dev-staging)any engineertheir tech lead4 hn/a

Principles behind the table:

  • Never approve your own request, except in a declared incident, and then always with review afterwards. An approval nobody can bypass at 3 a.m. becomes a reason to keep standing access.
  • A reason, every time. “INC-412: checkout errors” or a ticket number. It's what the reviewer reads, and what an auditor asks for.
  • Short by default. An hour, renewable. Long grants become standing access with extra steps.
  • Everyone on call can elevate. Larder's near miss came from too few people being able to act, not too many.

On AWS

Identity Center has no built-in “eligible” state, so elevation is built from what it has: account assignments can be created and deleted by API. An elevation tool creates the assignment when a request is approved and deletes it when the time is up, logging both. AWS publishes an open-source solution that does exactly this, TEAM (Temporary Elevated Access Management for IAM Identity Center); identity providers offer the same idea one level up, with time-limited group memberships that SCIM then pushes into Identity Center. Either way:

  • Keep the permission set's session duration no longer than the grant, so a session can't outlive its approval by much.
  • Remember that deleting the assignment stops new sessions; a session already started runs to its end (Chapter 13, 13.4).
  • Run the tool in a locked-down account of its own: whoever controls it can grant anything.

14.4Two people for the worst actions

A few actions are too dangerous even for a well-approved individual session: changing SCPs, editing Identity Center assignments wholesale, deleting a whole account, turning off organisation-wide logging. For these, require two people at the moment of action, not just at approval: changes made through code with a mandatory second reviewer (Chapter 24), and an explicit deny for everyone else (Chapter 10). Deleting backups, at Larder, is denied to everyone.

14.5Break-glass

Break-glass access is a way in that doesn't depend on the things that usually let people in: the identity provider, Identity Center, a phone, the approval tool. It exists for the day those fail, or are misconfigured, or are the very thing under attack. It should be strong, rare, loud and tested.

A design that works for most AWS estates:

  1. Two IAM users, break-glass-1 and break-glass-2, in the management account: console passwords, no access keys, each with two hardware security keys. These are the only IAM users for people in the whole organisation.
  2. A role in every account, break-glass-admin, with administrator permissions, trusting only those two users and only with MFA (Chapter 8). Guardrails list it as an exception (Chapter 10).
  3. Stored apart. Each password is sealed in an envelope in the office safe, and its security keys are in a second place, so using break-glass takes two people.
  4. Loud. Any sign-in by those users, and any assumption of break-glass-admin, pages every tech lead and posts to the incident channel (the lab builds this alarm).
  5. Tested every quarter. Two people follow the runbook, sign in, switch role into production, make a harmless change, check the alarm fired, then change the password and reseal it. An untested break-glass procedure fails on the day you need it.

Watch out: break-glass that depends on what broke

Break-glass accounts federated through the same IdP, protected by MFA on the same phones, or alerting through the same SSO-protected chat tool fail together with the everyday path. Check every dependency, including where the runbook itself is stored.

14.6Watching privileged sessions

  • Every elevation produces a record: who, which permission set, why, approved by whom, from when to when. Review self-approved incident elevations the next working day.
  • CloudTrail ties actions to the session (Chapter 12, 12.7), so “what did Cho do between 18:07 and 19:07?” is one query.
  • Shell access to servers goes through AWS Systems Manager Session Manager rather than SSH keys: sessions are authorised by IAM and can be logged, keystrokes and all, to S3 or CloudWatch Logs.
  • Alerts on the rare things: break-glass use, root sign-in (Chapter 7), changes to Identity Center assignments outside the elevation tool (Chapter 23).

14.7Across providers

Table 14.2 · Just-in-time and emergency access at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Just-in-time elevationtemporary account assignments (TEAM, or time-limited IdP groups)Privileged Identity Management: eligible roles, activation with MFA, justification, approval, maximum durationPrivileged Access Manager: entitlements with approval and maximum duration
Emergency accessbreak-glass IAM users and roles; the root user as a last resorttwo cloud-only emergency access accounts, excluded from Conditional Access policies that could lock them outa backup super administrator; break-glass accounts outside federation
Session controlSession Manager for shellsBastion, PIM activation historyIAP for SSH/RDP; audit logs

On Azure

Privileged Identity Management is built in: assign people as eligible for a role (on Entra ID or on Azure resources), and they activate it for a set time, with MFA, a justification and optionally an approver.
