PIM for groups extends the same to group membership. PIM also schedules access reviews of who is eligible.

On Google Cloud

Privileged Access Manager defines entitlements: which roles, on which resource, for whom, for how long, with which approvers. Principals request a grant, it's approved or not, and the binding is added and later removed automatically, with every step in the audit logs.

14.8At Larder: eligible, not entitled

At Larder: solved

With single sign-on in place (Chapter 12), Eli spent February 2027 on the review's three actions. First, no more standing production privilege: ProdOperator, DbChange and production PlatformAdmin stopped being permanently assigned, and became requests through TEAM, deployed in a small tooling account, with the rules in Table 14.1. Everyone on the rota can now get DbChange during an incident with the incident lead's approval, which closes the gap from 18 December. Dee's leftover staging admin became a four-hour request.

Second, break-glass, as in 14.5: two IAM users in larder-management, a break-glass-admin role in all three accounts, envelopes in the office safe and security keys in a locked drawer in Marco's office, and an alarm that pages every tech lead.

The new process had its first real use on 18 February: a payments incident (INC-412) at 18:07, Cho on call again. He self-approved ProdOperator in one click, and Eli approved a DbChange request from his phone three minutes later; the review the next morning took two minutes. Third, practice: the first break-glass test, on 26 February, took 19 minutes and found one problem: the runbook lived in the wiki, which signs in through the IdP. A printed copy now lives in the safe with the envelopes.

14.9Lab: an elevation broker, and a break-glass alarm

Lab 14 · Just-in-time rules in code; an alarm in AWS

You'll run a small elevation broker with Larder's rules on the evening of INC-412, then, optionally, build the alarm that fires when a break-glass user signs in. You need Python 3; for the AWS part, the AWS CLI v2 as an administrator of a sandbox account that has a CloudTrail trail recording management events (Appendix C). Cost: the Python part is free; the alarm costs nothing at this volume beyond the trail's S3 storage, which is pennies. The broker ignores accounts to stay short.

jit.pyPython 3.8+

# jit.py · just-in-time elevation: request, approve, grant for a while, expire, and log it all
from datetime import datetime, timedelta

GROUPS = {"on-call": {"ben", "cho", "eli"}, "db-owners": {"ben", "eli"},
          "platform": {"eli"}, "tech-leads": {"ben", "cho", "dee", "eli"}}

RULES = {   # permission set → who may ask, who may approve, longest grant
    "ProdOperator":  {"eligible": "on-call",    "approvers": "tech-leads", "max_min": 60,  "on_call_self": True},
    "DbChange":      {"eligible": "on-call",    "approvers": "db-owners",  "max_min": 60,  "on_call_self": False},
    "PlatformAdmin": {"eligible": "tech-leads", "approvers": "platform",   "max_min": 120, "on_call_self": False},
}

LOG, GRANTS, PENDING = [], [], {}

def log(at, text):
    LOG.append(f"{at:%H:%M}  {text}")

def request(rid, who, pset, minutes, reason, at, incident=None):
    rule = RULES[pset]
    if who not in GROUPS[rule["eligible"]]:
        return log(at, f"{rid} REFUSED  {who} isn't eligible for {pset}")
    minutes = min(minutes, rule["max_min"])
    if incident and rule["on_call_self"]:
        return grant(rid, who, pset, minutes, at, f"self-approved during {incident}, review tomorrow")
    PENDING[rid] = (who, pset, minutes, reason)
    log(at, f"{rid} PENDING  {who} asks for {pset} {minutes} min: {reason}")

def approve(rid, approver, at):
    who, pset, minutes, _ = PENDING[rid]
    if approver == who:
        return log(at, f"{rid} REFUSED  {approver} can't approve their own request")
    if approver not in GROUPS[RULES[pset]["approvers"]]:
        return log(at, f"{rid} REFUSED  {approver} isn't an approver for {pset}")
    del PENDING[rid]
    grant(rid, who, pset, minutes, at, f"approved by {approver}")

def grant(rid, who, pset, minutes, at, how):
    GRANTS.append((who, pset, at, at + timedelta(minutes=minutes)))
    log(at, f"{rid} GRANTED  {who} → {pset} until {at + timedelta(minutes=minutes):%H:%M} ({how})")

def has(who, pset, at):
    return any(w == who and p == pset and start <= at < end for w, p, start, end in GRANTS)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    t = lambda hhmm: datetime(2027, 2, 18, *map(int, hhmm.split(":")))
    request("R1", "cho", "ProdOperator", 60, "checkout errors", t("18:07"), incident="INC-412")
    request("R2", "cho", "DbChange", 90, "raise max_connections", t("18:12"))
    approve("R2", "cho", t("18:13"))
    approve("R2", "eli", t("18:15"))
    request("R3", "dee", "PlatformAdmin", 240, "set up a sandbox", t("18:20"))
    request("R4", "marco", "DbChange", 30, "curious", t("18:21"))
    print("\n".join(LOG), "\n")
    for when in ("18:30", "19:20"):
        print(f"at {when}: cho ProdOperator {has('cho', 'ProdOperator', t(when))}, "
              f"cho DbChange {has('cho', 'DbChange', t(when))}")
  1. Run it with python3 jit.py: 
    18:07  R1 GRANTED  cho → ProdOperator until 19:07 (self-approved during INC-412, review tomorrow)
18:12  R2 PENDING  cho asks for DbChange 60 min: raise max_connections
18:13  R2 REFUSED  cho can't approve their own request
18:15  R2 GRANTED  cho → DbChange until 19:15 (approved by eli)
18:20  R3 PENDING  dee asks for PlatformAdmin 120 min: set up a sandbox
18:21  R4 REFUSED  marco isn't eligible for DbChange

at 18:30: cho ProdOperator True, cho DbChange True
at 19:20: cho ProdOperator False, cho DbChange False
    Every line is something a reviewer can read: the 90 minutes asked for became 60, Dee's 240 became 120 and is still waiting, and at 19:20 nothing is left. Nobody removed anything.
  2. Change the rules. Let the incident lead approve DbChange: add an incident-leads group and accept approvers from either group. Then re-run 18 December 2026: remove Ben and Eli from every group (one on a plane, one at dinner) and see who could still have approved.
  3. Break it on purpose. Set "on_call_self": True for PlatformAdmin and add a request from Cho (a tech lead, so eligible) with incident="INC-999": anyone on call who is also a tech lead can now give themselves administrator rights by naming any incident. That's why self-approval is limited to the narrowest permission set, and reviewed.
  4. Optional: the break-glass alarm. Create an email topic and a rule that matches console sign-ins by IAM users whose names start with break-glass: 
    REGION=us-east-1      # console sign-ins usually arrive here; add the rule to your main region too
TOPIC=$(aws sns create-topic --name break-glass-alerts --region $REGION --query TopicArn --output text)
aws sns subscribe --topic-arn $TOPIC --protocol email --notification-endpoint you@example.com --region $REGION
cat > pattern.json <<'EOF'
{ "detail-type": ["AWS Console Sign In via CloudTrail"],
  "detail": { "userIdentity": { "type": ["IAMUser"], "userName": [{ "prefix": "break-glass" }] } } }
EOF
RULE=$(aws events put-rule --name break-glass-sign-in --event-pattern file://pattern.json \
  --region $REGION --query RuleArn --output text)
cat > topic-policy.json <<EOF
{ "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{ "Effect": "Allow", "Principal": { "Service": "events.amazonaws.com" },
                  "Action": "sns:Publish", "Resource": "$TOPIC",
                  "Condition": { "ArnEquals": { "aws:SourceArn": "$RULE" } } }] }
EOF
aws sns set-topic-attributes --topic-arn $TOPIC --attribute-name Policy \
  --attribute-value file://topic-policy.json --region $REGION
aws events put-targets --rule break-glass-sign-in --targets "Id=email,Arn=$TOPIC" --region $REGION
    Confirm the subscription email. Then create an IAM user break-glass-lab with a console password, sign in as it in a private browser window, and wait a few minutes for the email. If nothing arrives, find the sign-in in CloudTrail and check which region recorded it.
  5. Clean up: delete the IAM user, aws events remove-targets --rule break-glass-sign-in --ids email, aws events delete-rule --name break-glass-sign-in, and aws sns delete-topic --topic-arn $TOPIC (all with --region $REGION).

14.10Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Standing admin for “the people who need it”Months of exposure for minutes of use.Eligibility plus time-limited elevation.
Approval nobody can get at nightPeople keep standing access “for emergencies”.Incident self-approval for narrow sets, reviewed next day.
Self-approval for broad permission setsAnyone on call can make themselves admin.Self-approval only for operator-level access.
Session longer than the grantAccess outlives its approval.Permission set session duration ≤ grant length.
Break-glass through the same IdP or phonesIt fails with the thing it's meant to replace.Separate identities, hardware keys, printed runbook.
Never testing break-glassExpired passwords, lost keys, wrong runbook on the worst day.Quarterly test, with the alarm checked.

✓Chapter summary

  • Standing privilege is exposed all the time; just-in-time privilege only while it's used.
  • Privileged means admin, production change, IAM, bulk sensitive data and security tooling, and anything in the management account.
  • Elevation rules say who may ask, who approves (never yourself, except in an incident, reviewed), for how long, and why.
  • On AWS, an elevation tool creates and deletes Identity Center assignments; keep session durations short.
  • The worst actions need two people at the moment of action, through code review and explicit denies.
  • Break-glass: separate IAM users with hardware keys, a role in every account, stored apart, alarmed, and tested quarterly.

Key terms

Privileged access
Permissions that can cause serious harm or grant more access.
Standing access
Privilege held permanently, whether used or not.
Just-in-time access
Privilege activated for a limited time when needed.
Eligibility
Being allowed to request a privilege, without holding it.
Elevation
Activating eligible privilege, with justification and often approval.
Break-glass access
Emergency access that works when normal sign-in doesn't.
Two-person rule
Requiring two people for an action, not just for its approval.

Check your understanding

  1. An administrator says, “I'm trustworthy, so I don't need just-in-time access.” What's the answer? explain

    Show answer

    It isn't about their trustworthiness: it's about how long a powerful credential exists that an attacker could steal or misuse through their browser, laptop or session. Just-in-time access shrinks that exposure from all the time to the few hours it's used.

  2. Why should a permission set used for elevation have a session duration no longer than the grant? apply

    Show answer

    Removing the assignment stops new sessions but not ones already started. A twelve-hour session started near the end of a one-hour grant would keep the privilege for eleven more hours.

  3. Larder's first break-glass test found the runbook was in the wiki. Why is that a problem, and what else would you check? apply

    Show answer

    The wiki signs in through the IdP, which might be the thing that's down. Also check: alarm delivery doesn't depend on SSO-protected tools, the security keys aren't registered only on IdP-managed devices, passwords haven't expired, and the people who know the safe's combination aren't all the same two people.

  4. Design elevation for a team of four engineers where everyone is on call and the CTO is usually unreachable. design

    Show answer

    All four eligible for an operator set, self-approved during incidents with next-day review; database and admin sets approved by any other engineer, never yourself; one-hour grants and sessions; break-glass with two people for when nobody else is reachable; the CTO reviews the weekly elevation log rather than approving each one.

  5. What makes an action deserve a two-person rule rather than just an approval? explain

    Show answer

    Approval happens before; the approved person then acts alone and could do more than approved. For actions that are catastrophic or could disable oversight (SCPs, logging, account deletion), the second person must see and accept the exact change, as in a mandatory code review, with direct changes denied to everyone.

  6. Compare just-in-time elevation on Azure and on AWS. compare

    Show answer

    Azure has it built in: Privileged Identity Management makes people eligible for roles, which they activate with MFA, justification and optional approval for a limited time. AWS Identity Center has no eligible state, so a tool (such as TEAM) or time-limited IdP groups create and remove account assignments.

Going further

  • AWS: Temporary elevated access with IAM Identity Center, TEAM, Break glass access.
  • Azure: Privileged Identity Management. Google Cloud: Privileged Access Manager.

Part IV · Access for software

15

Identities for workloads

Code needs credentials too: to read a bucket, send an email, publish an event. The worst place for them is where people usually put them first: in the code, an environment file, a container image. On AWS, the platform hands every workload short-lived credentials for its own role, refreshes them automatically, and never lets anyone see a key. This chapter shows how that works on EC2, ECS and Lambda, and how to stop an attacker borrowing those credentials through your own application.

By the end you can

  • Explain how code on EC2, ECS and Lambda gets role credentials without any key being stored.
  • Explain why the instance metadata service is a target, and require IMDSv2.
  • Tell an ECS task role from a task execution role, and give each the right permissions.
  • Give each workload its own narrow role, owned by a team.
  • Choose the right identity for workloads outside AWS.

Before you start

  • Roles, service roles, iam:PassRole and the credential chain (Chapter 8).
  • Writing a least-privilege policy (Chapter 9, 9.9).

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, Python 3; optional AWS step, free
Main provider
AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 15.8

At Larder

On Monday 15 March 2027, an email arrived at security@larder.example from a security researcher. Larder Core, the original application, still running on three virtual machines, has a small endpoint that resizes product images: /img?url=…. It fetched any URL it was given. The researcher had asked it for http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/, and it had politely answered with a role name: larder-core. “I stopped there,” the email said. “The next request would have returned that role's credentials.”

The role had AmazonS3FullAccess and full access to the email-sending service. Section 15.9 shows what Larder did that afternoon, and the next week.

15.1The problem with keys in code

A workload is any software that calls cloud APIs: an application, a background job, a function. It needs credentials, and the tempting answer is an access key in a configuration file or environment variable. That key is long-lived (Chapter 1), so it ends up in backups, container images, crash dumps, logs and Git history, and works for whoever finds it until someone rotates it, which nobody does because rotation might break production.

On AWS the answer is always the same: give the workload a role, and let the platform deliver that role's temporary credentials to the code at run time (Chapter 8). The SDK's credential chain finds them automatically and refreshes them before they expire. Nobody, not the developer, not the deployment, not the attacker who reads the config, ever sees a key.

15.2How it works on EC2

An EC2 instance gets a role through an instance profile, a container for one role, attached at launch or later. Inside the instance, the instance metadata service (IMDS), reachable at the link-local address 169.254.169.254, serves that role's current temporary credentials. AWS rotates them automatically; the SDK fetches new ones before the old ones expire.

15.3Protecting the metadata service: IMDSv2

“Only answers requests from inside the instance” is exactly what makes IMDS a target. Any bug that makes your application send a request for an attacker, a server-side request forgery (SSRF) like Larder's image proxy, an open proxy, a misconfigured firewall, comes from inside the instance. With the original metadata service, IMDSv1, a single GET returns the role's credentials, and an attacker can then use them from anywhere.

IMDSv2 makes the metadata service session-oriented:

  1. The caller first asks for a session token with an HTTP PUT to /latest/api/token, with a header saying how long the token should live.
  2. Every metadata request must then carry that token in a header.

Most SSRF bugs can only make the application send a GET to a URL; they can't choose the method or add headers. IMDSv2 also refuses to issue tokens to requests carrying an X-Forwarded-For header (a sign of a proxy), and the token response has a hop limit, the network packet's time-to-live: with a hop limit of 1, the response can't travel beyond the instance itself, so it can't reach a container running behind the host's network. Set it to 2 only if containers on the instance genuinely need the instance's role, and usually they shouldn't (15.4).

Key idea

Require IMDSv2 on every instance. It costs nothing, every current SDK supports it, and it turns the commonest cloud SSRF from a credential leak into an error.

Make it the default everywhere: set HttpTokens=required on running instances, in launch templates and AMIs, and as the account's default for new instances in every region. Later, deny launching instances without it, with the condition key ec2:MetadataHttpTokens in an SCP (Chapter 20). For defence in depth, condition keys such as aws:EC2InstanceSourceVPC let a policy accept an instance's credentials only when they're used from inside your own network (Chapter 22).

15.4ECS: two roles per task

Containers on ECS (on EC2 or on Fargate) don't use the instance's metadata service for their own identity. Each task definition names up to two roles, and confusing them is one of the commonest ECS mistakes:

Table 15.1 · The two roles of an ECS task
Task roleTask execution role
Used byyour application code inside the containerthe ECS agent, before and around your code
Forwhatever the app does: read config, write route plans (Chapter 9)pulling the image from ECR, writing logs, fetching secrets to inject as environment variables
Delivered bya credentials endpoint at 169.254.170.2, found through AWS_CONTAINER_CREDENTIALS_RELATIVE_URInever visible to your code
Larder's Dispatchdispatch-taskdispatch-task-execution

Each task gets its own credentials, even when several tasks share an instance. On ECS on EC2, block containers from reaching the instance's metadata service (the ECS agent setting ECS_AWSVPC_BLOCK_IMDS, or a hop limit of 1), so a container can't step up to the instance's role.

15.5Lambda

A Lambda function has an execution role. Lambda assumes it and places the temporary credentials in the function's environment variables (AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID, AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY, AWS_SESSION_TOKEN) for the SDK to find. There's no metadata service to forge requests to, but there is an environment to leak: a function that echoes its environment in an error message, or reads arbitrary files (Linux exposes the environment at /proc/self/environ), hands out its role's credentials. Never log the environment, and give every function its own role.

15.6One role per workload

  • One role per workload, never one shared “app role”: a leak or a bug in one service then exposes only that service's permissions, and CloudTrail tells you which service did what (Chapter 1).
  • Least privilege, with the method from Chapter 9: list what the code does, map to actions, narrow resources, validate, test, watch.
  • An owning team, as a tag (team=dispatch), so the role isn't orphaned when people move (Chapter 13).
  • Pass only what you mean to. Whoever launches the workload needs iam:PassRole on its role, and only its role (Chapter 8, 8.7).

Watch out: “it already has a role” isn't the end

Moving from keys to roles removes the long-lived secret, but not the blast radius. A role with AmazonS3FullAccess is just as dangerous in an attacker's hands for the few hours its credentials last; they need minutes. Larder Core's problem wasn't a key; it was a broad role reachable through a bug.

15.7Workloads outside AWS

Code that runs elsewhere still shouldn't hold access keys:

  • CI/CD systems and other clouds that issue OIDC tokens exchange them for role sessions (AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity), as Chapter 17 does for GitHub Actions.
  • Servers in your own data centre or stores can use IAM Roles Anywhere: they authenticate with an X.509 certificate issued by a certificate authority you register with AWS, and get role sessions in return. The certificate's private key, ideally in a hardware module, replaces the access key.
  • Third-party SaaS that acts in your account gets a cross-account role with an external ID (Chapter 21).

15.8Across providers

Table 15.2 · Workload identity at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Identity for a VMinstance profile → rolemanaged identity (system- or user-assigned)attached service account
Where code gets tokensIMDS at 169.254.169.254, IMDSv2 token requiredIMDS at 169.254.169.254, header Metadata: true requiredmetadata server at metadata.google.internal, header Metadata-Flavor: Google required
Containers and functionsECS task role, Lambda execution rolemanaged identities on Container Apps, Functions, App Serviceper-service service accounts on Cloud Run and Cloud Functions
Outside the cloudRoles Anywhere; OIDC federationworkload identity federationworkload identity federation

On Azure

A managed identity is a service principal Azure creates and rotates for you. System-assigned identities live and die with one resource; user-assigned identities are separate resources you can share between, say, a pool of identical VMs. Code requests tokens for a specific resource (Storage, Key Vault, Graph) from the local metadata endpoint, which requires a Metadata: true header, the same anti-SSRF idea as IMDSv2's token.

On Google Cloud

VMs and serverless services run as a service account; tokens come from the metadata server, which requires a Metadata-Flavor: Google header. Avoid the Compute Engine default service account, which in many projects has the broad Editor role: give each workload its own service account with narrow roles, and stop new projects granting Editor to it with the organisation policy for automatic default grants.

15.9At Larder: an image proxy and a broad role

At Larder: solved

Within the hour, Ben's team limited the image proxy to Larder's own image hosts and made it refuse private and link-local addresses. Eli required IMDSv2 on the three Larder Core instances, with a hop limit of 1, which took one command each and no restart; the application's SDK had supported it for years. He set IMDSv2 as the default for new instances in both accounts' regions, and searched CloudTrail for any use of larder-core's credentials from outside Larder's network in the last 90 days: there was none. Larder thanked the researcher, and started a page about how to report vulnerabilities.

The next week, larder-core lost AmazonS3FullAccess and full email-service access, replaced by a policy written with the method from Chapter 9: two buckets, one sending identity, one queue. The same review removed the SSH key the engineers had shared since 2024 and the rule letting the office reach port 22: shell access now goes through Session Manager, which is authorised by Identity Center sessions and logged (Chapter 14).

15.10Lab: SSRF against IMDSv1 and IMDSv2

Lab 15 · A fake metadata service and a real bug

You'll run a small imitation of the instance metadata service on your own machine, then call it two ways: like the AWS SDK does, and like an SSRF bug does, with tokens optional and then required. You need Python 3; for the optional step, the AWS CLI. Cost: nothing. The credentials in it are fake, and it listens only on 127.0.0.1.

imds.pyPython 3.8+

# imds.py · a fake instance metadata service, an SSRF bug, and what IMDSv2 changes
# Usage: python3 imds.py optional   (IMDSv1 allowed)   |   python3 imds.py required   (IMDSv2 only)
import json, secrets, sys, threading, urllib.request
from http.server import BaseHTTPRequestHandler, HTTPServer

MODE = sys.argv[1] if len(sys.argv) > 1 else "optional"
BASE = "http://127.0.0.1:8169"                 # the real one is http://169.254.169.254
ROLE = "larder-core"
CREDS = {"Code": "Success", "AccessKeyId": "ASIAEXAMPLEFAKEKEY01", "SecretAccessKey": "fake/secret",
         "Token": "fake-session-token", "Expiration": "2027-03-15T16:00:00Z"}
TOKENS = set()

class IMDS(BaseHTTPRequestHandler):
    def log_message(self, *args):
        pass

    def reply(self, code, body):
        self.send_response(code)
        self.end_headers()
        self.wfile.write(body.encode())

    def do_PUT(self):
        if self.path != "/latest/api/token" or "X-aws-ec2-metadata-token-ttl-seconds" not in self.headers:
            return self.reply(400, "bad request")
        if "X-Forwarded-For" in self.headers:          # the real IMDS refuses tokens to proxied requests
            return self.reply(403, "forbidden")
        token = secrets.token_urlsafe(24)
        TOKENS.add(token)
        self.reply(200, token)

    def do_GET(self):
        token = self.headers.get("X-aws-ec2-metadata-token")
        if token not in TOKENS and (MODE == "required" or token is not None):
            return self.reply(401, "unauthorized")
        if self.path == "/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/":
            return self.reply(200, ROLE)
        if self.path == f"/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/{ROLE}":
            return self.reply(200, json.dumps(CREDS))
        self.reply(404, "not found")

def fetch_image(url):
    """Larder Core's product-image proxy: fetches whatever URL a request names. The SSRF bug."""
    try:
        with urllib.request.urlopen(url, timeout=2) as r:
            return r.read().decode()[:60]
    except Exception as e:
        return f"error: {e}"

def sdk_get_credentials():
    """What the AWS SDK does: get a session token with PUT, then read with it."""
    put = urllib.request.Request(f"{BASE}/latest/api/token", method="PUT",
                                 headers={"X-aws-ec2-metadata-token-ttl-seconds": "21600"})
    token = urllib.request.urlopen(put, timeout=2).read().decode()
    get = urllib.request.Request(f"{BASE}/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/{ROLE}",
                                 headers={"X-aws-ec2-metadata-token": token})
    return json.loads(urllib.request.urlopen(get, timeout=2).read())["AccessKeyId"]

if __name__ == "__main__":
    server = HTTPServer(("127.0.0.1", 8169), IMDS)
    threading.Thread(target=server.serve_forever, daemon=True).start()
    print(f"metadata service, tokens {MODE}")
    print("  SDK, with a token:      ", sdk_get_credentials())
    print("  attacker via the proxy: ", fetch_image(f"{BASE}/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/"))
    print("                          ", fetch_image(f"{BASE}/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/{ROLE}"))
    server.shutdown()
  1. Tokens optional (IMDSv1 allowed). Run python3 imds.py optional: 
    metadata service, tokens optional
  SDK, with a token:       ASIAEXAMPLEFAKEKEY01
  attacker via the proxy:  larder-core
                           {"Code": "Success", "AccessKeyId": "ASIAEXAMPLEFAKEKEY01", "
    Two GETs through the image proxy: the first finds the role's name (the researcher's request), the second returns its credentials.
  2. Tokens required (IMDSv2). Run python3 imds.py required: 
    metadata service, tokens required
  SDK, with a token:       ASIAEXAMPLEFAKEKEY01
  attacker via the proxy:  error: HTTP Error 401: Unauthorized
                           error: HTTP Error 401: Unauthorized
    The application still works, because the SDK does the PUT first. The bug is still there, but it no longer leads anywhere.
  3. Break it on purpose. Make the proxy “smarter”: let it accept a method and headers from the request (urllib.request.Request(url, method=…, headers=…)) and use it to do the PUT. Now it can get a token, unless it adds X-Forwarded-For, as many proxies do: add that header and the token request is refused with 403. This is why SSRF fixes belong in the application too: IMDSv2 is a seat belt, not a reason to keep the bug.
  4. Optional: check your account. List instances that still allow IMDSv1, and make IMDSv2 the default for new instances in the current region (free, nothing is launched): 
    aws ec2 describe-instances --query \
  'Reservations[].Instances[?MetadataOptions.HttpTokens==`optional`].[InstanceId]' --output text
aws ec2 modify-instance-metadata-defaults --http-tokens required --http-put-response-hop-limit 1
aws ec2 get-instance-metadata-defaults
    To fix one instance: aws ec2 modify-instance-metadata-options --instance-id i-… --http-tokens required --http-put-response-hop-limit 1.

15.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Access keys in config, env files or imagesLong-lived keys leak with backups, images and repos.Instance profiles, task roles, execution roles.
IMDSv1 left enabledAn SSRF bug becomes a credential leak.IMDSv2 required, hop limit 1, account default, SCP.
Task role and execution role swapped or mergedThe app can pull every image and read every secret, or the agent can't start the task.Two roles, each with its own narrow policy.
One role shared by many workloadsOne bug exposes everything; CloudTrail can't tell services apart.One role per workload, tagged with its team.
Broad managed policies on workload rolesMinutes of stolen credentials are enough to do damage.Least-privilege customer managed policies.
SSH keys shared by a teamNo attribution, no expiry, an open port.Session Manager, through SSO sessions.

✓Chapter summary

  • Workloads get roles, and the platform delivers short-lived credentials; no key is stored or seen.
  • EC2: instance profile → role → credentials from the metadata service, refreshed by the SDK.
  • IMDSv2 (PUT for a token, token on every request, hop limit) turns SSRF credential theft into an error. Require it everywhere.
  • ECS: a task role for the app, a task execution role for the agent. Lambda: an execution role, with credentials in the environment.
  • One narrow role per workload, owned by a team; restrict who can pass it.
  • Outside AWS: OIDC federation, IAM Roles Anywhere, cross-account roles; never access keys.

Key terms

Workload identity
The identity software uses to call cloud APIs.
Instance profile
The container that attaches a role to an EC2 instance.
Instance metadata service
The link-local endpoint that serves an instance's details and role credentials.
IMDSv2
The session-oriented metadata service: PUT for a token, then token on each request.
SSRF
Server-side request forgery: making a server send requests on an attacker's behalf.
Hop limit
How many network hops IMDSv2's token response may travel.
Task role / execution role
An ECS task's role for its code, and the ECS agent's role for starting it.
IAM Roles Anywhere
Role sessions for workloads outside AWS, authenticated with X.509 certificates.

Check your understanding

  1. Where does code running on an EC2 instance with an instance profile get its credentials, and who rotates them? recall

    Show answer

    From the instance metadata service at 169.254.169.254. AWS issues and rotates the role's temporary credentials; the SDK fetches fresh ones before the old ones expire.

  2. Explain why IMDSv2 stops a typical SSRF bug from stealing credentials, but doesn't make the bug harmless. explain

    Show answer

    A typical SSRF bug can only make the server send a GET to a chosen URL; IMDSv2 needs a PUT with a special header first, and refuses proxied token requests. But the bug can still reach other internal services, so it must be fixed in the application too.

  3. An ECS task can't start: “unable to pull image”. Which role do you look at? And if the app gets “access denied” writing to S3? apply

    Show answer

    Pulling the image is the task execution role's job (ECR permissions). Writing to S3 is the application's job, so the task role.

  4. A Lambda function returns its full environment in an error message to the caller. Why is that a credential leak? apply

    Show answer

    Lambda places the execution role's temporary credentials in environment variables. Anyone who sees the error gets a working key, secret and session token until they expire.

  5. Design the identities for a new service with an API on ECS, a nightly batch job on Lambda, and a script on a store's back-office PC. design

    Show answer

    An ECS task role and execution role for the API; a separate execution role for the batch function; IAM Roles Anywhere with a device certificate (or a narrowly scoped OIDC federation) for the PC. Each role narrow, tagged with the owning team, with PassRole restricted to the deployment pipeline.

  6. Compare how Azure and Google Cloud protect their metadata endpoints from SSRF with how AWS does. compare

    Show answer

    Azure requires a Metadata: true header and Google a Metadata-Flavor: Google header, which a GET-only SSRF usually can't add. AWS's IMDSv2 requires a PUT to obtain a session token, then a token header on every request, plus a hop limit and a refusal of proxied token requests.

Going further

  • AWS: Configure the instance metadata service, ECS task IAM roles, Lambda execution role, IAM Roles Anywhere.
  • OWASP SSRF Prevention Cheat Sheet.
  • Azure: Managed identities. Google Cloud: Service accounts for VMs.

Part IV · Access for software

16

Kubernetes and workload identity

A Kubernetes cluster on a cloud has two permission systems stacked on top of each other: Kubernetes' own RBAC, which decides who may do what to the cluster, and the cloud's IAM, which decides what pods may do to everything else. Each has its own traps, and the gap between them, where a pod quietly borrows its node's cloud role, is the biggest trap of all. This chapter covers both, and how to join them safely on EKS, AKS and GKE.

By the end you can

  • Explain the two permission systems in a managed cluster, and which one each request goes through.
  • Read and write Kubernetes Roles, ClusterRoles and bindings, and spot the dangerous ones.
  • Give people cluster access from your identity provider, without shared kubeconfigs.
  • Give each pod its own AWS role with EKS Pod Identity or IRSA, and keep pods away from the node's role.

Before you start

  • Workload identity and the metadata service (Chapter 15); OIDC tokens and audiences (Chapter 3, Chapter 4).
  • Roles, attributes and relationships (Chapter 6). Basic Kubernetes words: pod, namespace, deployment.

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, Python 3; optional local cluster with kind, free
Main provider
AWS (EKS), with Azure and Google Cloud in 16.6

At Larder

Larder runs its containers on ECS (Chapter 15), and in March 2027 the question came up that every growing team asks: should we move to Kubernetes? Eli and Dee built a proof of concept in the staging account: an EKS cluster running Engine A, the route planner, in a namespace called dispatch-poc.

Two weeks in, Dee ran a throwaway debugging pod in that namespace and found she could read Engine A's route plans in S3 from it, and list every secret in the cluster. Neither was intended, and neither was a bug in Kubernetes. Section 16.7 explains both, and what Larder decided.

16.1Two permission systems

In a managed Kubernetes cluster, every action goes through one of two doors:

  • Requests to the cluster (create a deployment, read a secret, exec into a pod) go to the Kubernetes API server and are decided by Kubernetes RBAC.
  • Requests to the cloud (read a bucket, publish to a queue) go to the cloud's APIs and are decided by IAM, using whatever cloud credentials the pod has.

People need an identity in both (to deploy, and sometimes to look at cloud resources); pods need one in both too (a Kubernetes service account for the API, a cloud role for everything else). The cloud provider glues them together, and the glue is where the security decisions are.

16.2Kubernetes RBAC

Kubernetes RBAC (Chapter 6, in its role-based form) has four objects:

  • A Role lists rules (API groups, resources, verbs) inside one namespace. A ClusterRole does the same cluster-wide, or as a reusable template.
  • A RoleBinding grants a Role, or a ClusterRole, to subjects within one namespace. A ClusterRoleBinding grants a ClusterRole everywhere.
  • Subjects are users, groups and service accounts. Kubernetes has no user database: users and groups are whatever the authenticator says (on EKS, mapped from IAM; 16.4).

Everything is allow-only and default deny, like AWS security groups (Handbook 1, Chapter 9). Some permissions are far more powerful than they look, because they let you become someone else:

Table 16.1 · Kubernetes permissions that are really admin
PermissionWhy it's dangerous
get / list on secretsreads every secret in the namespace, including other service accounts' credentials
create on pods (or deployments, jobs)runs a pod as any service account in the namespace, with its permissions and its cloud role
create on pods/execa shell inside someone else's running pod
bind, escalate, impersonategrant yourself roles, or act as another user or group
anything on nodes/proxy, or privileged podsreaches the node, and from there everything on it
cluster-admin, or * on *everything, everywhere

Watch out: bindings to groups you didn't think about

Every service account belongs to the groups system:serviceaccounts and system:serviceaccounts:namespace, and every authenticated identity to system:authenticated. Binding a role to one of these, often copied from an installation guide “to make the chart work”, grants it to every pod in the namespace, or the cluster, including ones created later.

16.3Service accounts and their tokens

Every pod runs as a Kubernetes service account, the namespace's default one unless you choose another. Kubernetes mounts a token for it into the pod, which the pod uses to call the API server. Modern clusters issue projected tokens: JWTs (Chapter 3) bound to the pod, with an audience and an expiry of about an hour, refreshed by the kubelet. Three habits:

  • One service account per workload, never default, so permissions and cloud roles attach to exactly one thing.
  • No API token unless needed: most application pods never call the Kubernetes API. Set automountServiceAccountToken: false on them.
  • Bind narrowly: a Role and RoleBinding in the workload's namespace, for the few resources it needs.

16.4People into the cluster

On EKS, people authenticate with their AWS identity: aws eks get-token (run for you by kubectl) produces a short-lived token from their current role session, and the API server checks it with IAM. Access entries then map IAM principals to Kubernetes permissions, either through AWS-managed access policies (cluster admin, admin, edit, view; cluster-wide or per namespace) or through Kubernetes groups that your own RoleBindings use.

Combine that with Identity Center (Chapter 12) and cluster access follows the same groups as everything else: an access entry for the AWSReservedSSO_Developer_… role with view on all namespaces and edit on the team's own, and one for the elevated PlatformAdmin role with cluster admin, which itself is only granted just in time (Chapter 14). No kubeconfig contains a long-lived credential. Turn off the default that makes the cluster's creator a permanent admin, once access entries exist.

aws-authOlder EKS clusters map IAM to Kubernetes users through a ConfigMap called aws-auth in kube-system. Anyone who can edit it can make themselves admin, and a typo can lock everyone out. Access entries replace it and are managed through the EKS API, with CloudTrail recording every change.

16.5Pods into AWS

Every EKS worker node is an EC2 instance with an instance profile: the node role, which the node needs to join the cluster, pull images and manage pod networking. Here's the trap: unless you stop it, any pod on the node can reach the node's metadata service (Chapter 15) and get the node role's credentials. Add an application permission to the node role, “just so Engine A can read its config”, and every pod on every node has it.

EKS offers two ways to give a pod its own role, both based on its service account:

Table 16.2 · EKS Pod Identity and IRSA
EKS Pod IdentityIRSA (IAM roles for service accounts)
How it worksan agent on each node exchanges the pod's identity for the role's credentialsthe pod's projected service account token (an OIDC JWT) is exchanged with AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity
Configuredan association (cluster, namespace, service account → role) in the EKS APIan IAM OIDC provider per cluster, and an annotation on the service account
Trust policytrusts the service pods.eks.amazonaws.com, the same for every clustertrusts the cluster's OIDC provider, with conditions on sub and aud
Extrassession tags (cluster, namespace, service account) for ABACworks outside EKS too, with any OIDC-issuing cluster
Choose it fornew EKS clustersexisting setups, or clusters that aren't EKS

engine-a-pod-role trust policyEKS Pod Identity

{ "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{ "Effect": "Allow",
                  "Principal": { "Service": "pods.eks.amazonaws.com" },
                  "Action": ["sts:AssumeRole", "sts:TagSession"] }] }
aws eks create-pod-identity-association --cluster-name larder-poc \
  --namespace dispatch-poc --service-account engine-a \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::444455556666:role/engine-a-pod-role

Then close the trap: keep the node role to the AWS-managed node policies and nothing else, and stop pods reaching the node's metadata service (IMDSv2 with a hop limit of 1 on the nodes, or a network policy that blocks 169.254.169.254). A pod without an association then has no AWS access at all, which is the right default.

Key idea

In a cluster, the service account is the workload's identity in both worlds. Give each workload its own, bind it narrowly in Kubernetes, and associate it with its own role in the cloud.

16.6Across providers

Table 16.3 · Managed Kubernetes identity at the big three
EKS (AWS)AKS (Azure)GKE (Google Cloud)
People into the clusterIAM + access entriesEntra ID integration; Kubernetes RBAC or Azure RBAC for KubernetesGoogle identities and Google Groups for RBAC
Pods into the cloudPod Identity or IRSAMicrosoft Entra Workload ID: a federated credential maps a service account to a managed identity or appWorkload Identity Federation for GKE: grant roles to the Kubernetes service account's principal
The node's identitynode role; block IMDS from podskubelet managed identity; restrict pod access to IMDSnode service account; the GKE metadata server hides node credentials from pods

On Azure

Microsoft Entra Workload ID uses the cluster's OIDC issuer: you add a federated identity credential to a user-assigned managed identity saying “trust tokens for service account engine-a in namespace dispatch-poc from this cluster”. The pod exchanges its projected token for an Entra token, much like IRSA. For people, Entra ID groups can be used directly in Kubernetes RoleBindings, or Azure RBAC can govern Kubernetes objects too.

On Google Cloud

With Workload Identity Federation for GKE, you grant IAM roles directly to a Kubernetes service account's principal identifier, and the GKE metadata server on each node gives pods tokens for it while hiding the node's own service account. It's on by default in Autopilot clusters, which removes the node-role trap entirely.

16.7At Larder: the experiment, and the decision

At Larder: solved

Dee's two findings had two causes. The route plans: to get Engine A working on the first day, someone had added read access to the route-plan bucket to the node role, and the nodes allowed pods to reach the metadata service, so every pod, debugging pods included, could read them. The secrets: Engine A's Helm chart documentation suggested a ClusterRoleBinding of cluster-admin to the namespace's service accounts group, “to make installation simple”, and that binding was still there, so every pod in dispatch-poc was cluster admin.

Fixing both took a morning: an Engine A service account with a Pod Identity association to its own role, the S3 permission removed from the node role, IMDS blocked for pods, the binding replaced by a Role for the two config maps Engine A reads, and access entries for the Identity Center roles. But the exercise answered the bigger question too. On 30 April 2027, Eli and Dee recommended staying on ECS: a team of Larder's size gets per-task roles there by default, with one permission system instead of two. They wrote down the identity rules above for the day the question comes back, and deleted the cluster that afternoon.

16.8Lab: ask “can I?”

Lab 16 · Kubernetes RBAC, by hand and for real

You'll answer kubectl auth can-i questions from the experiment's Roles and bindings with a small evaluator, find the dangerous binding, fix it, then (optionally) repeat it in a real local cluster. You need Python 3; for the optional part, Docker, kind and kubectl. Cost: nothing; kind runs on your machine.

kube_rbac.pyPython 3.8+

# kube_rbac.py · answer "kubectl auth can-i" from Roles, ClusterRoles and their bindings
CLUSTER_ROLES = {
    "cluster-admin": [{"resources": ["*"], "verbs": ["*"]}],
    "view":          [{"resources": ["pods", "configmaps", "deployments", "services"],   # no secrets
                       "verbs": ["get", "list", "watch"]}],
}
ROLES = {   # (namespace, name) → rules
    ("dispatch-poc", "engine-a-config"): [{"resources": ["configmaps"], "verbs": ["get"]}],
}
BINDINGS = [   # (kind, namespace or None, role kind, role name, subjects)
    ("ClusterRoleBinding", None, "ClusterRole", "view", ["Group:larder:engineers"]),
    ("RoleBinding", "dispatch-poc", "Role", "engine-a-config",
     ["ServiceAccount:dispatch-poc:engine-a"]),
    ("ClusterRoleBinding", None, "ClusterRole", "cluster-admin",            # "to make the chart install"
     ["Group:system:serviceaccounts:dispatch-poc"]),
]

def identity(subject):
    """A subject string → (name, groups), as the API server sees it."""
    kind, rest = subject.split(":", 1)
    if kind == "ServiceAccount":
        ns, name = rest.split(":")
        return f"system:serviceaccount:{ns}:{name}", {"system:serviceaccounts",
                                                      f"system:serviceaccounts:{ns}", "system:authenticated"}
    name, groups = rest.split("|")
    return name, set(groups.split(",")) | {"system:authenticated"}

def matches(binding_subject, name, groups):
    kind, value = binding_subject.split(":", 1)
    if kind == "ServiceAccount":
        ns, sa = value.split(":")
        return name == f"system:serviceaccount:{ns}:{sa}"
    return value in groups if kind == "Group" else value == name

def can_i(subject, verb, resource, namespace=None):
    name, groups = identity(subject)
    for kind, ns, role_kind, role, subjects in BINDINGS:
        if not any(matches(s, name, groups) for s in subjects):
            continue
        if kind == "RoleBinding" and ns != namespace:       # a RoleBinding only works in its namespace
            continue
        rules = CLUSTER_ROLES[role] if role_kind == "ClusterRole" else ROLES[(ns, role)]
        for r in rules:
            if ("*" in r["resources"] or resource in r["resources"]) and ("*" in r["verbs"] or verb in r["verbs"]):
                return f"yes  ({kind} → {role})"
    return "no"

if __name__ == "__main__":
    DEE = "User:dee@larder.example|larder:engineers"
    ENGINE = "ServiceAccount:dispatch-poc:engine-a"
    DEBUG = "ServiceAccount:dispatch-poc:default"
    for who, verb, res, ns in [(DEE, "list", "pods", "dispatch-poc"), (DEE, "get", "secrets", "dispatch-poc"),
                               (ENGINE, "get", "configmaps", "dispatch-poc"), (ENGINE, "get", "secrets", "kube-system"),
                               (DEBUG, "delete", "nodes", None), ("ServiceAccount:other:default", "get", "secrets", "other")]:
        print(f"{who.split('|')[0].split(':', 1)[1]:<28} {verb:<6} {res:<10} {ns or '(cluster)':<13} {can_i(who, verb, res, ns)}")
  1. Run it with python3 kube_rbac.py: 
    dee@larder.example           list   pods       dispatch-poc  yes  (ClusterRoleBinding → view)
dee@larder.example           get    secrets    dispatch-poc  no
dispatch-poc:engine-a        get    configmaps dispatch-poc  yes  (RoleBinding → engine-a-config)
dispatch-poc:engine-a        get    secrets    kube-system   yes  (ClusterRoleBinding → cluster-admin)
dispatch-poc:default         delete nodes      (cluster)     yes  (ClusterRoleBinding → cluster-admin)
other:default                get    secrets    other         no
    Dee's answers are right: engineers can view, not read secrets. The next three lines are Dee's discovery: the chart's convenience binding makes every service account in dispatch-poc, including default, cluster admin.
  2. Fix it. Delete the cluster-admin binding and run again. Engine A keeps exactly what it needs; the debugging pod's default account can do nothing.
  3. Break it differently. Add a RoleBinding in dispatch-poc that grants the view ClusterRole to the group system:authenticated. Who can now list pods in dispatch-poc? (Every identity the cluster accepts, including every service account in every namespace.)
  4. Optional: in a real cluster. 
    kind create cluster --name rbac-lab
kubectl create namespace dispatch-poc
kubectl create serviceaccount engine-a -n dispatch-poc
kubectl create clusterrolebinding chart-convenience --clusterrole=cluster-admin \
  --group=system:serviceaccounts:dispatch-poc
kubectl auth can-i delete nodes --as=system:serviceaccount:dispatch-poc:default        # yes
kubectl delete clusterrolebinding chart-convenience
kubectl auth can-i delete nodes --as=system:serviceaccount:dispatch-poc:default        # no
kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:dispatch-poc:engine-a -n dispatch-poc
kind delete cluster --name rbac-lab

16.9Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Application permissions on the node roleEvery pod gets them through the metadata service.Pod Identity or IRSA per service account; node role minimal.
Pods can reach the node's IMDSAny pod can become the node.Hop limit 1, or block 169.254.169.254 for pods.
Convenience bindings to built-in groupsWhole namespaces or clusters become admin.Narrow Roles bound to specific service accounts.
Everything running as defaultPermissions and cloud roles can't be told apart.One service account per workload.
Long-lived kubeconfigs and aws-auth editsShared admin credentials; lock-outs.Access entries mapped from SSO roles.
Treating create pods or secrets as harmlessEscalation to any service account in the namespace.Review Table 16.1 permissions like admin.

✓Chapter summary

  • A managed cluster has two permission systems: Kubernetes RBAC for the cluster API, IAM for the cloud.
  • RBAC: Roles and ClusterRoles, bound per namespace or cluster-wide to users, groups and service accounts. Some permissions (secrets, create pods, exec, bind, impersonate) are admin in disguise.
  • Each workload gets its own service account with projected, short-lived tokens, mounted only if needed.
  • People come in through their cloud identity: access entries on EKS, mapped from SSO roles.
  • Pods get their own AWS roles with Pod Identity or IRSA; keep the node role minimal and block pods from IMDS.

Key terms

Kubernetes RBAC
Kubernetes' allow-only, role-based authorisation for its API.
Role / ClusterRole
Rules within a namespace / cluster-wide (or reusable).
RoleBinding / ClusterRoleBinding
Grants of a role to subjects in a namespace / everywhere.
Service account
A pod's identity for the Kubernetes API, and the anchor for its cloud role.
Projected token
A short-lived, audience-bound service account JWT mounted into a pod.
Access entry
An EKS mapping from an IAM principal to Kubernetes permissions.
Node role
The IAM role of an EKS worker node.
EKS Pod Identity / IRSA
Two ways to give a service account its own IAM role.

Check your understanding

  1. A pod reads an S3 bucket and lists pods in its namespace. Which permission system decides each? recall

    Show answer

    Reading S3 is decided by IAM, using the pod's AWS credentials. Listing pods is decided by Kubernetes RBAC, using its service account.

  2. Why is create on pods in a namespace roughly as powerful as everything any service account there can do? explain

    Show answer

    Whoever can create a pod chooses which service account it runs as, so the pod gets that account's RBAC permissions and its associated cloud role.

  3. Every pod in a cluster can read a bucket that only one application should. Name the two likely causes. apply

    Show answer

    The bucket permission is on the node role, and pods can reach the node's metadata service. Or a shared service account (such as default) is associated with a role that has the permission.

  4. Write the conditions an IRSA trust policy needs, and say what goes wrong without the sub condition. apply

    Show answer

    StringEquals on …:sub = system:serviceaccount:namespace:name and …:aud = sts.amazonaws.com. Without sub, any service account in the cluster can assume the role.

  5. Design people's access to a new EKS cluster for a company already using Identity Center with just-in-time admin. design

    Show answer

    Access entries: ViewOnly's role gets view on all namespaces; each team's Developer role gets edit on its namespace; the just-in-time PlatformAdmin role gets cluster admin. Remove the creator's bootstrap admin, and record changes in CloudTrail.

  6. Compare how GKE and EKS stop pods using the node's cloud identity. compare

    Show answer

    GKE's metadata server, with Workload Identity Federation, hides the node's service account from pods and serves only the pod's own identity. On EKS you must do it yourself: hop limit 1 or a network policy blocking IMDS, and a minimal node role.

Going further

  • Kubernetes: Using RBAC authorization and RBAC good practices.
  • AWS: EKS Pod Identity, IRSA, access entries, EKS best practices: IAM.
  • Azure: Workload ID on AKS. Google Cloud: Workload Identity Federation for GKE.

Part IV · Access for software

17

CI/CD without long-lived keys

A deployment pipeline needs to change production, which makes its credentials some of the most powerful in the company, and they usually sit in a CI system's secret store, readable by every workflow and every third-party action it runs. This chapter replaces that stored key with OpenID Connect federation: each job proves who it is with a short-lived token from the CI system, and AWS gives it a role session for exactly that repository, branch and environment.

By the end you can

  • Explain how a CI job's OIDC token becomes an AWS role session.
  • Read the claims in a GitHub Actions token, and choose which to trust.
  • Write a trust policy that admits one repository and environment, and no others.
  • Split deployment into per-environment roles with deploy-time least privilege.
  • Reduce what a compromised workflow or third-party action can do.

Before you start

  • OIDC and ID tokens (Chapter 4); trust policies and conditions (Chapter 8, Chapter 9).
  • Workload identity (Chapter 15). Basic GitHub Actions workflows.

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, Python 3; optional AWS + GitHub step, free
Main provider
AWS and GitHub Actions, with Azure and Google Cloud in 17.8

At Larder

On Sunday 21 March 2027, the maintainers of a popular third-party GitHub Action announced that its version tags had been quietly pointed at malicious code for nineteen hours. The new code printed every secret available to the workflow into the job's log, encoded to slip past log masking. Larder's main repository used that action in its deployment workflow, which ran three times in the window.

Among the secrets in those logs: the access key of deploy-bot, the IAM user Larder had deployed with since 2024, with AdministratorAccess in production (Chapter 7, Chapter 10). The logs were private to Larder's GitHub organisation, but nobody could say where else the action had sent them. Eli rotated the key within two hours. Section 17.9 shows why there's no longer a key to rotate.

17.1The deploy key problem

A stored deploy key combines every weakness from Part I: it's long-lived, it's shared by every job that can read the secret, it usually has broad permissions because deployments touch many things, and it's exposed to every piece of code the pipeline runs, including third-party actions, build tools and test dependencies. Rotating it is a chore that breaks deploys, so it rarely happens: Larder's was two and a half years old.

The fix is the same idea as workload identity (Chapter 15), across a boundary: the CI system already knows exactly which repository, branch and environment a job belongs to, and can vouch for it with a signed token. AWS can be configured to trust that token, and nothing secret needs to be stored at all.

17.2How OIDC federation works

Two things are configured once in AWS: an IAM OIDC identity provider for https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com, with sts.amazonaws.com as its allowed audience; and, per role, a trust policy that names that provider and says which tokens to accept (17.4). In the workflow, the job must be allowed to request a token (permissions: id-token: write), and the official aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials action does the exchange.

17.3What's in the token

GitHub's token is a JWT (Chapter 3) with standard claims and many about the run. The ones that matter for trust:

Table 17.1 · GitHub Actions OIDC claims worth knowing
ClaimExampleNotes
isshttps://token.actions.githubusercontent.comchecked by the IAM OIDC provider
audsts.amazonaws.comwho the token is for; always check it
subrepo:larder/larder-core:environment:productionthe main claim to trust; its form depends on the trigger (below)
repository, repository_ownerlarder/larder-core, lardernames, which can be renamed and reused
repository_id, repository_owner_idnumbersstable IDs; never reused
ref, environment, event_namerefs/heads/main, production, pushwhat ran, where, why
job_workflow_reflarder/larder-core/.github/workflows/deploy.yml@refs/heads/mainwhich workflow file; useful for reusable workflows

By default, sub takes one of these forms: repo:owner/repo:environment:name when the job uses an environment; repo:…:ref:refs/heads/branch (or a tag) for other pushes; and repo:…:pull_request for pull requests. You can customise it per repository or organisation, for example to include the stable owner and repository IDs.

17.4Writing the trust policy

github-deploy-prod trust policyproduction account

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "Federated": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:oidc-provider/token.actions.githubusercontent.com" },
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity",
    "Condition": {
      "StringEquals": {
        "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:aud": "sts.amazonaws.com",
        "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub": "repo:larder/larder-core:environment:production"
      }
    }
  }]
}

Read aloud: “Allow a session for any GitHub Actions token meant for AWS whose subject is exactly the production environment of larder/larder-core.” Everything else, including other branches, pull requests, other Larder repositories and the rest of GitHub, is refused.

Key idea

The OIDC provider says “GitHub signed this”. Only the sub condition says “and it's our repository”. A trust policy without it trusts every repository on GitHub.

Watch out: three ways to trust too much

No sub condition: anyone with a GitHub account can create a repository, request a token with your audience, and assume the role; this misconfiguration has been found in real companies. Wide wildcards such as repo:larder/*: every repository in the organisation, and every branch and pull request in them, gets the role. Names alone: if an organisation or repository is deleted and its name re-registered by someone else, name-based conditions trust them; include the stable repository_owner_id in a customised sub when that risk matters.

17.5One role per environment

Don't give one role every environment. Map GitHub's controls onto AWS's:

  • Staging: a role in the staging account trusting pushes to main (repo:larder/larder-core:ref:refs/heads/main). Every merge deploys to staging.
  • Production: a role in the production account trusting only the production environment, which in GitHub has protection rules: required reviewers, deployment branches limited to main. The approval in GitHub becomes the only way to get production credentials.
  • Pull requests: no deploy role at all. If tests need AWS, a separate role with read-only access to test fixtures, in a sandbox account. Pull request code is untrusted until reviewed.

17.6Deploy-time least privilege

“The pipeline deploys” sounds like it needs administrator rights. It rarely does. List what a deploy actually does (Chapter 9, 9.9). For a container service, that's typically: push an image to one ECR repository, register a new task definition, update one ECS service, and pass two specific roles to ECS:

{ "Sid": "PassOnlyDispatchRoles", "Effect": "Allow", "Action": "iam:PassRole",
  "Resource": ["arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/dispatch-task",
               "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/dispatch-task-execution"],
  "Condition": { "StringEquals": { "iam:PassedToService": "ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com" } } }

Infrastructure changes (Terraform plans and applies) are a different job with different risks; give them their own roles, with plan read-only and apply behind its own protected environment (Chapter 24). Keep sessions at one hour, give the roles the permissions boundary from Chapter 10, and let the guardrails apply to them like to anyone.

17.7Shrinking what a bad workflow can do

OIDC removes the stored secret, but a malicious action running inside your deploy job can still use the job's token while the job runs. Defence in depth:

  • Pin third-party actions to a full commit SHA, not a tag. Tags can be moved, which is exactly what happened at Larder; a SHA can't.
  • Set permissions: explicitly in every workflow, with id-token: write only on jobs that deploy, and contents: read by default.
  • Keep deploy jobs small: no third-party actions you don't need in the job that holds production credentials.
  • Watch CloudTrail for the deploy roles: what they did, and from which job (sourceIdentity and the session name record the run).

17.8Across providers

Table 17.2 · CI/CD federation at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Trust set up asIAM OIDC provider + role trust policya federated identity credential on an app registration or user-assigned managed identitya workload identity pool and provider
Which jobs are trustedconditions on sub and audexact issuer, subject and audience in the credentialan attribute condition (e.g. on assertion.repository_owner_id) and attribute mapping
GitHub actionaws-actions/configure-aws-credentialsazure/login with client, tenant and subscription IDs, no secretgoogle-github-actions/auth

On Azure

A federated identity credential says “trust tokens from this issuer with exactly this subject”, such as repo:larder/larder-core:environment:production. Matching is exact, which avoids the wildcard trap, but means one credential per environment or branch. The workflow logs in with the identity's client ID and tenant ID, and no client secret exists.

On Google Cloud

A workload identity pool with an OIDC provider for GitHub maps token claims to attributes; an attribute condition on the provider rejects everything else up front. Google requires a condition for GitHub providers, precisely because of the “any repository” mistake. Roles are then granted to principals in the pool, such as all jobs from one repository.

17.9At Larder: the last IAM user goes

At Larder: solved

The rotated key was a stopgap. In the two weeks after 21 March, Eli added the GitHub OIDC provider to the staging and production accounts and created two roles: github-deploy-staging, trusting pushes to main of larder/larder-core, and github-deploy-prod, trusting only its production environment, which requires one tech lead's approval in GitHub. Their policies were written from what a deploy actually does: ECR push for the service's repository, task definitions, one ECS service per deploy, and iam:PassRole for the service's two roles only. Both carry team-boundary, so the backup guardrail applies to them as well.

The same change pinned every third-party action to a commit SHA and set permissions: in every workflow. CloudTrail showed no use of the leaked key from outside GitHub's runners before it was rotated. On 9 April 2027, deploy-bot was deleted: the last IAM user in either account that wasn't a break-glass account. Larder now stores no AWS secret anywhere.

17.10Lab: who could assume this role?

Lab 17 · Trust conditions against token claims

You'll test four trust policies against five kinds of GitHub job, including a stranger's, then optionally set up a real OIDC role for a repository of your own. You need Python 3; for the optional part, the AWS CLI as an administrator of a sandbox account and a GitHub repository you control. Cost: free.

oidc_trust.pyPython 3.8+

# oidc_trust.py · which GitHub Actions jobs could assume your role? Trust conditions vs token claims
from fnmatch import fnmatchcase

P = "token.actions.githubusercontent.com"
JOBS = {   # the claims GitHub puts in each job's OIDC token (the ones trust policies use)
    "push to main":         {"aud": "sts.amazonaws.com", "sub": "repo:larder/larder-core:ref:refs/heads/main"},
    "deploy, production":   {"aud": "sts.amazonaws.com", "sub": "repo:larder/larder-core:environment:production"},
    "pull request":         {"aud": "sts.amazonaws.com", "sub": "repo:larder/larder-core:pull_request"},
    "another Larder repo":  {"aud": "sts.amazonaws.com", "sub": "repo:larder/website:ref:refs/heads/main"},
    "a stranger's repo":    {"aud": "sts.amazonaws.com", "sub": "repo:mallory/anything:ref:refs/heads/main"},
}
TRUSTS = {   # role → its trust policy's Condition block
    "aud only":           {"StringEquals": {f"{P}:aud": "sts.amazonaws.com"}},
    "any Larder repo":    {"StringEquals": {f"{P}:aud": "sts.amazonaws.com"},
                           "StringLike":   {f"{P}:sub": "repo:larder/*"}},
    "staging: main only": {"StringEquals": {f"{P}:aud": "sts.amazonaws.com",
                                            f"{P}:sub": "repo:larder/larder-core:ref:refs/heads/main"}},
    "prod: environment":  {"StringEquals": {f"{P}:aud": "sts.amazonaws.com",
                                            f"{P}:sub": "repo:larder/larder-core:environment:production"}},
}

def allowed(condition, claims):
    for op, tests in condition.items():
        for key, want in tests.items():
            have = claims[key.split(":")[-1]]
            if op == "StringEquals" and have != want:
                return False
            if op == "StringLike" and not fnmatchcase(have, want):
                return False
    return True

print(f"{'':<22}" + "".join(f"{role:<20}" for role in TRUSTS))
for job, claims in JOBS.items():
    print(f"{job:<22}" + "".join(f"{'ASSUMES' if allowed(c, claims) else '-':<20}" for c in TRUSTS.values()))
  1. Run it with python3 oidc_trust.py: 
                          aud only            any Larder repo     staging: main only  prod: environment
push to main          ASSUMES             ASSUMES             ASSUMES             -
deploy, production    ASSUMES             ASSUMES             -                   ASSUMES
pull request          ASSUMES             ASSUMES             -                   -
another Larder repo   ASSUMES             ASSUMES             -                   -
a stranger's repo     ASSUMES             -                   -                   -
    The first column is the misconfiguration from 17.4: a stranger's repository can deploy to you. The second lets every Larder repository and every pull request in. Only the last two columns are what each role was meant to allow.
  2. Try a tempting shortcut. Change the staging role's condition to StringLike with repo:larder/larder-core:*, “so tags work too”. Which jobs can now deploy to staging? (Pull requests, and production-environment jobs.)
  3. Optional: set it up for real. In a sandbox account, create the provider and a role trusted by one of your repositories' main branch (replace YOU/REPO): 
    ACCOUNT=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
aws iam create-open-id-connect-provider --url https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com \
  --client-id-list sts.amazonaws.com
cat > trust.json <<EOF
{ "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{ "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "Federated": "arn:aws:iam::$ACCOUNT:oidc-provider/token.actions.githubusercontent.com" },
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity",
    "Condition": { "StringEquals": {
      "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:aud": "sts.amazonaws.com",
      "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub": "repo:YOU/REPO:ref:refs/heads/main" } } }] }
EOF
aws iam create-role --role-name github-lab --assume-role-policy-document file://trust.json
aws iam attach-role-policy --role-name github-lab \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/job-function/ViewOnlyAccess
    (Older CLI versions also ask for --thumbprint-list; current ones don't need it.) Then add a workflow to the repository, with the action pinned to a commit SHA you look up on its releases page: 
    name: whoami
on: { push: { branches: [main] } }
permissions: { id-token: write, contents: read }
jobs:
  whoami:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@<full commit SHA>   # v4
        with:
          role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::<ACCOUNT>:role/github-lab
          aws-region: eu-central-1
      - run: aws sts get-caller-identity
    Push to main: the job prints assumed-role/github-lab/…. Push the same workflow on another branch (adjust on:): it fails with “Not authorized to perform sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity”. Clean up by deleting the role, the provider and the workflow.

17.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Access keys in CI secretsEvery workflow and action can read a long-lived key.OIDC federation; no stored AWS secret.
Trust policy without a sub conditionAny repository on GitHub can assume the role.Exact sub and aud with StringEquals.
Wildcards over the organisation or branchesPull requests and unrelated repositories get deploy rights.One role per environment, exact subjects.
Administrator rights for deploysA compromised job owns the account.Deploy-time least privilege; narrow PassRole.
Production without an approval gateAny merge, or any branch, can deploy to production.A protected environment trusted only by the production role.
Third-party actions by tagA moved tag runs new code with your credentials.Pin to commit SHAs; explicit, minimal permissions:.

✓Chapter summary

  • A stored deploy key is long-lived, shared and exposed to everything the pipeline runs.
  • With OIDC federation, each job gets a signed token from the CI system and exchanges it with AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity for a one-hour session.
  • The sub claim names the repository and branch, environment or pull request; trust it exactly, with aud.
  • One role per environment; production trusts only a protected environment with required approval.
  • Deploy roles get what deploys do, not administrator rights; boundaries and guardrails still apply.
  • Pin actions to SHAs and set minimal workflow permissions.

Key terms

OIDC federation
Trusting tokens from an external OpenID Connect issuer to grant role sessions.
IAM OIDC identity provider
The IAM resource that tells AWS to trust an issuer's tokens.
AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity
The STS call that exchanges an OIDC token for role credentials.
sub claim
The token's subject; for GitHub, the repository plus branch, environment or pull request.
Protected environment
A GitHub environment with deployment rules such as required reviewers.
Deploy-time least privilege
Giving a pipeline only the permissions a deployment needs.

Check your understanding

  1. What's in GitHub's OIDC token that tells AWS which repository and environment a job belongs to? recall

    Show answer

    The sub claim, such as repo:larder/larder-core:environment:production, plus claims like repository, ref and environment.

  2. A role trusts the GitHub OIDC provider with only an aud condition. Describe the attack. explain

    Show answer

    An attacker creates any GitHub repository, writes a workflow that requests a token with audience sts.amazonaws.com, and calls AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity on your role's ARN. The token is genuinely signed by GitHub, and nothing checks which repository it came from.

  3. Why should production trust an environment rather than the main branch? explain

    Show answer

    An environment can carry protection rules, such as required reviewers and allowed branches, so the approval in GitHub is the only path to production credentials. Trusting main gives every push to main production access, approved or not.

  4. Write the iam:PassRole statement for a pipeline that deploys one ECS service with a task role and an execution role. apply

    Show answer

    Allow iam:PassRole on exactly those two role ARNs, with StringEquals iam:PassedToService = ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com, as in 17.6.

  5. Design CI access for a monorepo that deploys three services to staging and production, with Terraform for infrastructure. design

    Show answer

    Per environment: a deploy role per service (or one with per-service resources) trusted by the matching protected environment; a Terraform plan role (read-only, trusted by pull requests from the same repository, in a sandbox or with read-only access); a Terraform apply role behind its own environment with approval. All exact sub conditions, one-hour sessions, boundaries, pinned actions.

  6. Compare how you'd restrict which GitHub repositories can use your cloud identity on AWS and on Google Cloud. compare

    Show answer

    AWS: conditions on the sub (and aud) claims in each role's trust policy. Google Cloud: an attribute condition on the workload identity pool provider (for example on repository_owner_id), which Google requires for GitHub, plus role grants to specific mapped principals.

Going further

  • GitHub: About security hardening with OpenID Connect and security hardening for GitHub Actions.
  • AWS: Create a role for OIDC federation and configure-aws-credentials.
  • Azure: Workload identity federation. Google Cloud: Federation with deployment pipelines.

Part IV · Access for software

18

Secrets and encryption keys

Roles removed most secrets from Larder's systems, but not all: databases still have passwords, partners still issue API keys. This chapter covers where the remaining secrets should live and how they rotate, then turns to encryption keys, where AWS KMS adds a second permission system beside IAM: who may use a key is decided by the key's own policy, and that turns encryption into an access control you can reason about.

By the end you can

  • Choose between Secrets Manager and Parameter Store, and have applications fetch secrets at run time.
  • Rotate a secret without downtime.
  • Explain envelope encryption, data keys and encryption context.
  • Read and write a KMS key policy, and know how it combines with IAM.
  • Decide when a customer managed key is worth it.
  • Find leaked secrets, and respond in the right order.

Before you start

  • Resource-based policies and evaluation (Chapter 10); the staging KMS denial (Chapter 11).
  • Workload roles (Chapter 15).

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
25 minutes, Python 3 with cryptography; optional AWS step, a few cents
Main provider
AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 18.8

At Larder

After the compromised action in March (Chapter 17), Larder turned on GitHub's secret scanning for every repository. On Thursday 6 May 2027, the first scan of larder-core's full history found a file called .env.production, committed by Jasper in June 2024 and deleted the next day. It contained the password of the orders database's application user, and the password had never been changed. It was also in /etc/larder/env on each of Larder Core's three virtual machines, where the application read it at start-up.

The same week, Eli had to settle a question left over from December (Chapter 11): staging's forgotten customer managed key. Should Larder use them at all? Section 18.9 has both answers.

18.1The secrets that remain

After workloads use roles (Chapter 15) and pipelines use federation (Chapter 17), what's left are secrets AWS can't replace: database passwords, third-party API keys (a maps provider, a payment provider), signing keys for webhooks and tokens (Chapter 19), and certificates' private keys. Each one is a long-lived credential, so the rules from Chapter 1 apply: as few as possible, each stored in exactly one place, readable by exactly the workloads that need it, and rotated.

They leak from the same places every time: source code and its history, environment files on servers, container images, CI logs (Chapter 17), chat messages and tickets, laptops' shell history. Notice that Larder's password was in two of them, and that deleting a file from a repository doesn't remove it from the history.

18.2Where secrets should live

Table 18.1 · AWS's two places for secrets
Secrets ManagerParameter Store (SecureString)
Made forsecrets that rotateconfiguration, some of it secret
Rotationbuilt in: managed for some services, a Lambda function for othersnone built in
EncryptionKMS, AWS managed or customer managed keyKMS, AWS managed or customer managed key
Cross-account accessresource-based policy on the secretadvanced parameters can be shared through AWS RAM
Cost (at the time of writing)$0.40 per secret per month, plus API callsstandard parameters free

Use Secrets Manager for anything that should rotate; Parameter Store for settings, including the odd secret that genuinely can't. Either way, access is IAM like everything else: the workload's role is allowed secretsmanager:GetSecretValue on exactly its own secrets, as Dispatch's policy did in Chapter 9.

Have applications fetch secrets at run time, through the SDK, with a short cache, rather than receiving them once in an environment variable at start. Then a rotated secret is picked up without a restart. ECS can inject secrets into containers' environments at start (through the task execution role, Chapter 15), which is better than a file, but means a restart after every rotation.

18.3Rotation without downtime

Rotation fails when there's a moment where the new password isn't valid yet, or the old one no longer is, but something still uses it. Secrets Manager's rotation works in four steps to avoid that: create the new secret value, set it in the database, test it, then mark it current.
