The safest pattern for databases is alternating users: two database users take turns, so while one's password is being changed, the other's still works. For RDS master passwords, RDS can manage the secret itself, including rotation, with no function to write.

Clients must cope: fetch the current secret when a connection fails authentication, rather than retrying the cached one forever. Rotate often enough that it's routine (every 30 to 90 days), and immediately when a secret may have leaked.

18.4Encryption keys and envelope encryption

AWS KMS holds encryption keys inside hardware security modules: a KMS key never leaves KMS in plain form. You don't send gigabytes to KMS to encrypt them; you use envelope encryption:

Three consequences matter for access control:

  • Reading the data needs two permissions: access to where it's stored (S3, a database) and kms:Decrypt on the key. That's why Larder's staging uploads needed kms:GenerateDataKey (Chapter 11).
  • Encryption context is a set of key–value pairs bound to the encryption: decrypting needs the same pairs, they're recorded in CloudTrail, and policies can require them. S3, for example, uses the object's ARN.
  • Disabling or deleting the key makes every piece of data encrypted under it unreadable, which is either a disaster or, deliberately, a way to destroy data you can't otherwise find (“crypto-shredding”).

18.5Key policies: the second permission system

Every KMS key has a key policy, a resource-based policy (Chapter 10) that is the primary control on the key. KMS is stricter than most services: no principal, not even the account's administrators, can use or manage a key unless its key policy allows it, either directly or by delegating to IAM. The default key policy delegates with one statement:

{ "Sid": "Enable IAM User Permissions", "Effect": "Allow",
  "Principal": { "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:root" },
  "Action": "kms:*", "Resource": "*" }

That means “let this account's IAM policies decide”, so anyone with AdministratorAccess can decrypt. To make the key a real second gate, replace it with statements that name who may administer the key and who may use it, and keep the two apart:

order-exports key policyLarder, production

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "PlatformAdminsManageButCannotUse",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": { "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:root" },
      "Action": ["kms:Describe*", "kms:Get*", "kms:List*", "kms:Enable*", "kms:Disable*", "kms:Put*",
                 "kms:Update*", "kms:Revoke*", "kms:TagResource", "kms:UntagResource",
                 "kms:ScheduleKeyDeletion", "kms:CancelKeyDeletion"],
      "Resource": "*",
      "Condition": { "ArnLike": { "aws:PrincipalArn":
        "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/aws-reserved/sso.amazonaws.com/*AWSReservedSSO_PlatformAdmin_*" } }
    },
    {
      "Sid": "ExportJobAndFinanceUseItOnlyThroughS3",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": { "AWS": ["arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/order-export-job",
                               "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/finance-reports"] },
      "Action": ["kms:Decrypt", "kms:GenerateDataKey"],
      "Resource": "*",
      "Condition": { "StringEquals": { "kms:ViaService": "s3.eu-central-1.amazonaws.com" } }
    }
  ]
}

Read aloud: “Platform admins may manage this key but not use it. The export job and the finance reporting job may use it, and only through S3.” An engineer with production PlatformAdmin can now read the export bucket's object list, but not one export's contents. They could still edit the key policy to let themselves in, but that's a deliberate, logged change you can alert on (Chapter 23), not a side effect of being an administrator. In a key policy, "Resource": "*" means “this key”.

Key idea

A customer managed key turns “who can decrypt this data?” into one short policy you can read, independent of everything IAM grants.

Watch out: locking yourself out

A key policy that names nobody able to manage it makes the key unmanageable: only AWS Support can help, and data under it may be lost. KMS refuses a new key policy that would stop you changing it, unless you explicitly bypass that safety check. Don't. Keep an administration statement for a role that will always exist, and include the break-glass role (Chapter 14).

Services that use keys on your behalf, such as EBS attaching an encrypted volume, use grants: temporary, narrow permissions on a key that the service creates and retires. You'll see them in aws kms list-grants; you rarely create them yourself.

18.6Which key for which data

Table 18.2 · Encryption at rest on AWS, by who controls the key
AWS owned (e.g. SSE-S3)AWS managed (aws/s3, aws/rds…)Customer managed
Who controls the key policyAWS; invisible to youAWS: any principal allowed by IAM to use the service can use ityou
Each use in CloudTrailnoyesyes
Share encrypted data across accountsn/anoyes
Disable or deletenonoyes
Cost (at the time of writing)freefree key; requests charged$1 per key per month, plus requests

Every option encrypts the data. What a customer managed key adds is control: a separate decision about who can read, cross-account sharing (copying an encrypted database snapshot to another account needs one), a per-use audit trail, and the ability to switch everything off. Use one where those matter; use the defaults elsewhere. With S3 and customer managed keys, turn on S3 Bucket Keys, which cut KMS requests, and their cost, by orders of magnitude.

18.7When a secret leaks

  1. Rotate first. A leaked secret is compromised the moment it's visible; investigation can wait an hour, rotation can't. Deleting the file or rewriting history doesn't un-leak it.
  2. Look for use between the leak and the rotation: database logs, CloudTrail, the provider's dashboard.
  3. Remove the copies you control: history rewrites, old images, environment files, tickets. This limits the next leak, not this one.
  4. Prevent the next one: secret scanning with push protection on every repository, pre-commit hooks for developers, and fewer secrets overall.

18.8Across providers

Table 18.3 · Secrets and keys at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
SecretsSecrets Manager; Parameter StoreKey Vault secretsSecret Manager
KeysKMS (CloudHSM for dedicated HSMs)Key Vault keys; Managed HSMCloud KMS (and Cloud HSM)
Who may use a keykey policy, plus IAM if delegatedAzure RBAC data roles (or legacy access policies) on the vault or keyIAM roles on the key or key ring
Your key for a service's datacustomer managed keyscustomer-managed keys (CMK)customer-managed encryption keys (CMEK)

On Azure

Key Vault holds secrets, keys and certificates. Use the Azure RBAC permission model rather than the older vault access policies, so that data-plane roles such as Key Vault Secrets User can be assigned to managed identities per vault or per secret. Turn on soft delete and purge protection, so a deleted vault or key can be recovered.

On Google Cloud

Cloud KMS keys live in key rings, and access is plain IAM: roles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyEncrypterDecrypter granted on one key to one service account. There's no separate key policy, so the separation of duties comes from granting admin roles and use roles to different principals. Secret Manager stores versioned secrets with rotation notifications.

18.9At Larder: a password from 2024, and a key decision

At Larder: solved

The password was rotated within the hour, following 18.7, and the database's connection logs showed no logins from anywhere but Larder Core's three hosts. Then the root cause: Ben's team moved the database credentials into Secrets Manager with rotation every 30 days using two alternating database users, and changed Larder Core to fetch the secret through the SDK with a five-minute cache, retrying once with a fresh copy when authentication fails. /etc/larder/env lost its last secret, and the larder-core role (Chapter 15) gained GetSecretValue on exactly that secret. Secret scanning with push protection now runs on every repository.

Eli's key decision, written up on 14 May: S3-managed keys remain the default everywhere. Customer managed keys are used where they buy something: for the order exports bucket, with the key policy in 18.5, so that platform admins can't read customers' order histories; and for the database's snapshots, so that a copy can go to a separate backup account (Chapter 20). Staging's forgotten key from Chapter 11: CloudTrail showed no use since December, so it was scheduled for deletion with the maximum 30-day waiting period, in case something nobody remembered still needed it. Nothing did.

18.10Lab: envelope encryption by hand

Lab 18 · A pretend KMS, then the real one

You'll encrypt an order export the way S3 does with KMS, then see what the encryption context, tampering and a disabled key do; optionally, you'll repeat the key ideas with a real KMS key. You need Python 3 and the cryptography package (pip install cryptography); for the optional part, the AWS CLI. Cost: the Python part is free; a real KMS key costs $1 a month, charged by the hour, so a few cents if you schedule its deletion at the end.

envelope.pyPython 3.8+, cryptography

# envelope.py · envelope encryption the way KMS does it, with a pretend KMS
# Needs: pip install cryptography
import json, os
from cryptography.exceptions import InvalidTag
from cryptography.hazmat.primitives.ciphers.aead import AESGCM

class PretendKMS:
    """Holds the key-encryption key. In AWS it never leaves KMS's hardware; here, it never leaves this object."""
    def __init__(self):
        self._kek, self.enabled = AESGCM.generate_key(bit_length=256), True

    def _aad(self, context):
        return json.dumps(context, sort_keys=True).encode()

    def generate_data_key(self, context):
        if not self.enabled:
            raise PermissionError("KMS: key is disabled")
        data_key, nonce = AESGCM.generate_key(bit_length=256), os.urandom(12)
        wrapped = nonce + AESGCM(self._kek).encrypt(nonce, data_key, self._aad(context))
        return data_key, wrapped                    # like GenerateDataKey: Plaintext + CiphertextBlob

    def decrypt(self, wrapped, context):
        if not self.enabled:
            raise PermissionError("KMS: key is disabled")
        return AESGCM(self._kek).decrypt(wrapped[:12], wrapped[12:], self._aad(context))

def encrypt(kms, plaintext, context):
    data_key, wrapped = kms.generate_data_key(context)
    nonce = os.urandom(12)
    blob = {"wrapped_key": wrapped, "nonce": nonce, "data": AESGCM(data_key).encrypt(nonce, plaintext, None)}
    del data_key                                    # only the wrapped copy is kept
    return blob

def decrypt(kms, blob, context):
    data_key = kms.decrypt(blob["wrapped_key"], context)
    return AESGCM(data_key).decrypt(blob["nonce"], blob["data"], None)

def attempt(label, fn):
    try:
        print(f"{label:<34} {fn()}")
    except InvalidTag:
        print(f"{label:<34} refused: authentication failed")
    except PermissionError as e:
        print(f"{label:<34} refused: {e}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    kms = PretendKMS()
    ctx = {"bucket": "larder-order-exports", "file": "2027-05-11.csv"}
    blob = encrypt(kms, b"order,total\nB-1042,312.40\n", ctx)
    print("stored:", {k: f"{len(v)} bytes" for k, v in blob.items()}, "\n")
    attempt("decrypt with the right context", lambda: decrypt(kms, blob, ctx))
    attempt("decrypt claiming another file", lambda: decrypt(kms, blob, dict(ctx, file="2027-05-12.csv")))
    tampered = dict(blob, data=blob["data"][:-1] + bytes([blob["data"][-1] ^ 1]))
    attempt("decrypt after one bit changed", lambda: decrypt(kms, tampered, ctx))
    kms.enabled = False
    attempt("decrypt after the key is disabled", lambda: decrypt(kms, blob, ctx))
  1. Run it with python3 envelope.py: 
    stored: {'wrapped_key': '60 bytes', 'nonce': '12 bytes', 'data': '42 bytes'}

decrypt with the right context     b'order,total\nB-1042,312.40\n'
decrypt claiming another file      refused: authentication failed
decrypt after one bit changed      refused: authentication failed
decrypt after the key is disabled  refused: KMS: key is disabled
    What's stored is the encrypted data and a 60-byte wrapped key (a 32-byte key, its nonce and an authentication tag); no plain key is kept anywhere. The context is checked like a password nobody has to keep secret, and switching the key off makes the data unreadable.
  2. Break it on purpose. In encrypt, remove the line del data_key and add blob["plain_key"] = data_key, as a hurried implementation might “for debugging”. The data now decrypts without ever asking KMS: the key policy, the audit trail and the off switch are all gone.
  3. Optional: with real KMS. 
    KEY=$(aws kms create-key --description "iam-book lab" --query KeyMetadata.KeyId --output text)
aws kms get-key-policy --key-id $KEY --policy-name default --output text     # the delegation statement
printf 'order,total\nB-1042,312.40\n' > export.csv
aws kms encrypt --key-id $KEY --plaintext fileb://export.csv \
  --encryption-context file=2027-05-11.csv --query CiphertextBlob --output text | base64 --decode > export.enc
aws kms decrypt --ciphertext-blob fileb://export.enc --encryption-context file=2027-05-11.csv \
  --query Plaintext --output text | base64 --decode                          # works
aws kms decrypt --ciphertext-blob fileb://export.enc --encryption-context file=2027-05-12.csv
                                                                             # InvalidCiphertextException
aws kms generate-data-key --key-id $KEY --key-spec AES_256 \
  --encryption-context file=2027-05-11.csv --query CiphertextBlob --output text   # the wrapped data key, base64
aws kms schedule-key-deletion --key-id $KEY --pending-window-in-days 7
rm export.csv export.enc
    (encrypt sends small data, up to 4 KB, straight to KMS; generate-data-key is the envelope method for anything bigger.) Find your calls in CloudTrail with the encryption context recorded in each.

18.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Secrets in code, env files or imagesThey leak with history, backups and images.Secrets Manager, fetched at run time by the workload's role.
“Deleted it from the repo” as the fixThe secret stays in history and every clone.Rotate first, then clean up.
Never rotatingOld leaks stay valid; rotation becomes frightening.Automatic rotation with alternating users.
Customer managed keys with the default policy onlyEvery administrator can decrypt; no second gate.Separate administrators from users in the key policy.
A key policy nobody can manageAn unmanageable key, possibly lost data.An admin statement for a durable role, plus break-glass.
Customer managed keys everywhereCost and complexity without benefit.Use them where control, sharing or audit matters.

✓Chapter summary

  • The secrets left after roles and federation are database passwords, third-party keys, signing keys and private keys.
  • Store them in Secrets Manager (or Parameter Store), fetch at run time, rotate automatically with alternating users.
  • Envelope encryption: data keys encrypt data locally; KMS keys wrap data keys and never leave KMS. Encryption context binds and records each use.
  • A key policy is the primary control on a KMS key; a customer managed key can separate who manages it from who can decrypt.
  • Use customer managed keys where control, cross-account sharing or audit matter; defaults elsewhere.
  • When a secret leaks: rotate, check for use, clean up, prevent.

Key terms

Secrets Manager
AWS's service for storing and rotating secrets.
Rotation
Replacing a secret's value regularly, without downtime.
KMS key
A key held in AWS KMS's hardware, used to wrap data keys or encrypt small data.
Data key
A key generated per object or batch to encrypt data locally.
Envelope encryption
Encrypting data with a data key and the data key with a KMS key.
Encryption context
Key–value pairs bound to an encryption, required to decrypt and logged.
Key policy
The resource-based policy that controls a KMS key.
Grant
A temporary, narrow permission on a KMS key, usually created by a service.

Check your understanding

  1. Why isn't deleting a committed password from the repository enough? recall

    Show answer

    It stays in the history and in every clone and fork, and may have been copied before the deletion. Only rotating it makes the leaked value useless.

  2. An administrator with AdministratorAccess can't decrypt a file in S3 encrypted with a customer managed key. Is that a bug? explain

    Show answer

    Not if the key policy doesn't delegate to IAM and doesn't name them: KMS requires the key policy to allow use, whatever IAM says. That's the intended second gate.

  3. Explain what encryption context adds, using S3 as the example. explain

    Show answer

    S3 binds each object's data key to the object's ARN. The wrapped key can't be used for another object; every decrypt is recorded in CloudTrail with the ARN; and key policies can require specific context values.

  4. An application caches a database password for a day; rotation runs every 30 days. What breaks, and how do you fix it? apply

    Show answer

    After rotation, new connections fail with the stale password until the cache expires. Retry once with a freshly fetched secret on authentication failure, use alternating users so the previous password stays valid during the switch, and keep the cache short.

  5. Decide which of these deserve customer managed keys: application logs; customer order exports; RDS snapshots copied to a backup account; public product images. design

    Show answer

    Order exports (a separate decision about who may read them) and snapshots copied cross-account (required to share encrypted snapshots). Logs and public images: default keys are fine, unless logs contain sensitive data that needs a separate gate.

  6. Compare how you'd let one workload read one secret on AWS and on Google Cloud. compare

    Show answer

    AWS: allow secretsmanager:GetSecretValue on that secret's ARN in the workload role's policy (and, with a customer managed key, kms:Decrypt through the key policy). Google Cloud: grant roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor on that one secret to the workload's service account.

Going further

  • AWS: Rotating secrets, KMS key policies, envelope encryption, encryption context.
  • GitHub: Push protection. OWASP Secrets Management Cheat Sheet.
  • Azure: Key Vault with Azure RBAC. Google Cloud: Envelope encryption.

Part IV · Access for software

19

Service-to-service and API access

Software calls software: your services call AWS, call each other, and call partners; partners call your APIs and send you webhooks. Each call has to say who's calling and prove it, without a person typing anything. This chapter shows how AWS signs its own API calls, how to put authentication in front of your own APIs, how to give partners keys that expire, and how to trust a webhook. It ends with the security review where Larder found three of these going wrong.

By the end you can

  • Explain what Signature Version 4 signs, and why it's stronger than a bearer token.
  • Choose an authoriser for an API on AWS: IAM, JWT, Lambda or mutual TLS.
  • Design partner API keys that expire, rotate and can be traced.
  • Verify webhooks: signature, freshness and replay.
  • Check object-level access on every endpoint, not just most of them.

Before you start

  • HMAC (Chapter 2), bearer tokens and JWT checks (Chapter 3), OAuth client credentials (Chapter 4).
  • Relationship-based checks (Chapter 6); workload roles (Chapter 15); secrets (Chapter 18).

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
25 minutes, Python 3, no cloud account
Main provider
AWS, with Azure and Google Cloud in 19.7

At Larder

This chapter moves forward in time. By October 2027, Larder for Business has a portal for office managers, a Business API that some customers' procurement systems call directly, federated sign-in for clients' staff, and a billing provider that tells Larder about payments by webhook. Each piece was built and reviewed by its own team, feature by feature, and three more office clients were about to start.

On 11 October, Ana, Larder's solution architect, ran a security architecture review of the whole design with Cho, Ben, Eli and an external reviewer: two half-days, with the trust boundaries marked on the diagram. Eleven findings, three of them high. Every one of the three was about software calling software. Section 19.8 has them, and their fixes.

19.1How AWS authenticates every API call

When the SDK calls an AWS service, it doesn't send your secret access key. It sends a signature: Signature Version 4 (SigV4), an HMAC (Chapter 2) computed over the request itself. AWS recomputes it with its copy of the key and compares. The signature covers:

  • the method, path, query string and chosen headers (always host and the date);
  • a SHA-256 hash of the body;
  • the date, region and service, as a credential scope.

Compared with a bearer token (Chapter 3), this buys three things. A captured request can't be altered, because any change breaks the signature. It can't be replayed much later, because AWS rejects signatures more than a few minutes from its own clock. And the long-term secret never crosses the network. With temporary credentials, the session token travels as a signed header, and the session itself expires (Chapter 8).

Your own services can use SigV4 too: API Gateway (with IAM authorisation), Lambda function URLs and VPC Lattice can accept SigV4-signed calls and evaluate them against IAM, so a service calls another with nothing but its own role.

19.2Authorising your own APIs on AWS

Table 19.1 · API Gateway authorisation options
OptionCaller proves itself withUse it for
IAM (SigV4)an AWS identity's signature; decided by IAM policies and the API's resource policyyour own services and accounts calling each other
JWT / Cognito authoriseran OAuth access token from a trusted issuer, checked for issuer, audience, expiry and scopesapps and partners using OAuth (Chapter 4)
Lambda authoriserwhatever your function checks (custom tokens, hashed API keys)schemes the built-ins don't cover
Mutual TLSa client certificate from a CA you trust (on a custom domain)B2B links where both sides manage certificates
Resource policysource account, VPC endpoint or IP rangeprivate APIs reachable only from your networks, combined with one of the above

Watch out: API Gateway's “API keys” aren't authentication

API Gateway has a feature called API keys, used with usage plans for throttling and quotas. AWS says plainly not to rely on them for authorisation: they identify a customer's quota, they're sent in the clear in a header, and they don't expire. Authenticate with one of the options in Table 19.1, and use usage-plan keys only for metering.

Whatever authenticates the call, the API must still authorise it, per request and per object. A valid token says who's calling and what kind of thing they may do; only your code knows whether this office manager may see order 1043.

19.3Inside: services calling services

For calls between your own services, prefer identities the platform already gives you over secrets you have to manage:

  • IAM-authenticated calls (SigV4 with each service's role) where the target supports them: API Gateway with IAM authorisation, function URLs, VPC Lattice. The callee's resource policy names exactly which roles may call it.
  • OAuth client credentials (Chapter 4) where services already speak OAuth: each service gets a client identity and asks for tokens with narrow scopes.
  • Mutual TLS with short-lived certificates, typically from a service mesh (Handbook 1, Chapter 21).

Avoid the pattern most systems start with: one shared “internal API key” in every service's configuration. It's every problem from Chapter 1, inside your own walls.

19.4Partners calling you: keys that expire

The best credential for a partner's system is OAuth client credentials or a federated token: short-lived, scoped, revocable. But many partners' systems can only send a static key, and you'll issue API keys anyway. Issue them properly:

Table 19.2 · An API key done properly
PropertyWhy
Long and random (32+ bytes), with a recognisable prefix such as lb_live_unguessable; the prefix lets secret scanners find leaked keys (Chapter 18)
Stored hashed, shown oncea database leak doesn't leak keys (Chapter 1's lab does this)
Expires (for example after 90 days)a forgotten key stops working by itself
Two active keys per clientrotation without downtime: add the new one, switch, revoke the old
Scoped to one customer account and specific operationsleast privilege, and object-level checks have something to check against
Created and revealed only in your portal, by the customer's adminno keys in email; you know who created each
Last-used time, source and revocation visiblecustomers and you can spot misuse and retire unused keys

19.5Webhooks: trusting a call nobody logged in for

A webhook is a partner calling an endpoint of yours to say something happened: an invoice was paid, a payment failed. There's no session and no user; the endpoint is public, so anyone who learns its URL can post to it. The standard protection is a signature: the sender and you share a secret, and the sender attaches an HMAC of the timestamp and the exact body.

  1. Verify the signature over the raw body bytes, before parsing them, comparing in constant time.
  2. Check freshness: reject timestamps more than a few minutes old, so a captured request can't be replayed next week.
  3. Reject duplicates by event ID, since senders retry and attackers replay within the window.
  4. Treat the content as a hint for important events: “invoice paid” can trigger a call back to the provider's API to confirm, rather than marking the invoice paid on the webhook's word alone.
  5. Keep the secret in Secrets Manager, and support two at once during rotation.

When you send webhooks to customers, do the same for them: sign every delivery, document how to verify, and include a timestamp and event ID.

Key idea

Sign the request, not just the sender: a signature over the exact request, with a timestamp, proves who sent it, that nobody changed it, and that it's new.

19.6Every endpoint checks the object

Chapter 6 warned about broken object-level authorisation: checking that a caller may “view orders” but not whether they may view this order. It's worth repeating here because APIs grow: a list endpoint that filters by the caller's office is written carefully, and six months later a detail, export or receipt endpoint takes an ID from the path and passes it straight to the backend, which only checks the role. Make the object check structural, not a habit: a single function that loads an object for a principal and refuses if they aren't related, which every endpoint must use, and a test for each endpoint that tries another customer's ID.

19.7Across providers

Table 19.3 · API authentication at the big three
AWSAzureGoogle Cloud
Cloud API callsSigV4 signaturesbearer tokens from Entra IDOAuth bearer tokens
Gateway for your APIsAPI Gateway: IAM, JWT, Lambda authorisers, mTLSAPI Management: validate-jwt, client certificates, managed identity to back endsAPI Gateway / Apigee: JWT, API keys for metering
Service to serviceIAM roles + SigV4; VPC Lattice auth policiesmanaged identity tokens for app registrations with app rolesID tokens with the target's URL as audience (Cloud Run, Cloud Functions)
“API keys” in the gatewayusage plans, not authenticationsubscription keys: identify a subscriber; pair with real authAPI keys identify a project; pair with real auth

On Azure

A calling service uses its managed identity (Chapter 15) to get an Entra ID token for the target's app registration; the target checks the token's audience and an app role assigned to the caller's identity. No secret exists on either side. API Management can enforce the same checks at the gateway with its validate-jwt policy.

On Google Cloud

To call a private Cloud Run service, the caller fetches an ID token from its metadata server with the service's URL as the audience, and the platform checks it against IAM (roles/run.invoker) before the request reaches your code: service-to-service authentication with nothing to store.

19.8At Larder: three high findings, fixed in two weeks

At Larder: solved

Business API keys never expired (internet → Business API). Some had been created when a client first integrated in early 2027, and sent to them by email. Ben's team reissued every key under the rules in Table 19.2: 90-day expiry with reminders, two active keys per client, created and revealed only in the portal by the client's own administrator, hashed at rest, with an lb_live_ prefix registered with GitHub's secret scanning. New integrations are steered to OAuth (Chapter 4). Done 22 October.

Another office's orders by ID (portal → Larder Core). The portal had been designed to check orders by relationship (Chapter 6), and its list and search screens did. But an order-detail call added in the summer passed the order number from the address straight to Larder Core, which only checked that the caller was an office manager. Changing the number showed another office's order. The fix, done 18 October: one function that loads an order for a principal and checks the office relationship, used by every portal and Business API endpoint, with a test per endpoint that tries someone else's order.

Billing webhooks weren't signature-checked (billing provider → Larder). The endpoint accepted any well-formed request, so anyone who found its address could have marked an invoice paid. Cho's team added verification as in 19.5, with the signing secret in Secrets Manager, rejected unsigned deliveries, and made “invoice paid” confirm with the provider's API before changing anything. Done 20 October. One of the medium findings went to Eli: no alert when a new client identity provider is trusted (Chapter 5); that one is Chapter 23's.

19.9Lab: sign a request, verify a webhook

Lab 19 · SigV4 by hand, and a webhook receiver

You'll implement AWS's request signing and check it against a case from AWS's published test suite, then write the verification Larder's billing webhooks were missing and attack it four ways. You need Python 3. Cost: nothing. (In real code, let the SDK sign; this shows what it does.)

sigv4.pyPython 3.8+

# sigv4.py · sign an AWS request by hand (Signature Version 4), checked against AWS's test suite
import hashlib, hmac

def h(data):
    return hashlib.sha256(data.encode()).hexdigest()

def hm(key, msg):
    return hmac.new(key, msg.encode(), hashlib.sha256).digest()

def sign(method, host, path, query, headers, body, access_key, secret_key, amz_date, region, service):
    date = amz_date[:8]
    headers = {k.lower(): v.strip() for k, v in {**headers, "host": host, "x-amz-date": amz_date}.items()}
    signed = ";".join(sorted(headers))
    canonical = "\n".join([method, path, query,
                           "".join(f"{k}:{headers[k]}\n" for k in sorted(headers)),
                           signed, h(body)])
    scope = f"{date}/{region}/{service}/aws4_request"
    to_sign = "\n".join(["AWS4-HMAC-SHA256", amz_date, scope, h(canonical)])
    key = hm(hm(hm(hm(("AWS4" + secret_key).encode(), date), region), service), "aws4_request")
    signature = hmac.new(key, to_sign.encode(), hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()
    return f"AWS4-HMAC-SHA256 Credential={access_key}/{scope}, SignedHeaders={signed}, Signature={signature}"

if __name__ == "__main__":
    # The "get-vanilla" case from AWS's Signature Version 4 test suite (example credentials)
    auth = sign("GET", "example.amazonaws.com", "/", "", {}, "",
                "AKIDEXAMPLE", "wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG+bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY",
                "20150830T123600Z", "us-east-1", "service")
    print(auth)
    print("matches the test suite:", auth.endswith("5fa00fa31553b73ebf1942676e86291e8372ff2a2260956d9b8aae1d763fbf31"))
    tampered = sign("GET", "example.amazonaws.com", "/admin", "", {}, "",
                    "AKIDEXAMPLE", "wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG+bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY",
                    "20150830T123600Z", "us-east-1", "service")
    print("same signature for another path:", tampered == auth)
  1. Run it with python3 sigv4.py: 
    AWS4-HMAC-SHA256 Credential=AKIDEXAMPLE/20150830/us-east-1/service/aws4_request, SignedHeaders=host;x-amz-date, Signature=5fa00fa31553b73ebf1942676e86291e8372ff2a2260956d9b8aae1d763fbf31
matches the test suite: True
same signature for another path: False
    Twenty lines reproduce AWS's own result, which the AWS SDK for Python (botocore) produces too for the same request and time. Change one character of the path and the signature is completely different.

webhooks.pyPython 3.8+

# webhooks.py · verify signed webhooks: HMAC over timestamp + raw body, a time window, and no replays
import hashlib, hmac, json

SECRET = b"whsec_lab_only_printed_in_a_book"     # shared with the billing provider, kept in Secrets Manager
TOLERANCE = 300                                # seconds
SEEN = set()                                   # event IDs already processed

def signature(timestamp, raw_body):
    return hmac.new(SECRET, f"{timestamp}.".encode() + raw_body, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()

def verify(headers, raw_body, now):
    try:
        parts = dict(p.split("=", 1) for p in headers["Billing-Signature"].split(","))
        timestamp, sig = int(parts["t"]), parts["v1"]
    except (KeyError, ValueError):
        return "rejected: missing or malformed signature"
    if not hmac.compare_digest(sig, signature(timestamp, raw_body)):
        return "rejected: bad signature"
    if abs(now - timestamp) > TOLERANCE:
        return "rejected: too old"
    event = json.loads(raw_body)
    if event["id"] in SEEN:
        return "rejected: already processed"
    SEEN.add(event["id"])
    return f"accepted: {event['type']} for {event['account']}"

if __name__ == "__main__":
    NOW = 1823148000                                                # 10 Oct 2027, 06:00 UTC
    body = b'{"id": "evt_81", "type": "invoice.paid", "account": "korenwolf", "amount": 12480.00}'
    good = {"Billing-Signature": f"t={NOW},v1={signature(NOW, body)}"}
    forged_body = body.replace(b"korenwolf", b"someone-else")
    tests = [
        ("as sent by the provider",    good, body,        NOW + 2),
        ("the same delivery again",    good, body,        NOW + 9),
        ("no signature header",        {},   body,        NOW + 2),
        ("body changed in transit",    good, forged_body, NOW + 2),
        ("captured, replayed later",   good, body,        NOW + 3600),
    ]
    for name, headers, raw, at in tests:
        print(f"{name:<28} {verify(headers, raw, at)}")
  1. Run it with python3 webhooks.py: 
    as sent by the provider      accepted: invoice.paid for korenwolf
the same delivery again      rejected: already processed
no signature header          rejected: missing or malformed signature
body changed in transit      rejected: bad signature
captured, replayed later     rejected: too old
    Before the October fix, Larder's endpoint would have accepted all five.
  2. Break it on purpose. Move the duplicate check and the SEEN.add line to the top of verify, before the signature check, then send a forged, unsigned body carrying the genuine event's ID before the real delivery arrives: the forgery is rejected, but the genuine event is then refused as “already processed”. Record an event as seen only after it has passed every other check. Then parse the JSON first and sign json.dumps(event) instead of the raw bytes: harmless re-encoding (spacing, key order) now breaks genuine signatures. Verify the exact bytes you received, first.

Stretch goal. Make verify accept two secrets at once, so the provider can rotate without a moment where deliveries fail.

19.10Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Gateway usage-plan keys as authenticationStatic, unexpiring, easily shared credentials.IAM, JWT, Lambda authorisers or mTLS; usage keys for metering only.
Partner keys that never expire, sent by emailForgotten keys work forever; nobody knows who has them.Table 19.2: expiry, two keys, portal-only, hashed, prefixed.
One shared internal API keyEvery service can impersonate every other.Roles with SigV4, client credentials or mTLS per service.
Unsigned or unverified webhooksAnyone who finds the URL can fake events.Verify HMAC over raw body, timestamp and event ID; confirm important events.
Object checks on most endpointsThe one that forgot leaks other customers' data.One “load for principal” function every endpoint uses; per-endpoint tests.
Hand-written signing in productionSubtle canonicalisation bugs.The SDK, or a maintained library.

✓Chapter summary

  • SigV4 signs each request with a key derived for one day, region and service; the secret never travels, and requests can't be changed or replayed later.
  • API Gateway authorises with IAM, JWTs, Lambda authorisers or mTLS; its API keys are for usage plans only.
  • Between your services, use roles, client credentials or mTLS, never a shared key.
  • Partner API keys must expire, rotate with two active keys, be stored hashed and be issued only through your portal.
  • Webhooks: verify an HMAC over the raw body and timestamp, reject old and repeated events, confirm important ones.
  • Every endpoint checks the object, through one shared function.

Key terms

SigV4
AWS Signature Version 4: HMAC signing of API requests with derived keys.
Canonical request
The normalised form of a request that gets hashed and signed.
Credential scope
Date, region and service that a SigV4 signature is valid for.
Authoriser
The component that authenticates callers in front of an API.
Mutual TLS
TLS where the client also presents a certificate.
Webhook
An HTTP callback a partner sends to report an event.
Replay
Re-sending a captured, valid request.

Check your understanding

  1. Name two things SigV4 protects against that a bearer token doesn't. recall

    Show answer

    Altering a captured request (the signature covers it) and replaying it later (signatures expire within minutes). Also, the long-term secret never crosses the network.

  2. Your team proposes API Gateway API keys to authenticate partners. What do you say? explain

    Show answer

    They're meant for usage plans and metering; AWS advises against using them for authorisation. Use a JWT authoriser with OAuth client credentials, mTLS, or a Lambda authoriser with properly managed keys, and keep usage-plan keys for quotas.

  3. Why must a webhook signature be checked against the raw body rather than parsed and re-encoded JSON? explain

    Show answer

    The sender signed specific bytes. Parsing and re-encoding can change spacing, key order or number formatting, which breaks genuine signatures; and code that parses first may act on unverified data.

  4. An API's list endpoint filters by the caller's office, but the detail endpoint doesn't check. Design a fix that stays fixed. design

    Show answer

    One shared function that loads an object for a principal and refuses unrelated callers; all endpoints must use it (enforced by review or a lint rule); and a test per endpoint that requests another customer's object and expects a refusal.

  5. Design credentials for a partner whose system can only send a static header. design

    Show answer

    An API key per partner integration, from the portal, with a prefix, stored hashed, expiring after 90 days with reminders, two active at once for rotation, scoped to the partner's account and needed operations, with last-used shown; mTLS in addition if the partner can manage certificates.

  6. Compare how one service calls another privately on Google Cloud and on AWS. compare

    Show answer

    Google Cloud: the caller fetches an ID token with the target's URL as audience from its metadata server; the platform checks it and roles/run.invoker. AWS: the caller signs with SigV4 using its role; API Gateway (IAM auth), a function URL or VPC Lattice checks IAM and the target's resource policy.

  • Azure: validate-jwt policy. Google Cloud: Cloud Run service-to-service authentication.
